Centralized vs Decentralized Monitors for Hyperproperties
Luca Aceto, Antonis Achilleos, Elli Anastasiadi, Adrian Francalanza, Daniele Gorla, Jana Wagemaker

Abstract
. This paper focuses on the runtime verification of hyperproperties expressed in Hyper- 𝗋𝖾𝖼 \mathsf{rec} HML, an expressive yet simple logic for describing properties of sets of traces. To this end, we consider a simple language of monitors that observe sets of system executions and report verdicts w.r.t. a given Hyper- 𝗋𝖾𝖼 \mathsf{rec} HML formula. We first employ a unique omniscient monitor that centrally observes all system traces. Since centralised monitors are not ideal for distributed settings, we also provide a language for decentralized monitors, where each trace has a dedicated monitor; these monitors yield a unique verdict by communicating their observations to one another. For both the centralized and the decentralized settings, we provide a synthesis procedure that, given a formula, yields a monitor that is correct (i.e., sound and violation complete). A key step in proving the correctness of the synthesis for decentralized monitors is a result showing that, for each formula, the synthesized centralized monitor and its corresponding decentralized one are weakly bisimilar for a suitable notion of weak bisimulation.



CCS:
Theory of computation Operational semantics
CCS:
Theory of computation Modal and temporal logics
CCS:
Theory of computation Logic and verification

. Elli Anastasiadi’s research has been supported by grant Grant #VR 2020-04430
of the Sponsor Swedish Research Council
.

1.
Introduction

Runtime verification (RV)
(
Bartocci et al., 2018
)
is a verification technique that observes system executions to determine whether some given specification is satisfied or violated.
This runtime analysis is usually conducted by a computational entity called a
monitor

(
Francalanza, 2021
)
.
RV is a lightweight verification technique
that is carried out as the system under observation executes,
thereby avoiding scalability issues caused by the state-explosion problem, as is the case for model checking.
Recently, RV has been extended to parallel set-ups
(
Bocchi et al., 2010
;
Cassar et al., 2017
;
Mezzina and Pérez, 2017
)
, and a large body of work in that setting aims to verify
hyperproperties
at runtime
(
Aceto et al., 2022a
;
Bonakdarpour and Finkbeiner, 2016
;
Bonakdarpour and Finkbeiner, 2018
;
Clarkson and Schneider, 2010
;
Finkbeiner et al., 2019
)
.

Hyperproperties
(
Clarkson and Schneider, 2010
)
are sets of
hypertraces
,
i.e.
sets of traces that may be seen as describing different system executions or the contributions of different sequential processes to a system execution.
As argued in
(
Bonakdarpour et al., 2018
)
, many properties of concurrent and distributed systems can be viewed as hyperproperties.
When verifying hyperproperties at runtime, several traces (i.e. several execution sequences) can be observed instead of just one, possibly at the same time.
Several extensions of temporal logics, such as HyperLTL, HyperCTL
∗

(
Clarkson et al., 2014
)
, Hyper
2
LTL
(
Beutner et al., 2023
)
, have been defined to express hyperproperties.
Extensions of standard logics to hyper properties also include variations of the
\mu
-calculus, such as
(
Aceto et al., 2022a
)
, setting the basis for the logic used in this paper, and
(
Gutsfeld et al., 2021
)
, which studies an asynchronous semantics.

Since they were proposed by Clarkson and Schneider in
(
Clarkson and Schneider, 2010
)
, hyperproperties have become a fundamental, trace-based formalism for expressing security and privacy properties,
verified using static and dynamic techniques
(
Agrawal and Bonakdarpour, 2016
;
Beutner et al., 2023
;
Beutner et al., 2024
;
Bonakdarpour and Finkbeiner, 2016
;
Bonakdarpour et al., 2018
;
Brett et al., 2017
;
Chalupa and Henzinger, 2023
;
Finkbeiner et al., 2019
)

implemented in a variety of tools
(
Beutner et al., 2024
;
Finkbeiner et al., 2018
;
Beutner and Finkbeiner, 2022
)
.
There is a large body of work, such as
(
Agrawal and Bonakdarpour, 2016
;
Brett et al., 2017
;
Hahn et al., 2019
)
, detailing several algorithms for monitoring (fragments of) hyperlogics under different assumptions and providing several correctness guarantees.
However, these proposals either construct a centralized monitoring algorithm that has access to all traces in the observed hypertrace, or verify single trace properties, over a distributed set-up
1
1

1

See e.g.
(
Bonakdarpour et al., 2022
;
Bonakdarpour et al., 2016
;
Fraigniaud et al., 2020
;
Francalanza et al., 2013
)
for distributed monitoring algorithms for classic trace-based logics.
.
Having an omniscient monitor simplifies the runtime analysis since the monitoring algorithm can compare all traces as needed by simply accessing different parts of its local memory.
But this power comes with drawbacks.
For starters, centralized monitors are unrealistic for distributed systems, where trace analysis is typically localised to network nodes so as to minimize communication across locations.
Moreover, centralized monitors
create single points of failure during verification
(
Aceto et al., 2024c
)
.
Furthermore, it can be problematic to store all the traces locally, especially in light of the wide availability of multi-core systems.
The goal of the decentralized monitor synthesis from logical specifications presented in this paper is to permit distributed monitor choreographies with
local
trace views whose components communicate in order to verify
global
properties (such as hyperproperties).
Decentralized monitors have been shown to avoid high contentions leading to vastly improved scalability
(
Aceto et al., 2024c
)
.
They also offer better privacy guarantees whenever they are stationed locally at the nodes where the respective traces are generated
(
Francalanza et al., 2013
;
Jia et al., 2016
)
.
To the best of our knowledge,
such a message-passing monitoring set-up has never been studied for the purpose of verifying hyperproperties so far.

In this paper, we study procedures for the

automated synthesis of
centralized and decentralized monitors
from hyperproperties described
in the logic Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML
(
Aceto et al., 2022a
)
.
This logic extends the linear-time
(
Vardi, 1988
)

\mu
-calculus
(
Kozen, 1983
)
(also known as Hennessy-Milner logic with recursion
(
Larsen, 1990
)
)
with constructs to describe properties
of hypertraces inspired by the work on HyperLTL (namely variables
ranging over traces, modal operators parametrized by trace variables, matching/mismatching between trace variables,
and existential and universal quantification over them).
Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML can
describe hyperproperties not expressible in HyperLTL or HyperCTL*,
such as properties that speak about consensus (see Example
2.2
) and periodicity (see Example
2.4
).
Furthermore, Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML supports a general, syntax-driven monitor synthesis that can handle both the aforementioned hyperproperties, at least in the centralized case (see also the discussion in Section
5
).

In both the centralized and decentralized set-ups, we work in the parallel model
(
Finkbeiner et al., 2019
)
, where a fixed number
of system executions is processed in parallel by monitors in an online
fashion. We specify monitors using a process-algebraic formalism that
builds on the one presented in
(
Aceto et al., 2019a
;
Francalanza et al., 2017
)
to define a class of monitors
called regular. Such monitors are easy to describe, resemble
(alternating) automata, and have sufficient expressive power to
provide standard monitoring guarantees.
Moreover, their algebraic
structure supports the compositional definition of their operational
semantics and monitor synthesis procedures from formulas,
building on
previous work relating algebraic process calculi with RV
(
Bocchi et al., 2017
;
Inoue and Yamagata, 2017
;
Francalanza, 2017
;
Aceto et al., 2019c
;
Francalanza, 2021
;
Lanotte et al., 2021
;
Aceto et al., 2023
;
Lanotte et al., 2023
)
.

In the centralized case, for each formula in the fragment of
Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML limited to greatest-fixed-point operators, our synthesis procedure yields a monolithic monitor that has access to all
the traces in an observed hypertrace.
However, in order to synthesize decentralized monitors for a sufficiently
expressive fragment of the logic, it is necessary to extend the
monitor capabilities with communication, as
shown already in
(
Aceto et al., 2022a
)
.
For instance, to monitor for
the property
“If there is a trace where event
a
occurs, then there
exists another trace where event
b
does not occur thereafter”,
monitors observing different traces need to
communicate to record that event
a
occurred in some
trace at some point and that there is some trace where
b
does not
occur from that point onwards. Allowing monitors to send and receive messages
significantly complicates their operational semantics (see Section
4
), the monitor
synthesis procedure (see Section
4.3
), and all consequent proofs.
The operational semantics for communicating monitors is one of the main contributions of the paper since its design is crucial to obtain the correctness guarantees provided by the synthesis procedure for decentralized monitors.
In
particular, the semantics of decentralized monitors and their
synthesis from formulas have to be designed carefully to ensure that
monitors are reactive (they are always ready to process any
system event) and input-enabled (they can always receive any
input from other monitors in their environment), properties that are desirable in any decentralized RV set-up.

We show that both
the centralized and the decentralized monitor
synthesis procedures are correct
.
More precisely, the monitors
synthesized from formulas are
sound
and

violation-complete
, meaning that (1) if the monitor synthesized from a formula
\varphi
reports a positive (resp., negative) verdict when observing a hypertrace
T
, then
T
does (resp., does not) satisfy
\varphi
, and (2) if
T
does not satisfy
\varphi
, then its associated monitor will report a negative verdict when observing
T
(see Theorems
3.9
and
3.12
, and Corollaries
4.5
and
4.6
). The proof of correctness in the decentralized case is considerably more technical than the corresponding proof in the centralized setting, due to the intricate communication semantics. To address the resulting technical challenges, we develop a proof strategy where we prove the correctness of the decentralized monitor
synthesis procedure using the centralized one as a yardstick.

This methodology is one of the key contributions we offer in this study. More
precisely, in Section
4.2

we identify six properties of a decentralized monitor
synthesis that make it ‘principled’
(see Definition
4.8
) and we show that,
when a decentralized monitor synthesis is principled, the centralized
and decentralized monitors synthesized from a formula are related by a
suitable notion of weak bisimulation (Theorem
4.10
). Apart from
supporting the definition of decentralized monitor synthesis
procedures, this result allows us to reduce the correctness of our
decentralized monitor synthesis to that of the centralized one, which can in turn drive the definition of further synthesis procedures in future work.
We also conjecture that our methodology provides a path to proving similar results for other models of communicating monitors independent of the monitoring strategy.
In summary, our contributions are the following:

•

a framework for monitoring hyperproperties by a central monitor that has access to all locations (
Section

3
)
and
a decentalized monitoring set-up for hyperproperties, with monitors that communicate (
Section

4
);

•

a synthesis function that returns a correct centralized monitor for every formula without least fixed points (
Section

3
);

•

a synthesis function that returns a correct (decentralized) choreography of communicating monitors for every formula without least fixed points that has no location quantifier within a fixed point operator (
Section

4
); and

•

a methodology to prove the correctness of a synthesis of communicating monitors, by establishing a list of desirable properties and relating the behavior of the decentralized monitors to that
of the corresponding centralized monitor (
Definitions

4.8
and
4.10
).

This is an extended and revised version of
(
Aceto et al., 2024a
)
. With respect to the extended abstract, here we provide
all the details and proofs,
we better justify Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML (through the formulation of a version of the non-interference property taken from
(
Clarkson and Schneider, 2010
)
– see Example
2.3
), we elaborate on the safety properties monitorable in our framework (
Section

3.3
), and we also explicitly include the decentralized synthesis of the diamond operator, that was omitted from the extended abstract (see however the drawback of omitting it in
Remark

4.14
).

2.
The Model and the Logic

Let
\mathsf{Act}
be a finite set of actions with at least two elements
2
2

2

When
\mathsf{Act}
is a singleton, every property in the logic becomes
equivalent to true or false.

, ranged over by
a,b
;
the set of (infinite) traces over
\mathsf{Act}
is
\mathsf{Trc}=\mathsf{Act}^{\omega}
, ranged over by
t
.
Given a finite and non-empty set of locations
{\mathcal{L}}
ranged over by
\ell
, a hypertrace
T
on
{\mathcal{L}}
is a function from
{\mathcal{L}}
to
\mathsf{Trc}
; the set of hypertraces on
{\mathcal{L}}
is denoted by
\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

{\mathcal{L}}
and
\mathsf{Act}
are fixed throughout this paper.
A hypertrace describes
a (distributed) system with
|{\mathcal{L}}|
users, and every user is located at a unique location chosen from
{\mathcal{L}}
.
A system behavior is
captured
by a hypertrace
T
on
{\mathcal{L}}
, mapping every user to the trace they perform.

For
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Trc}
, we write
t\xrightarrow{a}t^{\prime}
whenever
t=at^{\prime}
.
Let
A:{\mathcal{L}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Act}
; for
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
, we write
T\xrightarrow{A}T^{\prime}
whenever
T(\ell)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}T^{\prime}(\ell)
, for every
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
.
Notice that, for each
T
, there is a
unique
pair
A
and
T^{\prime}
such that
T\xrightarrow{A}T^{\prime}
:
more precisely, for every
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
, we have that
A(\ell)=a
and
T^{\prime}(\ell)=t^{\prime}
, whenever
T(\ell)=at^{\prime}
. We denote the
A
and
T^{\prime}
just defined by
{\mathit{h}d}(T)
and
{\mathit{t}l}(T)
respectively.
For a partial function
f:D\rightharpoonup E
(where
D
and
E
are sets ranged over by
d
and
e
, respectively), we denote by
\mathsf{dom}(f)
the set
\{d\in D\mid f(d)\mbox{ is defined}\}
and
by
\mathsf{rng}(f)
the set
\{e\mid\exists d\in\mathsf{dom}(f).~f(d)=e\}
.
Notation
f[d\mapsto e]
denotes the (partial) function mapping
d
to
e
and behaving like
f
otherwise.

2.1.
The Logic Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML

We consider Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML as the logic to specify
hyperproperties
.
We assume two disjoint and countably infinite sets
\Pi
and
V
of
location variables
and
recursion variables
, ranged over by
\pi
and
x
, respectively. Formulas of Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML are constructed as follows:

\varphi::=\mathsf{tt}\mid\mathsf{ff}\mid\varphi\land\varphi\mid\varphi\lor\varphi\mid\max x.\varphi\mid\min x.\varphi\mid x\mid\exists\pi.\varphi\mid\forall\pi.\varphi\mid\pi=\pi\mid\pi\neq\pi\mid[a_{\pi}]\varphi\mid\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\varphi

Apart from the basic boolean constructs,
we include the greatest and and least fixed-point operators to describe unbounded and/or infinite behaviors in a finitary manner,
3
3

3

In LTL, this behavior is captured by the ‘Until’ and ‘Release’ operators, but these are less expressive than fixed-points; see
(
Aceto et al., 2021
)
.
existential/universal quantifiers and equality/inequality tests on location variables, and the usual Hennessy-Milner modalities where

[a_{\pi}]
stands for ‘necessarily after
a
at the location bound to
\pi
’, and
\langle a_{\pi}\rangle
denotes ‘possibly after
a
at the location bound to
\pi
’.
A formula is said to be
guarded
if every
recursion variable appears within the scope of a modality within its fixed-point binding. All formulas are assumed to be guarded (without loss of expressiveness

(
Kupferman et al., 2000
)
).
We write
\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{l}}(\varphi)
to denote the free location variables of
\varphi
, and
\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{r}}(\varphi)
for the free recursion variables.

Remark 2.1.

We consider formulas where bound location variables are all pairwise distinct (and different from the free variables); hence, the formula
\forall\pi.[a_{\pi}]\exists\pi.\varphi
denotes the formula
\forall\pi.[a_{\pi}]\exists\pi^{\prime}.(\varphi\{^{\pi^{\prime}}\!/_{\pi}\})
, where
\varphi\{^{\pi^{\prime}}\!/_{\pi}\}
stands for the capture-avoiding substitution of
\pi^{\prime}
for
\pi
in
\varphi
. A similar notation for other kinds of substitutions is used throughout the paper.

\blacktriangleleft

The semantics for formulas
\varphi
in Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML is defined over
\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
through the function
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}
, as shown in
Table

1
, by exploiting two partial functions:
\rho\colon V\rightharpoonup 2^{\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}}
, which assigns a set of hypertraces on
{\mathcal{L}}
to all free recursion variables of
\varphi
, and
\sigma\colon\Pi\rightharpoonup{\mathcal{L}}
, which assigns a location to all free location variables of
\varphi
. In what follows, we tacitly assume that the free recursion and location variables in a formula
\varphi
are always included in
\mathsf{dom}(\rho)
and
\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
, respectively.
Function
\rho
gives the semantics of recursion variables. Intuitively,
\rho(x)
is the set of hypertraces that are assumed to satisfy
x
, as formally described on the first line of Table 1. Formulae of the form
\textrm{max}~x.\psi
and
\min x.\psi
are interpreted as the greatest and least fixed points of the function induced by their body
\psi
. Function
\sigma
keeps track of the location associated with each location
variable; it is extended every time a new variable
\pi
is introduced (through
\exists\pi
or
\forall\pi
, resp.) to check whether the body of the quantification holds for some (resp., for all) locations.

All the other operators have quite a straightforward semantics. In particular,
a formula
\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\varphi
holds true at hypertrace
T
if the trace in
T
at the location bound to
\pi
starts with an
a
and
{\mathit{t}l}(T)
satisfies
\varphi
; by contrast, a formula
[a_{\pi}]\varphi
can also hold true if the trace in
T
at the location associated to
\pi
does not start with an
a
.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\mathsf{tt}\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left\llbracket\mathsf{ff}\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\emptyset

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\left\llbracket x\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\rho(x)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\varphi\wedge\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}\cap\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\left\llbracket\varphi\vee\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}\cup\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\textrm{max}~x.\psi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\bigcup\{S\mid S\subseteq\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket^{\rho[x\mapsto S]}_{\sigma}\}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\left\llbracket\textrm{min}~x.\psi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\bigcap\{S\mid S\supseteq\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket^{\rho[x\mapsto S]}_{\sigma}\}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\exists\pi.\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\bigcup_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\left\llbracket\forall\pi.\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\bigcap_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\pi=\pi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}&\text{if }\sigma(\pi)=\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\left\llbracket\pi\neq\pi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}&\text{if }\sigma(\pi)\neq\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket[a_{\pi}]\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\{T\mid{\mathit{h}d}(T)(\sigma(\pi))=a\ \text{ implies }{\mathit{t}l}(T)\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}\}\qquad

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\left\llbracket\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\{T\mid{\mathit{h}d}(T)(\sigma(\pi))=a\ \wedge\ {\mathit{t}l}(T)\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma})\}

Table 1.
The semantics of Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML.

Whenever
\varphi
is
closed
(i.e., without any free variable), the semantics is given by
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\emptyset}_{\emptyset}
, where
\emptyset
denotes the partial function with empty domain. Notationally, we shall simply write
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket
instead of
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket^{\emptyset}_{\emptyset}
. We say that
T
satisfies the closed formula
\varphi
if
T\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket
.

Example 2.2.

For example, consider the set of actions
\{a,b\}
; then,
the hyperproperty

(1)

\varphi_{a}=\forall\pi.\max x.\bigl(\langle b_{\pi}\rangle x\ \vee\ \exists\pi^{\prime}.(\pi^{\prime}\neq\pi\ \wedge\ \langle a_{\pi^{\prime}}\rangle x)\bigr)

is a consensus-type property stating that,
at every position of every trace, whenever there is an
a
there is another trace that also has
a
.
Using the semantic definition
of the logic, it is not hard to see that the hypertrace
T_{1}
over the set of locations
\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\}
that maps
\ell_{1}
to
a^{\omega}
,
\ell_{2}
to
ba^{\omega}
and
\ell_{3}
to
(ba)^{\omega}
does not satisfy the property
\varphi_{a}
: what breaks the property is the first position. On the other hand, the hypertrace
T_{2}
that maps
\ell_{1}
to
a^{\omega}
,
\ell_{2}
to
(ab)^{\omega}
and
\ell_{3}
to
(ba)^{\omega}
does satisfy
\varphi_{a}
because at each position there are two traces that exhibit an
a
.

\blacktriangleleft

Example 2.3.

As a second example, we show how the well-known property of
non-interference
, in the formulation by Goguen and Meseguer from
(
Clarkson and Schneider, 2010
)
, can be written in our logic.
The property requires that “commands issued by users holding high clearances be removable without affecting observations of users holding low clearances”. They model this security policy as a hyperproperty by treating commands as inputs and observations as outputs, and by requiring that a system contains, for any trace
t
, a corresponding trace
t^{\prime}
with no high inputs, yet with the same low outputs as
t
:

GMNI=\{T\mid\forall t\in T.\exists t^{\prime}\in T\ .\ (H_{in}(t^{\prime})=\epsilon\wedge L_{out}(t)=L_{out}(t^{\prime}))\}~,

In our setting, we assume that actions can be either high or low, i.e. that
\mathsf{Act}
can be bipartitioned into
\{l^{1},\ldots,l^{m}\}
and
\{h^{1},\ldots,h^{n}\}
.
Then, we define the auxiliary formula

low\_agree(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=max~x.\left(\ \bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\big(\langle l^{i}_{\pi}\rangle\mathsf{tt}\leftrightarrow\langle l^{i}_{\pi^{\prime}}\rangle\mathsf{tt}\big)\ \wedge\ \bigvee_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}[a_{\pi}]x\ \right).

This formula says that the two given locations
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
exhibit the same low behaviour, and this holds repeatedly, after any possible action (being it high or low).
By using it,
GMNI
can be formulated in Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML as follows:

\varphi_{GMNI}=\forall\pi.\exists\pi^{\prime}.\left(\ low\_agree(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\ \wedge\ max~x.\left(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}[h^{i}_{\pi^{\prime}}]\mathsf{ff}\ \wedge\ \bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}[l^{i}_{\pi^{\prime}}]x\right)\ \right).

Formula
\varphi_{GMNI}
states that, for every trace, there must exist another trace such that:
(1) the two completely agrees on the low actions (as expressed by the
low\_agree
conjunct);
and (2) the second one cannot perform any high action (as expressed by the
max~x
conjunct).

2.2.
On the Expressiveness of Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML

The logic Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML adapts linear-time
\mu
HML
(
Larsen, 1990
)
to express properties of hypertraces, just as HyperLTL and HyperCTL*
(
Clarkson et al., 2014
)
are variations on LTL
(
Pnueli, 1977
)
and CTL*
(
Emerson and Halpern, 1986
)
, respectively, interpreted over hypertraces.
It is well known that
\mu
HML is more expressive than LTL and CTL*
(
Wolper, 1983
)
;
in a companion paper, we are working to formally show that the same result holds also in the hyper-setting, i.e. that
Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML can express all properties that can be described using HyperLTL and HyperCTL*.
Here we confine ourselves to show that
Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML can express properties that cannot be described using HyperLTL and HyperCTL*.

First, we recall that Wolper showed in
(
Wolper, 1983
)
that the property “event
a
occurs at all even positions in a trace” cannot be expressed in LTL (see
(
Wolper, 1983
, Corollary 4.2)
that is based on Theorem 4.1 in that reference).
We will refer to this property as
\varphi_{e}
, where “
e
” stands for even,
and
adapt it to a hypertrace setting for proving strictness of the inclusion of HyperLTL in Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML.

Example 2.4.

Let
\varphi_{e}^{h}
be the hyperproperty on the set of actions
\{a,b\}
that results
from adding an existential trace quantifier
\exists\pi
at the beginning of
\varphi_{e}
, and
replacing all modalities with
\pi
-indexed ones:

(2)

\varphi^{h}_{e}=\exists\pi.\max x.\bigl([a_{\pi}]\langle a_{\pi}\rangle x\wedge[b_{\pi}]\langle a_{\pi}\rangle x\bigr)

This is a liveness property that
describes the periodicity of events; when evaluated over singleton hypertraces, it coincides with the evaluation of
\varphi_{e}
.

\blacktriangleleft

Theorem 2.5.

Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML is more expressive than HyperLTL.

Proof.

By contradiction,
assume that a formula
\varphi_{h-LTL}
expresses exactly that there exists a trace in a hypertrace such that
a
holds on even positions.
We cannot trivially claim that this would be expressed with a single quantifier
\exists\pi
, but we know that over singleton hypertraces, say for
T=\{t_{0}\}
,
T\models\varphi_{h-LTL}
iff
T\models\varphi^{h}_{e}
.
However, since
T
contains only a single trace, we know that all the trace variables in
\varphi_{h-LTL}
must be mapped to
t_{0}
.
Consequently, all propositional variables that occur in
\varphi_{h-LTL}
are now interpreted to hold on the same trace.
Therefore, for this variable mapping, we get an LTL formula which expresses exactly that the single trace (that all trace variables have been mapped to) satisfies Wolper’s property.

Therefore, we can express the same formula in plain LTL, by replacing all propositional variables with non-trace quantified ones, and arrive at a contradiction.
∎

For HyperCTL* we exploit the possibility of the latter to use quantifiers in any part of a formula.
This has already been shown in the hyperproperty
\varphi_{a}
defined in (
1
): such a formula can potentially spawn an unbounded number of quantifiers, by unfolding the recursion when encountering
a
events.

Theorem 2.6.

Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML is more expressive than HyperCTL*.

Proof.

To prove this, we refer the reader to
(
Bozzelli et al., 2015
)
, where it is shown that the property “there
is an
n\geq 0
such that
a\neq t(n)
for every
t\in T
” is not expressible in HyperCTL* (notice that this property was also shown not to be expressible in HyperLTL by
(
Finkbeiner and Zimmermann, 2017
)
).
In our logic this property is expressible (over the set of actions
\{a,b\}
) with the formula:

\min x.((\forall\pi.\langle b_{\pi}\rangle\mathsf{tt})\vee(\forall\pi^{\prime}.([a_{\pi^{\prime}}]x\wedge[b_{\pi^{\prime}}]x))~.

Note that this formula can also be simplified. We choose to write it like this so that it is explicit that either all traces have
b
, or all traces take a step. However the following formulation is also valid, and only uses one quantifier:

\min x.((\forall\pi.((\langle b_{\pi}\rangle\mathsf{tt})\vee([a_{\pi}]x\wedge[b_{\pi}]x))~.\vskip-19.91684pt

∎

To conclude, we remark that this additional expressiveness of Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML is present in the fragments for which we synthesize monitors, as we shall see in the next sections.

3.
Centralized Monitoring

We first present a centralized, omniscient monitor that enforces hyperproperties expressed in Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML; here, the monitoring algorithm can compare all traces as needed by simply accessing different parts of its local memory.

3.1.
Centralized Monitors: Syntax and Operational Semantics

The set of centralized monitors
\mathsf{CMon}
is given by the following grammar:

\displaystyle\mathsf{CMon}\ni m

\displaystyle::=\mathsf{yes}~\mid~\mathsf{no}~\mid~\mathsf{end}~\mid~a_{\ell}.m~\mid~m+m~\mid~m\oplus m~\mid~m\otimes m~\mid~\mathsf{rec}~x.m~\mid~x

Verdicts
, taken from the set
\{\mathsf{yes},\mathsf{no},\mathsf{end}\}
and ranged over by
v
, are the possible outcomes of the monitor observation: it can be
\mathsf{yes}
, if the hyperproperty associated to the monitor is satisfied,
\mathsf{no}
, it if is not, or
\mathsf{end}
, if the monitor has not enough evidence to conclude either
\mathsf{yes}
or
\mathsf{no}
. The prefixed monitor
a_{\ell}.m
checks if
\ell
is currently performing an
a
and, in case, continues as
m
. We can compose monitors by using

non-deterministic choice, used to specify alternative and mutually exclusive observation capabilities, and
parallel-and and parallel-or, used to build parallel monitors that combine their verdicts according to a conjunctive or disjunctive strategy, respectively.

Notationally, we denote with
\odot
any of
\otimes
and
\oplus
.
Finally, monitors can also be recursively defined.

v\xrightarrow{A}v\frac{A(\ell)=a}{a_{\ell}.m\xrightarrow{A}m}\frac{A(\ell)\neq a}{a_{\ell}.m\xrightarrow{A}\mathsf{end}}\frac{m\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.m}/_{x}\}\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}}{\mathsf{rec}~x.m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}}\frac{m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}}{m+n\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}}\frac{n\xrightarrow{A}n^{\prime}}{m+n\xrightarrow{A}n^{\prime}}\frac{m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}\quad n\xrightarrow{A}n^{\prime}}{m\odot n\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}\odot n^{\prime}}

Table 2.
The operational semantics for centralized monitors, where
\odot\in\{\otimes,\oplus\}
.

\begin{array}[]{llll}v\Rrightarrow v&\frac{m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{end}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptn\Rrightarrow\mathsf{end}}{m\odot n\Rrightarrow\mathsf{end}}&\frac{m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}}{m\oplus n\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}}&\frac{m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}}{m\otimes n\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}}\\
\frac{m\Rrightarrow v}{m+n\Rrightarrow v}&\frac{m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptn\Rrightarrow v}{m\oplus n\Rrightarrow v}&\frac{m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447ptn\Rrightarrow v}{m\otimes n\Rrightarrow v}&\frac{m\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.m}/_{x}\}\Rrightarrow v}{\mathsf{rec}~x.m\Rrightarrow v}\end{array}

Table 3.
Verdict evaluation for centralized monitors (up to commutativity of
+
,
\otimes
, and
\oplus
).

\begin{array}[]{lll}\frac{m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447ptT\xrightarrow{A}T^{\prime}}{m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail m^{\prime}\triangleright T^{\prime}}\\
\frac{m\Rrightarrow v}{m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail v}\end{array}

Table 4.
The instrumentation rules for centralized monitors.

The operational semantics of centralized monitors is given in
Table

2

and follows the intuitive explanations we gave above for the different operators. In particular, a monitor that waits for an action at some location (as prescribed by
a_{\ell}
) can either see that action at
\ell
(as stated by
A
) and proceed in its observation, or it does not observe that action and stops its monitoring activity, by reporting
\mathsf{end}
. Recursive monitors should be unfolded to evolve. A non-deterministic choice
m+n
can initially behave like
m
and discard
n
in doing so or vice versa. Finally, once issued, a verdict never changes; possibly different verdicts obtained in different parallel branches of the monitor can be composed conjunctively or disjunctively.
This is represented by the judgement
\Rrightarrow
, whose intended use is to evaluate monitors and reach a verdict, whenever possible. The rules are given in
Table

4
.
They state that an
\mathsf{end}
verdict can be obtained only when combining two (or more) end verdicts, that
\mathsf{yes}
and
\mathsf{no}
are the absorbing elements for
\oplus
and
\otimes
, respectively, and the neutral elements for
\otimes
and
\oplus
, respectively, that verdict evaluation of a non-deterministic monitor is non-deterministic as well, and that recursive monitors must be unfolded before they can be properly evaluated.
Also notice that there can be multiple ways to infer the same verdict for the same monitor: e.g., for
\mathsf{yes}\oplus\mathsf{no}
we can either use the third rule of the first line or the (symmetric version of the) second rule from the second line of
Table

4
. However, the inferred value is of course the same (i.e.,
\mathsf{yes}
, in the previous situation).

We instrument a monitor
m

on a hypertrace
T
based on the rules of
Table

4
.
As usual, we write
\rightarrowtail^{*}
for the reflexive-transitive closure of
\rightarrowtail
.

3.2.
From Formulas to Centralized Monitors

We derive monitors for the subset of formulas without least fixed-points, denoted with Hyper-maxHML.
More precisely, given a formula
\varphi
, we want to derive a monitor that, when monitoring a hypertrace
T
, returns
\mathsf{no}
if and only if
T
does not belong to the semantics of
\varphi
; furthermore, if it returns
\mathsf{yes}
, then
T
belongs to the semantics of
\varphi
.
All regular properties of infinite traces that can be monitored for violations with the aforementioned guarantees can be expressed without using least fixed-point operators
(see the maximality results presented in

(
Aceto et al., 2019a
, Proposition 4.18)
and
(
Aceto et al., 2021
, Theorem 5.2)
in the setting of logics interpreted over infinite traces).
Intuitively, we use least fixed-points to describe liveness properties, whose violation does not have a finite witness in general.

The definition of the synthetized monitor is given by induction on
\varphi
. This definition is parametrized by a partial function
\sigma
, assigning a location to all the free location variables of
\varphi
; when
\varphi
is closed, we consider
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)
. The formal definition is
given in
Table

5
.
A monitor synthetized from a greatest-fixed-point formula is itself recursive and intuitively checks whether some unfolding of the formula is violated. The monitor for
\varphi\wedge\varphi^{\prime}
(respectively,
\varphi\vee\varphi^{\prime}
) is obtained as the parallel-and (respectively, the parallel-or) of the monitors synthesized from
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
. Universal and existential quantifiers are treated as conjunctions and disjunctions, respectively, and use the function
\sigma
to assign values to the newly introduced location variable.
Finally, the monitors for formulae of the form
[a_{\pi}].\varphi
and
\langle a_{\pi}\rangle.\varphi
look for an occurrence of action
a
at (the location bound to)
\pi
; if such action is observed at that location, then the monitor proceeds by checking the rest of the formula, otherwise the monitor for
\langle a_{\pi}\rangle.\varphi
returns
\mathsf{no}
whereas the monitor for
[a_{\pi}].\varphi
returns
\mathsf{yes}
.
Notice that we are using the choice operator ‘
+
’ here to consider only one of the possible observations (corresponding to the action occurring at the location
\sigma(\pi)
) and discarding all the other ones.

Example 3.1.

Let

\mathcal{L}=\{1,2\}
and
\mathsf{Act}=\{a,b\}
, and consider
the formula (
2
).
The
monitor synthesis in
Table

5
produces the following monitor
m
when applied to that formula:

m=\bigoplus_{{\ell\in\{1,2\}}}\mathsf{rec}~x.((a_{\ell}.(a_{\ell}.x+b_{\ell}.\mathsf{no})+b_{\ell}.\mathsf{yes})\otimes(b_{\ell}.(a_{\ell}.x+b_{\ell}.\mathsf{no})+a_{\ell}.\mathsf{yes})).

When monitor
m
is instrumented with the hypertrace
T
mapping
location
1
to
a^{\omega}
and location
2
to
(ab)^{\omega}
, the
verdict
\mathsf{no}
cannot be reached: indeed,
T
satisfies the formula
\varphi

since the trace at location
1
has
a
at all positions. On the
other hand, when
m
is instrumented with the hypertrace
T^{\prime}
mapping
location
1
to
b^{\omega}
and location
2
to
(ab)^{\omega}
, the
\mathsf{no}

verdict is reached after the monitor has observed the first two
actions at locations 1 and 2; this is in line with the fact that
T^{\prime}

does not satisfy
\varphi^{h}_{e}
.
\blacktriangleleft

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}lr@{\,}l}&{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathsf{tt})=\mathsf{yes}\quad\quad{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathsf{ff})=\mathsf{no}&{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(x)=x\qquad{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\max x.\varphi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\\
&{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi\wedge\varphi^{\prime})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\otimes{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi^{\prime})&{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi\vee\varphi^{\prime})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\oplus{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi^{\prime})\\
&{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\forall\pi.\varphi)=\bigotimes_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi)&{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\exists\pi.\varphi)=\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi)\\
&{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\pi=\pi^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\mathsf{yes}~\mbox{if }\sigma(\pi)=\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\\
\mathsf{no}~\mbox{otherwise }\end{cases}&{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\pi\neq\pi^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\mathsf{yes}~\mbox{if }\sigma(\pi)\neq\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\\
\mathsf{no}~\mbox{otherwise }\end{cases}\\
&{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}([a_{\pi}]\varphi)=a_{\sigma(\pi)}.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)+\sum_{b\neq a}b_{\sigma(\pi)}.\mathsf{yes}&{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\varphi)=a_{\sigma(\pi)}.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)+\sum_{b\neq a}b_{\sigma(\pi)}.\mathsf{no}\end{array}

Table 5.
Centralized monitor synthesis.

The main results of this section are that the centralized monitors
synthesized from formulas report sound verdicts and their verdicts are
complete for formula violations. We refer the reader to
(
Aceto et al., 2021
)
for a discussion on notions of correctness for monitors and the significance of soundness and violation-completeness.
The main point is that, already in the setting of
\mu
HML, it is possible to have soundness and completeness only for formulae without fixed points; hence, satisfaction completeness and violation completeness were introduced to weaken the completeness requirement and having the possibility of monitoring also some recursive formulae.

To prove soundness (
Theorem

3.9
), we first need three lemmata:
the first one claims soundness of synthesized monitors when they have not taken any transition yet;
the second one claims that synthesized monitors always transition to other synthesized monitors;
the last one claims that the relation between the hypertrace and the semantics of the formula are carried over through the instrumentation steps.
The proofs of these results have been relegated to
Appendix

A
, to streamline reading.

{textAtEnd}

[allend, category=soundness]
We need a few preliminary lemmata to prove
Theorem

3.9
.
We first show that centralized monitors never output the
\mathsf{end}
verdict if they are
\mathsf{end}
-free themselves :

Lemma 3.2.

If
m
is an
\mathsf{end}
-free monitor and
m\Rrightarrow v
, then
v=\mathsf{no}
or
v=\mathsf{yes}
.

We link the semantics of a formula to the special case where the synthesized centralized monitor is a verdict, which we use in various subsequent proofs:

Lemma 3.3.

If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\mathsf{yes}
, then
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
, for all
\rho
; if
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\mathsf{no}
, then
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\emptyset
, for all
\rho
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\varphi
. If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\mathsf{yes}
, then the definition of the synthesis function (see
Table

5
) yields the following base cases:
\varphi=\mathsf{tt}
, or
\varphi=(\pi=\pi^{\prime})
and
\sigma(\pi)=\sigma(\pi^{\prime})
, or
\varphi=(\pi\neq\pi^{\prime})
and
\sigma(\pi)\neq\sigma(\pi^{\prime})
. In all cases we immediately conclude that
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
, for all
\rho
.

In the inductive cases we only need to consider
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell\}
and
\varphi=\forall\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
or
\varphi=\exists\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
where
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})=\mathsf{yes}
(because otherwise
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\neq\mathsf{yes}
). In both cases we obtain from the induction hypothesis that
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}^{\rho}=\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

The proof where
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\mathsf{no}
is similar.
∎

We show that if synthesized monitors of two formulas are equal, then these two formulas must have the same semantics, which is used to prove
Corollary

3.7
.

Lemma 3.4.

Let
\varphi
and
\psi
be (possibly open) formulas. If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi)
, then
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\rho}
, for all
\rho
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\varphi
. As a first base case we consider
\varphi=\mathsf{tt}
. We then have
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\mathsf{yes}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi)
. We can immediately conclude that, for all
\rho
, we have
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\rho}
using
Lemma

3.3
.
The base cases for
\varphi=\mathsf{ff}
,
\varphi=(\pi=\pi^{\prime})
and
\varphi=(\pi\neq\pi^{\prime})
are similar.

The last base case is for
\varphi=x
for
x
a recursion variable. Then
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=x={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi)
, from which we can conclude that
\psi=x
or
\psi=Q_{1}\dots Q_{n}.\psi^{\prime}
for
Q_{i}
a universal or existential quantifier,
n\geq 1
,
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell\}
and
\psi^{\prime}=x
. In the first case it follows immediately that, for all
\rho
, we have
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\rho}
. In the second case we obtain that
\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\psi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}^{\rho}=\rho(x)=\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket
.

We now proceed with the inductive cases.
We first consider
\varphi=[a_{\pi}]\varphi^{\prime}
. Thus
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=a_{\sigma(\pi)}.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi^{\prime})+\sum_{b\neq a}b_{\sigma(\pi)}.\mathsf{yes}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi)
. We distinguish two cases: either
\psi=[a_{\pi^{\prime}}]\psi^{\prime}
,
\sigma(\pi)=\sigma^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime})
and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi^{\prime})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
, or
\mathsf{Act}=\{a,b\}
,
\psi=\langle b_{\pi^{\prime}}\rangle\psi^{\prime}
,
\sigma(\pi)=\sigma^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime})
,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi^{\prime})=\mathsf{no}
and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})=\mathsf{yes}
. In the first case we use the induction hypothesis to obtain that, for all
\rho
, we have
\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\psi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\rho}
. The definition of the semantics gives the desired result immediately.
In the second case, we use
Lemma

3.3
to obtain that, for all
\rho
,
we have
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\{T\mid{\mathit{h}d}(T)(\sigma(\pi))=a\ \text{
implies }{\mathit{t}l}(T)\in\emptyset\}=\{T\mid{\mathit{h}d}(T)(\sigma^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime}))=b\ \wedge\ {\mathit{t}l}(T)\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}\}=\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\rho}
.

The case for
\varphi=\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\varphi^{\prime}
is similar.

Next we consider
\varphi=\textrm{max}~x.\varphi^{\prime}
. Hence,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi^{\prime})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi)
. From this we conclude that
\psi=\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}
and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi^{\prime})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
for some
\psi^{\prime}
. We use the induction hypothesis to obtain that, for all
\rho
, we have
\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\psi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\rho}
. We derive

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\textrm{max}~x.\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}=\bigcup\{S\mid S\subseteq\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\rho[x\mapsto S]}_{\sigma}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle IH}}{{=}}\bigcup\{S\mid S\subseteq\left\llbracket\psi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\rho[x\mapsto S]}_{\sigma}\}=\left\llbracket\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}

For the case where
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
and the case where
\varphi=\forall\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
, we need an intermediary result:

(3)

\displaystyle\text{If ${}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\varphi_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{k}}(\varphi_{k})$ for $k\geq 2$, then }\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\left\llbracket\varphi_{i}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{i}}^{\rho},\text{for all $\rho$.}

We will prove this claim at the end, but first finish the main proof using this result. Consider
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
. Hence,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{1})\otimes{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{2})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi)
. We use intermediary result (
3
) to conclude.
Now consider the case where
\varphi=\forall\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
and
|{\mathcal{L}}|=1
:
we get
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi)
. From the induction hypothesis we obtain that, for all
\rho
,
we have that
\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{\rho}
.
Since
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell\}
, we have, for all
\rho
, that
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}^{\rho}
,
and we are done.
If
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}\}
for
k\geq 2
, we have that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell_{1}]}(\varphi_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell_{k}]}(\varphi_{k})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi)
and we use intermediary result (
3
) to conclude.

The case for
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
and
\varphi=\exists\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
can be handled in a similar manner as the two previous cases, with an additional result dual to (
3
).

Now we prove claim (
3
) by induction on
\varphi
. Without loss of generality we can assume that for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
we have that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})\neq m\otimes n
for all
m,n
(we refer to this as maximality).

We distinguish the case where
k=2
or
k>2
. If
k=2
, we get from the synthesis function that
\varphi
is a conjunction or a universal formula. In case
\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}\wedge\varphi^{\prime\prime}
, we obtain that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi^{\prime})\otimes{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\varphi_{1})\otimes{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{2}}(\varphi_{2})
. We use the induction hypothesis of the main proof to conclude that, for all
\rho
, we have
\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}^{\rho}
and, for all
\rho
, we have
\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\varphi_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{2}}^{\rho}
. The desired result now follows immediately from the semantics:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}\cap\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle IH}}{{=}}\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}^{\rho}\cap\left\llbracket\varphi_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{2}}^{\rho}.

The case for
\varphi=\forall\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
and
k=2
implies that
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}
and it is handled similarly.
Then we treat the case where
k>2
.
From the synthesis function, we can again conclude that
\varphi
is a conjunction or a universal formula. The universal case is a complicated version of the conjunctive case, so we only threat the former. Let
\varphi=\forall\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
and
K=\{1,\dots,k\}
. Then,

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\otimes_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\psi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})
, and from our assumption of maximality of
\otimes_{i\in K}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
, there is a partition
(K_{\ell})_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}
, such that for every
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
,

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})=\otimes_{i\in K_{\ell}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
.
By the induction hypothesis,
for all
\rho
we have that
\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}^{\rho}=\bigcap_{i\in K_{\ell}}\left\llbracket\varphi_{i}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{i}}^{\rho}
and we can conclude that
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho}=\bigcap_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}^{\rho}=\bigcap_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}\bigcap_{i\in K_{\ell}}\left\llbracket\varphi_{i}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{i}}^{\rho}=\bigcap_{i\in K}\left\llbracket\varphi_{i}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{i}}^{\rho}
.
∎

The following immediate fact is used to prove in
Section

3.2
, which shows soundness of synthesized monitors when they have not taken any transition yet, and
Section

3.2
:

Corollary 3.5.

\left\llbracket\max x.\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\left\llbracket\psi\{^{\max x.\psi}/_{x}\}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
.

Proof.

Immediate, since we interpret recursive formulas as fixed points.
∎

The next lemma shows that, for any combination of synthesized parallel monitors, we can construct an equal synthesized monitor, and link their semantics. It is used in various lemmas below.

Lemma 3.6.

If
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\varphi_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{k}}(\varphi_{k})
with
k\geq 1
, then there exist
\sigma
and
\psi
such that
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)
and
\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\bigcap_{i\in I}\left\llbracket\varphi_{i}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{i}}
. Similarly, if
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\varphi_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{k}}(\varphi_{k})
, there exist
\sigma
and
\psi
such that
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)
and
\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\left\llbracket\varphi_{i}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{i}}
.

Proof.

We only prove the case for
\otimes
, the case for
\oplus
is similar.
We proceed by induction on
k
. The base case is trivial. For the inductive case we consider
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\varphi_{1})\otimes{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{2}}(\varphi_{2})\otimes\dots\otimes{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{k}}(\varphi_{k})
, where we know from the induction hypothesis that there exist
\sigma^{\prime},\psi^{\prime}
such that

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{2}}(\varphi_{2})\otimes\dots\otimes{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{k}}(\varphi_{k})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
and

\left\llbracket\psi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}=\bigcap_{i\in\{2,\dots,k\}}\left\llbracket\varphi_{i}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{i}}
. Let
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{j}
be all the variables occuring in
\varphi_{1}
. Let
\pi_{1}^{\prime},\dots\pi_{j}^{\prime}
all be fresh variables not occuring in
\varphi_{1}
or in
\psi^{\prime}
. Lastly, let
\varphi_{1}^{\prime}=\varphi_{1}\{^{\pi_{1}^{\prime}}/_{\pi_{1}}\}\dots\{^{\pi_{j}^{\prime}}/_{\pi_{j}}\}
.

Then
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\varphi_{1})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}[\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{1})]\dots[\pi_{j}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{j})]}(\varphi_{1}^{\prime})

and
\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}=\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}[\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{1})]\dots[\pi_{j}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{j})]}
.
Furthermore
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}[\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{1})]\dots[\pi_{j}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{j})]}(\psi^{\prime})

and
\left\llbracket\psi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}=\left\llbracket\psi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}[\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{1})]\dots[\pi_{j}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{j})]}
.
Thus we can conclude that
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}[\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{1})]\dots[\pi_{j}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{j})]}(\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\psi^{\prime})
. We also have that

\displaystyle\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\wedge\psi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}[\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{1})]\dots[\pi_{j}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{j})]}

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}[\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{1})]\dots[\pi_{j}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{j})]}\cap\left\llbracket\psi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}[\pi_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{1})]\dots[\pi_{j}^{\prime}\mapsto\sigma_{1}(\pi_{j})]}

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}\cap\left\llbracket\psi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}\cap\bigcap_{i\in\{2,\dots,k\}}\left\llbracket\varphi_{i}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{i}}=\bigcap_{i\in I}\left\llbracket\varphi_{i}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{i}}\qed

Then we obtain that, for any synthesized monitor that is a combination of synthesized parallel monitors, we can relate the semantics of the corresponding formulas:

Corollary 3.7.

For all
\varphi
and
\sigma
, if
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m\odot n
, and there exist
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
and
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
such that
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\psi_{1})
and
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{2}}(\psi_{2})
, then
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\left\llbracket\psi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}\cap\left\llbracket\psi_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{2}}
if
\odot=\otimes
, and
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\left\llbracket\psi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}\cup\left\llbracket\psi_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{2}}
if
\odot=\oplus
.

Proof.

Immediate from the synthesis definition, by
Lemma

3.4
and
Lemma

3.6
.
∎

Now we can prove soundness of synthesized monitors when they have not taken any transition yet:

{theoremEnd}

[default,category=soundness]lemma

If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\Rrightarrow v
, then
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
, if
v=\mathsf{yes}
, and
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\emptyset
, if
v=\mathsf{no}
.

{proofEnd}

We proceed by induction on
\Rrightarrow
.

The base is for
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=v
: here the result immediately follows from
Lemma

3.2
and
Lemma

3.3
.

For the first inductive case, we consider
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m\oplus n\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
and
m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
. From the synthesis function and possibly
Lemma

3.6
we conclude that there exist
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
and
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
such that
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\varphi_{1})
and
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{2}}(\varphi_{2})
. We apply the induction hypothesis to obtain that
\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}=\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
. The result then follows from
Corollary

3.7
.

Let
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m\otimes n\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
and
m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
. From the synthesis function and possibly
Lemma

3.6
we conclude that there exist
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
and
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
such that
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\varphi_{1})
and
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{2}}(\varphi_{2})
. We apply the induction hypothesis to obtain that
\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}=\emptyset
. The result then follows from
Corollary

3.7
.

The case for
\mathsf{end}
can be skipped, as no formula is synthesized into monitors with
\mathsf{end}
.

Let
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m\otimes n\Rrightarrow v
because
m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
and
n\Rrightarrow v
. From the synthesis function and possibly
Lemma

3.6
we conclude that there exist
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
and
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
such that
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\varphi_{1})
and
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{2}}(\varphi_{2})
. We can apply the induction hypothesis to obtain that
\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}=\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
and as we know that
v\neq\mathsf{end}
, we also get that
\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}=\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
if
v=\mathsf{yes}
and
\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}=\emptyset
if
v=\mathsf{no}
. The result then follows from
Corollary

3.7
.

The case for
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m\oplus n\Rrightarrow v
because
m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
and
n\Rrightarrow v
is similar to the previous case.

Let
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m+n\Rrightarrow v
because
m\Rrightarrow v
. This case can be skipped, because, by the definition of the synthesis function, we know that neither
m\Rrightarrow v
nor
n\Rrightarrow v
can hold whenever
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m+n
.

For the last inductive case, we consider
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.m\Rrightarrow v
because
m\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.m}/_{x}\}\Rrightarrow v
. By definition of the synthesis, we know that

\varphi=\textrm{max}~x.\psi
and
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)
. Note that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi}/_{x}\})
. From the induction hypothesis and
Corollary

3.5
, we obtain that

\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\left\llbracket\psi\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi}/_{x}\}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\emptyset
, if
v=\mathsf{no}
, and
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\left\llbracket\psi\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi}/_{x}\}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
, if
v=\mathsf{yes}
.

{textAtEnd}

[allend, category=soundness]

The following lemma is a basic fact regarding substitution, and we use it in various places throughout the paper.

Lemma 3.8.

If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m
,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=n
, and the free location variables of
\psi
are not bound in
\varphi
, then
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi\{{}^{\psi}/{}_{x}\})=m\{{}^{n}/{}_{x}\}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
\varphi
and the only interesting case is the one where
\varphi=\exists\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
or
\varphi=\forall\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
. We describe the case for
\varphi=\exists\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
, as the other one is dual.

In this case,
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\exists\pi.\varphi^{\prime})=\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})
.
By the induction hypothesis,

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime}\{^{\psi}/_{x}\})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})\{^{{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\psi)}/_{x}\}.

Since
\pi
does not appear free in
\psi
,

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\psi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=n.

Therefore,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi\{^{\psi}/_{x}\})=\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime}\{^{\psi}/_{x}\})=\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})\{^{n}/_{x}\}=m\{^{n}/_{x}\}
.
∎

The next lemma shows that synthesized monitors always transition to other synthesized monitors:

{theoremEnd}

[default,category=soundness]lemma

If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
, then
m^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
for some
\sigma^{\prime}
and
\varphi^{\prime}
.

{proofEnd}

We proceed by induction on
\xrightarrow{A}
. The interesting cases are the inductive cases. We first treat both inductive cases of the sum. If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m+n\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
, we have two cases: either
\varphi=[a_{\pi}]\psi
or
\varphi=\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi
. We treat the case for
\varphi=[a_{\pi}]\psi
, the other case is similar. Thus we are in the case where
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=a_{\sigma(\pi)}.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)+\sum_{b\neq a}b_{\sigma(\pi)}.\mathsf{yes}
. This means that
m^{\prime}
can be
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)
or
\mathsf{yes}
. In both cases we have obtained the required result.

If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
because
m\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.m}/_{x}\}\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
, we know that
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)
and
\varphi=\textrm{max}~x.\psi
. We use

Lemma

3.8
to conclude that
m\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.m}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi\{^{\varphi}/_{x}\})
(note that the free location variables of
\varphi
are not bound in
\psi
). Thus we can use the induction hypothesis to know that
m^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
for some
\sigma^{\prime}
and formula
\varphi^{\prime}
.

If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m\odot n\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}\odot n^{\prime}
, from the synthesis function and possibly
Lemma

3.6
we obtain
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
and
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
such that
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\psi_{1})
and
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{2}}(\psi_{2})
. We then use the induction hypothesis to obtain
\sigma_{1}^{\prime},\sigma_{2}^{\prime}
and
\psi_{1}^{\prime}
,
\psi_{2}^{\prime}
such that
m^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}^{\prime}}(\psi_{1}^{\prime})
and
n^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{2}^{\prime}}(\psi_{2}^{\prime})
. The required result now follows from
Lemma

3.6
.

{textAtEnd}

[allend, category=soundness]

The last result we need to prove soundness is the following, which essentially shows that the relation between the hypertrace and the semantics of the formula are carried over through the instrumentation steps:

{theoremEnd}

[default,category=soundness]lemma

Let
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\triangleright T\rightarrowtail{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})\triangleright T^{\prime}
. If

T^{\prime}\notin\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}
then
T\notin\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
; if

T^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}
then
T\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
.

{proofEnd}

From
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\triangleright T\rightarrowtail{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})\triangleright T^{\prime}
we deduce that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
and
T\xrightarrow{A}T^{\prime}
. We proceed by induction on
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
.

In the first base case we have
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=v={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
. The result follows immediately from
Lemma

3.3
.

The cases where
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=a_{\ell}.m
are not applicable, as the synthesis function cannot produce
a_{\ell}.m
(being
|\mathsf{Act}|>1
).

The first inductive case is for
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m+n
and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})=m^{\prime}
or
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})=n^{\prime}
. We distinguish two cases.

(1)

\varphi=[a_{\pi}]\psi
. Here we have two more cases:

(a)

m^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)
,
m=a_{\sigma(\pi)}.m^{\prime}
, and
A(\sigma(\pi))=a
.
Assume that
T^{\prime}\notin\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
. Then
T\notin\left\llbracket[a_{\pi}]\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
follows immediately from the definitions. If we assume instead that
T^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
, we also know immediately that
T\in\left\llbracket[a_{\pi}]\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
.

(b)

m^{\prime}=\mathsf{yes}
,
m=b_{\sigma(\pi)}.m^{\prime}
for
b\neq a
,
and
A(\sigma(\pi))\neq a
. It follows immediately that
T\in\left\llbracket[a_{\pi}]\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
as
A(\sigma(\pi))\neq a
, and that
T^{\prime}\notin\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}
never holds.

(2)

\varphi=\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi
. Also here we have two more cases:

(a)

m^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)
,
m=a_{\sigma(\pi)}.m^{\prime}
, and
A(\sigma(\pi))=a
. Assume that
T^{\prime}\notin\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
. Then
T\notin\left\llbracket\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
follows immediately from the definitions. If we assume instead that
T^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
, we also know immediately that
T\in\left\llbracket\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
.

(b)

m^{\prime}=\mathsf{no}
,
m=b_{\sigma(\pi)}.m^{\prime}
for
b\neq a
, and
A(\sigma(\pi))\neq a
. It follows immediately that
T\notin\left\llbracket\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
as
A(\sigma(\pi))\neq a
, and that
T^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}
never holds.

Consider
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
. Then we have
\varphi=\textrm{max}~x.\psi
and
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)
. We know
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})=m^{\prime}
, and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)}/_{x}\}\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
. Assume that
T^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}
. Note that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi}/_{x}\})
. From the induction hypothesis and
Corollary

3.5
, we obtain that
T\in\left\llbracket\psi\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi}/_{x}\}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}=\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
. The case for
T^{\prime}\notin\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}
is similar.

Last we consider
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=m\odot n\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}\odot n^{\prime}
, because
m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
,
n\xrightarrow{A}n^{\prime}
, and
m^{\prime}\odot n^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
. From
Lemma

3.6
, we know that there exist
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
and

\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
such that
m^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\tau_{1}}(\psi_{1})
and
n^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\tau_{2}}(\psi_{2})
. Similarly, we obtain also that
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{1}}(\varphi_{1})
and
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{2}}(\varphi_{2})
for some
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
and
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
.
We distinguish two cases.

(1)

\odot=\otimes
. We assume
T^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}
. Then by
Corollary

3.7
we know that
T^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\psi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\tau_{1}}
and
T^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\psi_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\tau_{2}}
.
From the induction hypothesis we get that
T\in\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}
and
T\in\left\llbracket\varphi_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{2}}
. By
Corollary

3.7
we know that
T\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
.

If on the other hand we assume
T^{\prime}\notin\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}
, the argument is dual.

(2)

\odot=\oplus
. We assume
T^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}
. Then by
Corollary

3.7

we know that
T^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\psi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\tau_{1}}
or
T^{\prime}\in\left\llbracket\psi_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\tau_{2}}
. From the induction hypothesis we get that
T\in\left\llbracket\varphi_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}
or
T\in\left\llbracket\varphi_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma_{2}}
.
By
Corollary

3.7
we know that
T\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}
. The case for
T^{\prime}\notin\left\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\right\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}
is dual.

Theorem 3.9 (Soundness).

Let
\varphi\in
Hyper-maxHML be a closed formula and
T\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
, then
T\not\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket
;
if
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{yes}
, then
T\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket
.

Proof.

By definition, there exist an integer
h>0
and
m_{1}\triangleright T_{1},\ldots,m_{h}\triangleright T_{h}
such that
m_{1}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)
,
T_{1}=T
,

m_{i}\triangleright T_{i}\rightarrowtail m_{i+1}\triangleright T_{i+1}
(for every
i=1,\ldots,h-1
), and

m_{h}\triangleright T_{h}\rightarrowtail v
.
We proceed by induction on
h
.
The base case holds because of
Section

3.2
.
The inductive case holds because of
Section

3.2
, which can be applied because of
Section

3.2
.
∎

We now move to violation completeness, i.e. that all hypertraces that do not belong to
\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket
are rejected by the monitor for
\varphi
. This is proved in
Theorem

3.12
, that requires two preliminary easy lemmata, whose proofs hold by definition of the operational semantics.

Lemma 3.10.

If
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
, then

(m_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes m_{h}\otimes m\otimes m_{h+1}\otimes\ldots\otimes m_{k})\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
, for every
m_{1},\ldots,m_{h},m_{h+1},\ldots,m_{k}

without free variables.

Lemma 3.11.

If
m_{i}\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
for every
i=1,\ldots,k
, then

(m_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus m_{k})\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
.

Theorem 3.12 (Violation Completeness).

Let
\varphi\in
Hyper-maxHML be a closed formula and
T\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
.
If
T\not\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket
, then
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
.

Proof.

We assume that in
\varphi
every recursive variable
x
appears in the scope of a unique (max) fixed-point subformula of
\varphi
of the form
\mathtt{fix}(x)\triangleq\max x.\psi
. Let
\leq_{\varphi}
be a partial order of the recursive variables in
\varphi
, such that
x\leq_{\varphi}y
if
\mathtt{fix}(x)
is a subformula of
\mathtt{fix}(y)
in
\varphi
.
We now define the closure
\mathtt{cls}(\psi)
of a subformula
\psi
of
\varphi
by induction on the number of the free recursion variables in
\psi
:
if
\psi
is closed, then
\mathtt{cls}(\psi)\triangleq\psi
; otherwise,
\mathtt{cls}(\psi)\triangleq\mathtt{cls}(\psi\{^{\mathtt{fix}(x)}/_{x}\})
, where
x
is
\leq_{\varphi}
-minimal in
\psi
.

For each
\sigma
, let
\rho_{\sigma}
be such that
\rho_{\sigma}(x)=\{T\mid{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(x))\triangleright T\not\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}\}
for every
x
in its domain.
By induction on the structure of
\psi
, we prove that:

for every (not necessarily closed) subformula
\psi
of
\varphi
,
T\not\in\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho_{\sigma}}
implies
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
,
for
\sigma
and
\rho_{\sigma}
such that

\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{l}}(\psi)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
and

\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{r}}(\psi)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\rho_{\sigma})
.

Clearly, the theorem is proved if we consider
\varphi
.
There are seven possible base cases:

•

\psi=\mathsf{tt}
, or
\psi=(\pi=\pi^{\prime})
for
\sigma(\pi)=\sigma(\pi^{\prime})
,
or
\psi=(\pi\neq\pi^{\prime})
for
\sigma(\pi)\neq\sigma(\pi^{\prime})
: in these cases, all
T
’s belong to
\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket^{\rho_{\sigma}}_{\sigma}
, so there is nothing to prove.

•

\psi=\mathsf{ff}
, or
\psi=(\pi=\pi^{\prime})
for
\sigma(\pi)\neq\sigma(\pi^{\prime})
,
or
\psi=(\pi\neq\pi^{\prime})
for
\sigma(\pi)=\sigma(\pi^{\prime})
: in these cases,

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\triangleq\mathsf{no}
and the claim is then trivial.

•

\psi=x
: immediate from the definition of
\rho_{\sigma}
.

For the inductive step, we distinguish the outmost operator in
\psi
:

•

\psi=\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
: by definition of the semantics,
T\not\in\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket^{\rho_{\sigma}}_{\sigma}
if and only if
T\not\in\left\llbracket\psi_{i}\right\rrbracket^{\rho_{\sigma}}_{\sigma}
, for some
i\in\{1,2\}
.
By inductive hypothesis,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi_{i}))\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
.
By definition of
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))
and Lemma
3.10
,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
.

•

\psi=\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}
: by definition of the semantics,
T\not\in\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket^{\rho_{\sigma}}_{\sigma}
if and only if
T\not\in\left\llbracket\psi_{i}\right\rrbracket^{\rho_{\sigma}}_{\sigma}
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
By inductive hypothesis,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi_{i}))\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
.
By definition of
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))
and Lemma
3.11
,

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
.

•

\psi=[a_{\pi}]\chi
: by definition of the semantics,
T\not\in\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket^{\rho_{\sigma}}_{\sigma}
if and only if
{\mathit{h}d}(T)(\sigma(\pi))=a
and
{\mathit{t}l}(T)\not\in\left\llbracket\chi\right\rrbracket^{\rho_{\sigma}}_{\sigma}
.
By definition,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))=a_{\sigma(\pi)}.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\chi))+\sum_{b\neq a}b_{\sigma(\pi)}.\mathsf{yes}
; hence,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))\triangleright T\rightarrowtail{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\chi))\triangleright{\mathit{t}l}(T)
. By inductive hypothesis,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\chi))\triangleright{\mathit{t}l}(T)\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
, and we easily conclude.

•

\psi=\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\chi
: by definition of the semantics,
T\not\in\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket^{\rho_{\sigma}}_{\sigma}
if and only if either
{\mathit{h}d}(T)(\sigma(\pi))\neq a
or
{\mathit{t}l}(T)\not\in\left\llbracket\chi\right\rrbracket^{\rho_{\sigma}}_{\sigma}
.
By definition,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))=a_{\sigma(\pi)}.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\chi))+\sum_{b\neq a}b_{\sigma(\pi)}.\mathsf{no}
. If
{\mathit{h}d}(T)(\sigma(\pi))\neq a
, then

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))\triangleright T\rightarrowtail\mathsf{no}\triangleright{\mathit{t}l}(T)
and we easily conclude.
Otherwise, we work like in the previous case.

•

\psi=\forall\pi.\chi
: by definition of the semantics,
T\not\in\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}
if and only if there exists
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
such that
T\not\in\left\llbracket\chi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}
.
By inductive hypothesis,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\mathtt{cls}(\chi))\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
.
We conclude by definition of
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))
and Lemma
3.10
.

•

\psi=\exists\pi.\chi
: by definition of the semantics,
T\not\in\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma}
if and only if for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
it holds that
T\not\in\left\llbracket\chi\right\rrbracket^{\rho}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}
.
By inductive hypothesis,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\mathtt{cls}(\chi))\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
, for all
\ell
. We conclude by definition of
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))
and Lemma
3.11
.

•

\psi=\max x.\chi
: we prove the contrapositive, i.e. that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))\triangleright T\not\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
implies that
T\in\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho_{\sigma}}
.
Let us consider
S\triangleq\{T^{\prime}\mid{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\chi))\triangleright T^{\prime}\not\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}\}
.
We observe that

\rho_{\sigma}[x\mapsto S]
satisfies the assumptions required by the inductive statement; so,
the inductive hypothesis gives us that
S\subseteq\left\llbracket\chi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho_{\sigma}[x\mapsto S]}
, and therefore
S\subseteq\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho_{\sigma}}
.
By the definition of the monitor semantics,
