{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\psi))\triangleright T\not\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
yields
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\mathtt{cls}(\chi))\triangleright T\not\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
, meaning that
T\in S
, and therefore
T\in\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket_{\sigma}^{\rho_{\sigma}}
, which completes the proof.
∎

3.3.
On the Monitorable Hypersafety Properties

The monitor synthesis we presented above does not cover the whole Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML, but just Hyper-maxHML; nevertheless, this fragment is able to capture all
hypersafety properties
, as defined in
(
Clarkson and Schneider, 2010
)
.
Using this definition, one can show that hypersafety properties are subset-closed, as shown in
(
Clarkson and Schneider, 2010
, Theorem 1)
.

In our setting,
S
(
\subseteq\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
) is a hypersafety property whenever

\forall T\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}.\ T\not\in S\implies\left(\exists M\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{{\mathcal{L}}}^{f}\ .\ \left(M\leq T\ \wedge\ \forall T^{\prime}\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{{\mathcal{L}}}.\ M\leq T^{\prime}\implies T^{\prime}\not\in S\right)\right).

where
\mathsf{HTrc}_{{\mathcal{L}}}^{f}
denotes the set of mappings from
{\mathcal{L}}
to
\mathsf{Act}^{*}
and
M\leq T
means that, for every
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
, there exists a
\ell^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
such that
M(\ell)
is a prefix of
T(\ell^{\prime})
.
The formula above tells that a set of hypertraces
S
is a hypersafety property if every hypertrace
T
that does not belong to
S
admits a “finite justification”
M
for this;
M
is a set of finte traces that can be extended to become
T
(by possibly changing the traces-to-locations assignment — as the ‘
\leq
’ relation allows) and that, however we extend it, we obtain some hypertrace that is not in
S
.

Notice that, in general,
(
Clarkson and Schneider, 2010
)
do not associate traces to locations and, most importantly, they allow hypertraces to be possibly infinite sets of traces.
By contrast, our
T
’s are finite sets, since
|{\mathcal{L}}|=k
, which also implies an upper bound on any possible “bad thing”
M
. For such
M
’s it has been proven (see
(
Bonakdarpour and Finkbeiner, 2016
)
) that, for each
k>1
, these properties coincide with the so-called
k
-safety hyperproperties
, which in turn correspond to HyperLTL sentences with at most
k
universal quantifiers.

However, our monitor synthesis also handles formulae with quantifier alternation; as an example, consider Goguen and Meseguer ’s classic definition of the non-interference property given in
(
Clarkson and Schneider, 2010
)

(see
GMNI
in
Example

2.3
).
Clearly, the hyperproperty
GMNI
can be expressed in the fragment of Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML for which we have synthesized centralized monitors in
Section

3.2

(see
\varphi_{GMNI}
in
Example

2.3
): indeed, to detect violations for a fixed set of traces, it suffices to check all pairs for such a violation, which is what the formula expresses and the monitor synthesized from it does.
However,
GMNI
is known not to be a hypersafety property, since it is not subset closed.

Hence, if we want to characterize all the properties monitorable for violations in our centralized monitoring set-up, where verdicts are irrevocable, what is needed is a variation on the notion of hypersafety properties that takes the fact that hypertraces are
{\mathcal{L}}
-indexed families of traces into account.
We believe that the following proposal better corresponds to our set-up, and hence to the power of the monitors we have discussed so far.
Intuitively, by following the characterization of safety property given in
(
Alpern and Schneider, 1985
)
(i.e., the well-known motto “nothing bad happens”), the two key characteristics of the notion of the “bad thing” witnessing a violation of a hypersafety property are that it must be finitely observable and irremediable for a given
fixed
set of locations
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Definition 3.13.

A hyperproperty
S
is
violation-monitorable
whenever

\forall T\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}.\ T\not\in S\implies\left(\exists M\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{{\mathcal{L}}}^{f}\ .\ \left(M\sqsubseteq T\ \wedge\ \forall T^{\prime}\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{{\mathcal{L}}}.\ M\sqsubseteq T^{\prime}\Rightarrow T^{\prime}\not\in S\right)\right).

where
M\sqsubseteq T
means that, for every
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
, it holds that
M(\ell)
is a prefix of
T(\ell)
.

Essentially, this definition allows for the “bad thing”
M
to be infinitely extended (as
T^{\prime}
), but, differently from hypersafety, no new trace can be added (i.e.,
M
and
T^{\prime}
are defined on the very same set of locations
{\mathcal{L}}
).
Notice that
GMNI
is a violation-monitorable property. Indeed, let
T\not\in GMNI
;
then, there is some location
\ell
such that, for every
\ell^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
, it holds that either
T(\ell^{\prime})

contains some high action, or the low parts of
T(\ell)
and
T(\ell^{\prime})
are different. For each
\ell^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
, let
i_{\ell^{\prime}}

be the first index such that
T(\ell^{\prime})(i_{\ell^{\prime}})
is high or
T(\ell)(i_{\ell^{\prime}})\neq T(\ell^{\prime})(i_{\ell^{\prime}})
, and let
i
be the
maximum of all the
i_{\ell^{\prime}}
. Let
M
be the
{\mathcal{L}}
-indexed set of finite traces mapping each
\ell^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
to the prefix
of
T(\ell^{\prime})
of length
i
.
Now, for every
T^{\prime}
such that
M\sqsubseteq T^{\prime}
, we have that
T^{\prime}\not\in GMNI
because its prefix
M
provides a witness breaking the definition of GMNI for
T^{\prime}(\ell)
.

We conjecture that violation-monitorable hypersafety corresponds to the properties that are monitorable for violations in the parallel monitor set-up.
Moreover, such a definition should also allow us to prove maximality results for a specific monitoring set-up; for example,
any violation-monitorable property is monitorable for violations by the monitors produced by our synthesis, and vice versa. This is a challenging direction for future research.
Furthermore, the classic definition of hypersafety property and that of violation-monitorable hyperproperty given above are parametrized on a relation between
{\mathcal{L}}
-index sets of finite traces and
{\mathcal{L}}
-indexed sets of traces: the relations
\leq
and
\sqsubseteq
. Another variation on such a relation could be defined modulo a permutation of
{\mathcal{L}}
, so that the names of the locations do not really matter:

M\preceq T
iff there is a permutation
\gamma
over
{\mathcal{L}}
such that
M(\ell)
is a prefix of
T(\gamma(\ell))
, for each
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
.

The study of the notion of safety that emerges by using
\preceq
and how it compares with the above notions of safety is another direction for future work.

4.
Decentralized Monitoring

When verifying a distributed system, having a central authority that performs any type of runtime verification is a strong assumption, as it reduces the appeal of distribution, creates single points of failure during verification and can pose problems in storing all the traces locally, especially in light of the wide availability of multi-core systems.
Thus, we study to what extent hyperproperties can be monitored by decentralized monitors; these avoid high contentions (leading to vastly improved scalability
(
Aceto et al., 2024c
)
) and also offer better privacy guarantees (whenever they are stationed locally at the nodes where the respective traces are generated
(
Francalanza et al., 2013
;
Jia et al., 2016
)
).

4.1.
Decentralized Monitors: Syntax and Operational Semantics

We associate monitors to locations, denoted by
\ell
, and monitors associated to
\ell
monitor only actions required to happen at
\ell
, thus allowing the processing of events to happen locally. This imposes some form of coordination between monitors at different locations. For this reason, we introduce the possibility for monitors to communicate.

We define a communication alphabet
\mathsf{Com}
, ranged over by
c
, over some finite alphabet of communication constants
\mathsf{Con}
(that contains
\mathsf{Act}
), ranged over by
\gamma
, as

\mathsf{Com}\ni c\ ::=\ (!G,\gamma)\ \mid\ (?G,\gamma),

where
G\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
and
\gamma\in\mathsf{Con}
.
We have a communication action
(!G,\gamma)
for sending
\gamma
to group
G
(multicast communication), and one
(?G,\gamma)
for receiving
\gamma
from any monitor from the set
G
.
Point-to-point communication can be represented by taking singleton sets for
G
.

The syntax of decentralized monitors is given by the following grammar:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DMon}\ni M

\displaystyle::=[m]_{\ell}~\mid~M\vee M~\mid M\wedge M

\displaystyle\mathsf{LMon}\ni m

\displaystyle::=\mathsf{yes}\,\mid\,\mathsf{no}\,\mid\,\mathsf{end}\,\mid\,a.m\,\mid\,c.m\,\mid\,m+m\,\mid\,m\oplus m\,\mid\,m\otimes m\,\mid\,\mathsf{rec}~x.m\,\mid\,x

Notationally, in what follows we shall sometimes use
\diamond
to denote any of
\wedge
and
\vee
.
Monitor
[m]_{\ell}
denotes that
m
monitors the trace located at location
\ell
, so, it is ‘localized’ at
\ell
(this justifies the name
\mathsf{LMon}
).
Monitors assigned to the same trace run in parallel and observe identical events;
contrary to
(
Aceto et al., 2022a
)
, monitors assigned to different traces are no longer completely isolated from each other, but can now communicate, which is the main new feature of the decentralized set-up.

The operational rules for
m\in\mathsf{LMon}
are given in
Table

6
. Notice that, when we have parallel monitors, only one of them at a time can send; by contrast,
all those that can receive from some location
\ell
are forced to do so.

a.m\xrightarrow{a}m\frac{\ell\in G}{(?G,\gamma).m\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}m}(!G,\gamma).m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}mv\xrightarrow{a}v\frac{m\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.m}/_{x}\}\xrightarrow{\lambda}m^{\prime}}{\mathsf{rec}~x.m\xrightarrow{\lambda}m^{\prime}}\frac{m\xrightarrow{a}m^{\prime}\quad n\xrightarrow{a}n^{\prime}}{m\odot n\xrightarrow{a}m^{\prime}\odot n^{\prime}}\frac{m\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}m^{\prime}\quad n\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n^{\prime}}{m\odot n\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}m^{\prime}\odot n^{\prime}}\frac{m\xrightarrow{\lambda}m^{\prime}}{m+n\xrightarrow{\lambda}m^{\prime}}\frac{m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}}{m\odot n\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}\odot n}\frac{m\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}m^{\prime}\quad n\hskip 14.01869pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-14.01869pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}}{m\odot n\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}m^{\prime}\odot n}

Table 6.
The operational semantics for decentralized local monitors (up to commutativity of +,
\otimes
and
\oplus
), where we let
\lambda
denote either
a
,
(!G,\gamma)
or
(?\ell,\gamma)
for
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
,
G\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
.

\begin{array}[]{lll}\frac{m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}}{[m]_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}[m^{\prime}]_{\ell}}&\frac{m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}m^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447pt\ell\in G}{[m]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[m^{\prime}]_{\ell}}\\
\frac{m\hskip 10.71553pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-10.71553pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}}{[m]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[m]_{\ell}}&\frac{\ell\notin G}{[m]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[m]_{\ell}}\\
\lx@intercol\frac{M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}M^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447ptN\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N^{\prime}}{M\diamond N\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}M^{\prime}\diamond N^{\prime}}\hfil\lx@intercol\vskip 8.5359pt\\
\lx@intercol\frac{M\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}M^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447ptN\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N^{\prime}}{M\diamond N\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}M^{\prime}\diamond N^{\prime}}\hfil\lx@intercol\vskip 5.0pt\end{array}

Table 7.
Operational semantics for communication of
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
(up to commutativity of
\wedge
and
\vee
– denoted by
\diamond
).

\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{A(\ell)=a\hskip 8.19447ptm\xrightarrow{a}m^{\prime}}{[m]_{\ell}\xrightarrow{A}[m^{\prime}]_{\ell}}\\
\frac{A(\ell)=a\hskip 8.19447ptm\hskip 4.56413pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-4.56413pt\xrightarrow{a}\hskip 8.19447ptm\hskip 4.32935pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-4.32935pt\xrightarrow{c}}{[m]_{\ell}\xrightarrow{A}[\mathsf{end}]_{\ell}}\\
\frac{M\xrightarrow{A}M^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447ptN\xrightarrow{A}N^{\prime}}{M\diamond N\xrightarrow{A}M^{\prime}\diamond N^{\prime}}\end{array}

Table 8.
Operational semantics for actions of
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
(up to commutativity of
\wedge
and
\vee
– denoted by
\diamond
).

For
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
, the operational semantics can be found in
Table

8
(the rules concerning communication) and
Table

8
(the rules concerning action steps). The operational semantics in
Table

8
defines multicast, where a monitor located at
\ell
sends a message to group
G
and every monitor at a location in
G
that can receive from
\ell
does so; every monitor that cannot, or that is not in
G
, does not change its state.
The first four rules capture the judgment for inferring when all components of a monitor which are able to receive a certain
\gamma
sent from a location do so. Intuitively,
\ell
is the location from which message
\gamma
was sent to group
G
, and
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N
indicates that every monitor in
M
located at a location in
G
that can receive
\gamma
from
\ell
indeed has received
\gamma
and transitioned appropriately in
N
.
The last two rules then actually define communication. In particular, the last rule in
Table

8
implements multicast by stipulating that the outcome of the synchronization between a send action
\ell:(!G,\gamma)
and a receive one of the form
G:(?\ell,\gamma)
is the send action
itself,
which can be received by other monitors at locations in
G
in a larger monitor of which
M\diamond N
is a sub-term. We note, in passing, that monitors
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
are ‘input-enabled’: for each
M,G,\ell
and
\gamma
, there is always some
M^{\prime}
such that
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}M^{\prime}
. So the last rule in
Table

8
(and its symmetric version) can always be applied when the send transition in its premise is available.

Monitors can also locally observe an action, as prescribed by a location-to-action function
A
; the rules are given in
Table

8
.
Monitors at the same location
observe the same action.
If a monitor cannot take the action prescribed by
A
at its location, the monitor becomes
\mathsf{end}
, as stipulated by the second rule given in
Table

8
.
Note that it is not sufficient to trigger that rule when
m

cannot
exhibit action
A(\ell)
:
we also require that
m
cannot communicate.
Note that the inability of
m
to exhibit action
A(\ell)
is not sufficient to trigger that rule: we also require that
m
cannot communicate.
Intuitively, this is because monitors exhibit an ‘alternating’ behavior in which they observe the next action produced by a system hypertrace and then embark in a sequence of communications with other monitors to inform them of what they observed.
The order in which these communication messages are received is immaterial, since for receive actions a confluence property holds (at least for the monitors synthetized from formulae — see Lemma
4.37
).

As will be made clear in our definition
of a weak bisimulation relation presented in
Definition

4.2
,
such communications are interpreted as internal actions in monitor behavior. Therefore, the inability of some monitor
[m]_{\ell}
to perform action
A(\ell)
can only be gauged in ‘stable states’—that is, monitor states in which no communication is possible. This design choice is akin to that underlying the definition of refusal testing presented in
(
Phillips, 1987
)
and of the stable-failures model for (Timed) CSP defined in
(
Reed and Roscoe, 1999
;
Roscoe, 1997
)
, where the inability of a process to perform some action can only be determined in states that afford no internal computation steps.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{m\Rrightarrow v}{[m]_{\ell}\Rrightarrow v}&\frac{M\Rrightarrow\mathsf{end}\hskip 8.19447ptN\Rrightarrow\mathsf{end}}{M\diamond N\Rrightarrow\mathsf{end}}\\
\frac{M\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}}{M\wedge N\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}}&\frac{M\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}\hskip 8.19447ptN\Rrightarrow v}{M\wedge N\Rrightarrow v}\\
\frac{M\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}}{M\vee N\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}}&\frac{M\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}\hskip 8.19447ptN\Rrightarrow v}{M\vee N\Rrightarrow v}\end{array}

Table 9.
The verdict combination rules for decentralized monitors (up to commutativity of
\wedge
and
\vee
– denoted by
\diamond
).

\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{M\xrightarrow{A}M^{\prime}\hskip 8.19447ptT\xrightarrow{A}T^{\prime}}{M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail M^{\prime}\triangleright T^{\prime}}\\
\frac{M\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}M^{\prime}}{M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail M^{\prime}\triangleright T}\\
\frac{M\Rrightarrow v}{M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail v}\end{array}

Table 10.
The evolution of a decentralized monitor instrumented on a hypertrace.

Verdict evaluation for
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
is defined in
Table

10
and relies on that for
m\in\mathsf{CMon}
provided in
Table

4
.
Finally, given a decentalized monitor
M
and a hypertrace
T
, the instrumentation of the monitor on the trace is described by the rules of
Table

10
.
As before, we denote with
\rightarrowtail^{*}
the reflexive transitive closure of
\rightarrowtail
.

To conclude, let us show how the operational semantics works, by considering the following toy example:

\displaystyle m_{1}\triangleq a_{1}.m_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{where }\qquad m_{1}^{\prime}\triangleq(!\{\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\},a).m_{1}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle m_{2}\triangleq a_{2}.m_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{where }\qquad m_{2}^{\prime}\triangleq(?\ell_{1},a).m_{2}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle m_{3}\triangleq a_{3}.m_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{where }\qquad m_{3}^{\prime}\triangleq(?\ell_{1},a).m_{3}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle m_{4}\triangleq a_{4}.m^{\prime}_{4}

Assume that we have four locations
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{4}
and the 4-tuple of actions
A\triangleq[\ell_{1}\mapsto a_{1},\ell_{2}\mapsto a_{2},\ell_{3}\mapsto a_{3},\ell_{4}\mapsto a]
. Then,

m_{i}\xrightarrow{a_{i}}m_{i}^{\prime}

for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
and so

\displaystyle[m_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\xrightarrow{A}[m_{i}^{\prime}]_{\ell_{i}}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{for every }i\in\{1,2,3\}\text{ (since $a_{i}=A(\ell_{i})$)}

\displaystyle[m_{4}]_{\ell_{4}}\xrightarrow{A}[\mathsf{end}]_{\ell_{4}}

(since
a_{4}\neq A(\ell_{4})=a
)

Thus,

\bigwedge_{i=1}^{4}[m_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\xrightarrow{A}\bigwedge_{i=1}^{3}[m_{i}^{\prime}]_{\ell_{i}}\ \ \wedge\ \ [\mathsf{end}]_{\ell_{4}}

Now, we can handle communication. First, we can infer that

m_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(!\{\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\},a)}m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\qquad\text{and}\qquad m_{i}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{1},a)}m_{i}^{\prime\prime}\quad\text{for }i\in\{2,3\}

and so

[m_{1}^{\prime}]_{\ell_{1}}\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!\{\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\},a)}[m_{1}^{\prime\prime}]_{\ell_{1}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad[m_{i}^{\prime}]_{\ell_{i}}\overset{\textup{$\{\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\}:(?\ell_{1},a)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[m_{i}^{\prime\prime}]_{\ell_{i}}\quad\text{for }i\in\{2,3\}

By observing that
\mathsf{end}\hskip 16.03867pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-16.03867pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{1},a)}
, we can also infer that

[\mathsf{end}]_{\ell_{4}}\overset{\textup{$\{\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\}:(?\ell_{1},a)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[\mathsf{end}]_{\ell_{4}}

and so

[m_{2}^{\prime}]_{\ell_{2}}\wedge[m_{3}^{\prime}]_{\ell_{3}}\wedge[\mathsf{end}]_{\ell_{4}}\overset{\textup{$\{\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\}:(?\ell_{1},a)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[m_{2}^{\prime\prime}]_{\ell_{2}}\wedge[m_{3}^{\prime\prime}]_{\ell_{3}}\wedge[\mathsf{end}]_{\ell_{4}}

Finally, we have that

\bigwedge_{i=1}^{3}[m_{i}^{\prime}]_{\ell_{i}}\ \ \wedge\ \ [\mathsf{end}]_{\ell_{4}}\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!\{\ell_{2},\ell_{3}\},a)}\bigwedge_{i=1}^{3}[m_{i}^{\prime\prime}]_{\ell_{i}}\ \ \wedge\ \ [\mathsf{end}]_{\ell_{4}}

The final verdict of this monitor will be
\mathsf{end}
if all
m_{i}^{\prime\prime}
(for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
) evaluate to either
\mathsf{end}
or
\mathsf{yes}
; in contrast, if any of them evaluates to
\mathsf{no}
, then the monitor evaluates to
\mathsf{no}
. In particular, because of the failure in observing
a
in
\ell_{4}
(as stipulated by
A
), there is no way to obtain a
\mathsf{yes}
verdict.

4.2.
Synthesizing Decentralized Monitors Correctly

In this section we describe how to synthesize decentralized monitors ‘correctly’ from formulas, i.e. such that their behavior corresponds to that of the corresponding centralized monitors. The advantage of this approach is that it simplifies the proof that monitors synthesized via a ‘correct’ decentralized synthesis function are sound and violation-complete, by utilizing the correspondence to centralized monitors. Moreover, it identifies desirable properties of a ‘correct’ decentralized synthesis function that can guide the development of further automated decentralized-monitor synthesis algorithms.

We first define the correspondence between centralized and decentralized monitors and show that this correspondence is sufficient to obtain soundness and violation-completeness in the decentralized setting from the corresponding results in the centralized setting (
Theorems

3.9
and
3.12
). In the remainder of the section, given a synthesis function which takes as inputs a formula
\varphi
and a mapping
\sigma
from location variables to locations, and outputs a monitor
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\in\mathsf{DMon}
, we specify criteria that allow us to derive this correspondence.

The correspondence between the centralized and the decentralized monitors is characterized as a weak bisimulation; this first requires defining sequences of communication actions, as the analogous of unobservable transitions in process calculi.

Definition 4.1.

We write

M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}
to denote the existence of an integer
h>0
, of

h
monitors
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}
and of
h-1
locations
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{h-1}
and communication actions
c_{1},\ldots,c_{h-1}
such that
M_{1}=M
,
M_{h}=M^{\prime}
, and

M_{i}\xrightarrow{\ell_{i}:c_{i}}M_{i+1}
(for every
i=1,\ldots,h-1
).

Similarly, we write
m\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m^{\prime}
to denote the existence of an integer
h>0
, of
h
local monitors
m_{1},\dots,m_{h}
and of
h-1
actions
c_{1},\cdots c_{h-1}\in\{(!G,\gamma),(?\ell,\gamma)\mid G\subseteq{\mathcal{L}},\ell\in{\mathcal{L}},\gamma\in\mathsf{Con}\}
such that
m_{1}=m
,
m_{h}=m^{\prime}
and
m_{i}\xrightarrow{c_{i}}m_{i+1}
(for every
i=1,\ldots,h-1
).

Notice that, by definition of
\rightarrow
on communicating monitors,
each
c_{i}
is
(!G_{i},\gamma_{i})
,
for some
G_{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
and
\gamma_{i}\in\mathsf{Con}
.

Definition 4.2.

A binary relation
\mathcal{R}
over
\mathsf{DMon}\times\mathsf{CMon}
is a weak bisimulation if and only if, whenever
M\mathcal{R}m
, it holds that:

(1)

\exists M^{\prime}\in\mathsf{DMon}
such that
M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\Rrightarrow v
if and only if
m\Rrightarrow v
.

(2)

If
M\xrightarrow{A}M^{\prime}
then
\exists m^{\prime}\in\mathsf{CMon}
such that
m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\mathcal{R}m^{\prime}
.

(3)

If
M\xrightarrow{c}M^{\prime}
then
M^{\prime}\mathcal{R}m
, where
c=\ell:(!G,\gamma)
for some
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
,
G\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
,
\gamma\in\mathsf{Con}
.

(4)

If
m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
then there exist
M_{1},M_{2},M^{\prime}
such that
M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M_{1}\xrightarrow{A}M_{2}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\mathcal{R}m^{\prime}
.

We now prove four facts concerning the instrumentation rules of weakly bisimilar monitors, which we use to prove
Proposition

4.4
, which shows that centralized and decentralized monitors behaviorally agree.

Lemma 4.3.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a weak bisimulation relation such that
M\mathcal{R}m
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

If there exists
M^{\prime}
such that
M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail M^{\prime}\triangleright T^{\prime}
, then there exists
m^{\prime}
such that
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}m^{\prime}\triangleright T^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\mathcal{R}m^{\prime}
.

(2)

If there exists
m^{\prime}
such that
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail m^{\prime}\triangleright T^{\prime}
, then there exists
M^{\prime}
such that
M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}M^{\prime}\triangleright T^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\mathcal{R}m^{\prime}
.

(3)

If
M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail v
then
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail v
.

(4)

If
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail v
then
M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}v
.

Proof.

For the first item, we assume that
M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail M^{\prime}\triangleright T^{\prime}
. Hence we have that
M\xrightarrow{A}M^{\prime}
and
T\xrightarrow{A}T^{\prime}
or
M\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}M^{\prime}
and
T=T^{\prime}
. In the first case, we use the definition of weak bisimulation to obtain that
m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
for
M^{\prime}\mathcal{R}m^{\prime}
, from which we conclude that
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail m^{\prime}\triangleright T^{\prime}
. In the second case, we use the definition of weak bisimulation to obtain that
M^{\prime}\mathcal{R}m
and we also know that
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}m\triangleright T
.

For the second item, we assume that
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail m^{\prime}\triangleright T^{\prime}
. Hence we have that
m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
and
T\xrightarrow{A}T^{\prime}
. From the definition of weak bisimulation, there exist
M_{1},M_{2},M^{\prime}
such that
M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M_{1}\xrightarrow{A}M_{2}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\mathcal{R}m^{\prime}
. Thus, we can conclude that
M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}M_{1}\triangleright T
,
M_{1}\triangleright T\rightarrowtail M_{2}\triangleright T^{\prime}
and
M_{2}\triangleright T^{\prime}\rightarrowtail^{*}M^{\prime}\triangleright T^{\prime}
, from which we obtain that
M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}M^{\prime}\triangleright T^{\prime}
.

For the third item we assume that
M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail v
, from which we conclude that
M\Rrightarrow v
. From the definition of weak bisimulation we obtain immediately that
m\Rrightarrow v
and thus
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail v
.

For the fourth item we assume that
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail v
, from which we conclude that
m\Rrightarrow v
. From the definition of weak bisimulation we obtain immediately that
M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\Rrightarrow v
and thus
M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}v
.
∎

Proposition 4.4.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a weak bisimulation such that
M\mathcal{R}m
. Then,

M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}v
if and only if

m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}v
.

Proof.

For the left to right direction, assume that
M\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}v
. By definition there exist an integer
h>0
and
M_{1}\triangleright T_{1},\ldots,M_{h}\triangleright T_{h}
such that
M_{1}=M
,
T_{1}=T
,

M_{i}\triangleright T_{i}\rightarrowtail M_{i+1}\triangleright T_{i+1}
(for every
i=1,\ldots,h-1
), and

M_{h}\triangleright T_{h}\rightarrowtail v
. We proceed by induction on
h
. The base case holds because of
Lemma

4.3
(Item
3
). In the inductive step we use
Lemma

4.3
(Item
1
) to obtain
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}m_{2}\triangleright T_{2}
for some
m_{2}
and
T_{2}
such that
M_{2}\mathcal{R}m_{2}
. Then we can use the induction hypothesis to conclude that
m_{2}\triangleright T_{2}\rightarrowtail^{*}v
. Thus,
m\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}v
.

For the right to left direction, the proof is identical but uses
Lemma

4.3
(Item
4
) and
Lemma

4.3
(Item
2
) instead.
∎

This allows us to obtain violation-completeness and soundness for decentralized monitors from the corresponding results for centralized monitors:

Corollary 4.5 (Soundness).

Let
T\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
,
\varphi\in
Hyper-maxHML be a closed formula such that
\mathcal{M}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)
is defined,
and
\mathcal{R}
a weak bisimulation such that
(\mathcal{M}_{\emptyset}(\varphi),{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\emptyset}(\varphi))\in\mathcal{R}
.
If
\mathcal{M}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
, then
T\not\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket
;
if
\mathcal{M}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{yes}
, then
T\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket
.

Corollary 4.6 (Violation Completeness).

Let
T\in\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
,
\varphi\in
Hyper-maxHML be a closed formula such that
\mathcal{M}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)
is defined,
and
\mathcal{R}
a weak bisimulation such that
(\mathcal{M}_{\emptyset}(\varphi),{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\emptyset}(\varphi))\in\mathcal{R}
.
If
T\not\in\left\llbracket\varphi\right\rrbracket
, then
\mathcal{M}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)\triangleright T\rightarrowtail^{*}\mathsf{no}
.

We now describe sufficient conditions for any decentralized synthesis function such that there is a weak bisimulation between the centralized and the decentralized monitors synthesized from a formula
\varphi
and a location environment
\sigma
. Whenever we write
M\xrightarrow{c}N
for
M,N\in\mathsf{DMon}
, we assume that
c\in\{\ell:(!G,\gamma)\mid\ell\in{\mathcal{L}},G\subseteq{\mathcal{L}},\gamma\in\mathsf{Con}\}
, as per the labeling of the communication transitions of decentralized monitors. We write
[m]_{\ell}\in M
, for
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
, if
[m]_{\ell}
is one of its constituents: formally,
[m]_{\ell}\in[m]_{\ell}
and, if
[m]_{\ell}\in M
, then
[m]_{\ell}\in M\diamond N
and
[m]_{\ell}\in N\diamond M
(recall that
\diamond
denotes either
\wedge
or
\vee
).
We start
by defining when
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
can(not) communicate:

Definition 4.7.

Let
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
. We say that
M
can communicate if there exists
[m]_{\ell}\in M
such that
m\xrightarrow{c}n
, for some
c\in\mathsf{Com}
and
n\in\mathsf{LMon}
. Otherwise, we say that
M
cannot communicate.

Definition 4.8.

We say that a monitor synthesis
\mathcal{M}_{-}(-)
is
principled

when it satisfies the following conditions,
for every formula
\varphi
and environment
\sigma
such that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)
is defined:

Verdict Agreement::

for every verdict
v
,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\Rrightarrow v
if and only if
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\Rrightarrow v
;

Verdict Irrevocability::

for every verdict
v
and
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M_{2}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M
, if
M_{2}\Rrightarrow v
then
M\Rrightarrow v
;

Reactivity::

for every
A
, if
\varphi
has no free recursion variables, then there exists
M
such that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M
;

Bounded Communication::

for every
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}
, there exists
M^{\prime\prime}
such that
M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime\prime}
and
M^{\prime\prime}
cannot communicate;

Processing-Communication Alternation::

for every
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M_{1}
,

(1):

\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)
cannot communicate, and

(2):

M_{1}\xrightarrow{c}M_{2}
implies
M_{1}\hskip 7.6285pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-7.6285pt\xrightarrow{A^{\prime}}
, for every
A^{\prime}
;

Formula Convergence::

if
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}
, with
M^{\prime}
that cannot communicate, and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
, for some formula
\varphi^{\prime}
and environment
\sigma^{\prime}
, then
M^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
.

From the Formula Convergence property, we can immediately derive an auxiliary property:

Lemma 4.9 (Uniqueness).

If
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M_{1}
, with
M_{1}
that cannot communicate, and
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M_{2}
, with
M_{2}
that cannot communicate, then
M_{1}=M_{2}
.

Proof.

Indeed, since
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)
has a possible transition for every
A
, by
Section

3.2
we know that

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
. By applying Formula Convergence twice, we obtain
M_{1}=\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})=M_{2}
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{M}_{-}(-)
be a decentralized synthesis function.
We define relation
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{M}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\left(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi),{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\right)\mid\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{l}}(\varphi)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)\text{ and }\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{r}}(\varphi)=\emptyset\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}

\displaystyle\triangleq\left\{\left(M^{\prime},{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})\right)\mid\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{l}}(\varphi)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma),~\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{r}}(\varphi)=\emptyset\text{ and }\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})\right\}

The crucial property of any principled synthesis function is the following:

Theorem 4.10.

For every principled synthesis
\mathcal{M}_{-}(-)
,

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{M}}
is a weak bisimulation.

Proof.

To prove that
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{M}}
is a weak bisimulation, we first consider a pair
(\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi),{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi))\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. The first condition of weak bisimulation holds because, from Processing-Communication Alternation and Reactivity, we conclude that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, and thus
M=M^{\prime}
. Then the result follows from Verdict Agreement.

For the second condition of weak bisimulation, we assume that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M
. Since
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)
has a possible transition for every
A
, by
Section

3.2
we know that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
for some
\sigma^{\prime}
and
\varphi^{\prime}
. From Bounded Communication, we can use Formula Convergence to obtain that
M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
. By definition,
M\mathcal{R}_{2}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
and this suffices to conclude.

The third condition of weak bisimulation is not relevant, as
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
via Processing-Communication Alternation and Reactivity.
To check the fourth condition, we assume that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
(because of

Section

3.2
, we know that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}
results in a monitor synthesized from a formula). Via Reactivity we conclude that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M
for some
M
. Then we can use Bounded Communication and Formula Convergence to derive that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
. As
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})\mathcal{R}_{1}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
, we can conclude.

Now consider a pair
(M^{\prime},{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{R}_{2}
, with
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
for some
\sigma,\varphi
and
M
.
To verify the first condition from left to right, we assume
M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime\prime}
and
M^{\prime\prime}\Rrightarrow v
. Since
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)
has a possible transition for every
A
and
Section

3.2
, we know that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
for some
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
and
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
. Hence, we can use Bounded Communication and Formula Convergence to conclude that
M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
. Furthermore, we obtain from Uniqueness and Processing-Communication Alternation that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})=\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
. Then we apply Verdict Irrevocability to conclude that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})\Rrightarrow v
. Finally, we can conclude using Verdict Agreement.
For the right to left direction, we use Verdict Agreement to obtain that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})\Rrightarrow v
, which immediately satisfies the first condition of weak bisimulation.
For the second condition of weak bisimulation, we assume that
M^{\prime}\xrightarrow{A^{\prime}}M^{\prime\prime}
. From Processing-Communication Alternation, we conclude that
M^{\prime}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, and thus that
M^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
. This sends back to the second condition for pairs in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
.

For the third condition, we assume that
M^{\prime}\xrightarrow{c}M^{\prime\prime}
. Since
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)
has a possible transition for every
A
and
Section

3.2
, we know that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
for some
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
and
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
. Hence, we can use Bounded Communication and Formula Convergence to conclude that
M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
. Furthermore, we obtain from Uniqueness and Processing-Communication Alternation that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})=\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
. By definition,
(M^{\prime\prime},{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{R}_{2}
and this suffices to conclude.

To check the fourth condition, we assume that

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{A^{\prime}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
.
We note that if
N\rightarrow N^{\prime}
and
N
has no free recursion variables, then neither does
N^{\prime}
— by straightforward induction on the derivation of
N\rightarrow N^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
has no free recursion variables, and neither does
\varphi^{\prime}
.
Via Reactivity we conclude that
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{A^{\prime}}M_{1}
. Then we use Bounded Communication and Formula Convergence to derive that
M_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
, resulting in

M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{A^{\prime}}M_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathcal{M}_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
,
which concludes the proof.
∎

4.3.
From Formulas to Decentralized Monitors

We now describe how to synthesize decentralized monitors for a fragment of Hyper-maxHML, and show that this synthesis function satisfies
Definition

4.8
. This allows us to apply
Theorem

4.10
and obtain soundness and violation-completeness of these synthesized monitors.

In what follows, we consider formulas from PHyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML, the subset of Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML given by the following grammar (see
Section

5
for a discussion on the fragment chosen):

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle::=\forall\pi.\varphi~\mid~\exists\pi.\varphi~\mid~\varphi\land\varphi~\mid~\varphi\lor\varphi~\mid~\psi

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle::=\mathsf{tt}\,\mid\,\mathsf{ff}\,\mid\,\psi\wedge\psi\,\mid\,\psi\lor\psi\,\mid\,\pi=\pi\,\mid\,\pi\neq\pi\,\mid\,\max x.\psi\,\mid\,\min x.\psi\,\mid\,x\,\mid\,[a_{\pi}]\psi\,\mid\,\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi

We denote the class of formulas of type
\psi
with
\mathsf{Qf}
(quantifier free).
PHyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML is a subset of Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML and thus its semantics over
\mathsf{HTrc}_{\mathcal{L}}
is the
one given in
Table

1
.

We synthesize decentralized monitors for the fragment of PHyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML only containing formulas of type

\varphi

without
least fixed-points,
which we call PHyper-maxHML.
The synthesis for decentralized monitors is given in
Table

11
.
To derive monitors belonging to
\mathsf{DMon}

for formulas of type
\varphi
(through function
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}
),
we need to derive a monitor belonging to
\mathsf{LMon}
for formulas of type
\psi\in\mathsf{Qf}
(through function
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}
).
The
synthesis functions are parametrized by a location
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
and a partial function
\sigma
from
\Pi
to
{\mathcal{L}}

that is defined for every free location variable in
\psi
and
\varphi
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}lr@{\,}l}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\forall\pi.\varphi)&=\bigwedge_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi)&\qquad{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\exists\pi.\varphi)&=\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi)\\
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi\wedge\varphi^{\prime})&={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\wedge{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi^{\prime})&\qquad{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi\vee\varphi^{\prime})&={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\vee{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi^{\prime})\\
\lx@intercol\hfil{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\begin{cases}\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)]_{\ell}&\mbox{if }\sigma\neq\emptyset\\
[v]_{\ell_{0}}&\mbox{if }\sigma=\emptyset\wedge{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\Rrightarrow v\end{cases}\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{r@{\,}lr@{\,}l}\lx@intercol\hfil{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\mathsf{tt})=\mathsf{yes}\qquad{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\mathsf{ff})=\mathsf{no}\qquad{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(x)=x\qquad{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\max x.\psi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\hfil\lx@intercol\vskip 8.5359pt\\
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi\wedge\psi^{\prime})&={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\otimes{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})&{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi\vee\psi^{\prime})&={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\oplus{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})\\
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\pi=\pi^{\prime})&=\begin{cases}\mathsf{yes}&\mbox{if }\sigma(\pi)=\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\\
\mathsf{no}&\mbox{otherwise }\end{cases}&{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\pi\neq\pi^{\prime})&=\begin{cases}\mathsf{yes}&\mbox{if }\sigma(\pi)\neq\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\\
\mathsf{no}&\mbox{otherwise }\end{cases}\\
\lx@intercol\hfil{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}([a_{\pi}]\psi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle a.(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma){\setminus}\{\ell\}),a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)+\displaystyle\sum_{b\neq a}b.(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma){\setminus}\{\ell\}),b).\mathsf{yes}&\mbox{if }\sigma(\pi)=\ell\\
\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\mathsf{Act}}b.\Big((?\{\sigma(\pi)\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\ +\displaystyle\sum_{b\neq a}(?\{\sigma(\pi)\},b).\mathsf{yes}\Big)&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\hfil\lx@intercol\vskip 8.5359pt\\
\lx@intercol\hfil{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle a.(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma){\setminus}\{\ell\}),a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)+\displaystyle\sum_{b\neq a}b.(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma){\setminus}\{\ell\}),b).\mathsf{no}&\mbox{if }\sigma(\pi)=\ell\\
\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\mathsf{Act}}b.\Big((?\{\sigma(\pi)\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\ +\displaystyle\sum_{b\neq a}(?\{\sigma(\pi)\},b).\mathsf{no}\Big)&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

Table 11.
Decentralized monitor synthesis, where
\ell_{0}
is any fixed element of
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Note that, in the definition of
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)
,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)
is the monitor resulting from the centralized synthesis function defined in
Table

5
. Intuitively
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)
synthesizes a local monitor at each location relevant to
\psi
, which are the locations associated by
\sigma
to the free location variables in
\psi
. If
\sigma=\emptyset
, then
\psi
does not have any free location variable and it is a boolean combination of
\mathsf{tt}
and
\mathsf{ff}
; thus, there is no need for communication between locations. In fact, a verdict can be obtained from
\psi
immediately
and it coincides with the one reached in the centralized synthesis.

Thus, every closed formula
\varphi
on which we apply our synthesis is

(1)

either
trivial
, i.e.,
\varphi
is logically equivalent to
\mathsf{tt}
or
\mathsf{ff}
,

(2)

or is such that every subformula
\psi\in\mathsf{Qf}
of
\varphi
is in the scope of a quantifier.

For non-trivial formulas,
the
\sigma=\emptyset
case for
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)
never applies, and we can ignore it.
The decentralized monitor for a closed formula
\varphi
is
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)
.

{textAtEnd}

[allend, category=syn]
The monitor
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)
is well-defined for closed formulas because of the following lemma:

Lemma 4.11.

If
\psi
has no free variables, then
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\Rrightarrow v
for some
v
.

Proof.

The proof follows from induction on
\psi
, making use of the fact that all formulas are assumed to be guarded.
∎

Remark 4.12.

In the first clause of the definition of the synthesis function for box formulas, it might seem superfluous to send a message also when the monitor observes some
b\neq a
. However, this is important to make sure monitors do not deadlock. To see this, consider a synthesis where that definition instead looks like

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}([a_{\pi}]\psi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle a.(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma){\setminus}\{\ell\}),a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)+\displaystyle\sum_{b\neq a}b.\mathsf{yes}&\mbox{if }\sigma(\pi)=\ell\\
\displaystyle\sum_{b\in\mathsf{Act}}b.(?\{\sigma(\pi)\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Consider
\mathsf{Act}=\{a,b\}
,
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}
and some hypertrace
T
such that
T(\ell)=b.t_{1}
and
T(\ell^{\prime})=b.t_{2}
for some traces
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Now consider
m\otimes n
,
where
m={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}([a_{\pi}]\psi)
,
n={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}([a_{\pi^{\prime}}]\psi^{\prime})
,
\sigma(\pi)=\ell
and
\sigma(\pi^{\prime})=\ell^{\prime}
. For
A(\ell)=A(\ell^{\prime})=b\neq a
, we then get
m\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}\mathsf{yes}
and
n\xrightarrow{A(\ell^{\prime})}(?\{\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
, and monitor
\mathsf{yes}\otimes(?\{\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
is stuck because the receive action of the monitor
(?\{\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
has no matching send. It is precisely to avoid these scenarios that we make sure that, for each sending transition, there is a corresponding receiving transition, and a monitor always sends the last action it read to all other locations in the range of the environment
\sigma
.

The same applies to the synthesis of diamonds, being the latter the same as the synthesis for box formulas in Table
11
with
\mathsf{no}
verdicts in place of
\mathsf{yes}
.

\blacktriangleleft

Example 4.13.

In order to highlight the inter-monitor communication, we consider the following formula

\varphi=\exists\pi.\exists\pi^{\prime}.([a_{\pi}]\mathsf{ff}\wedge[b_{\pi^{\prime}}]\mathsf{ff})

over
{\mathcal{L}}=\{1,2\}
and
\mathsf{Act}=\{a,b\}
, which states that the two traces must start with different actions. Indeed,
[a_{\pi}]\mathsf{ff}
requires that the trace at the location bound to
\pi
does not start with an
a
and the one at the location bound to
\pi^{\prime}
does not start with a
b
. Hence, the only way to satisfy this formula is to associate different locations to
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
, in such a way that the trace at the location bound to
\pi
starts with a
b
and the trace at the location bound to
\pi^{\prime}
starts with an
a
.
By letting
\sigma=[\pi\mapsto\ell,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto\ell^{\prime}]
, the synthesis function applied to this property gives:

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\emptyset}(\varphi)=\bigvee_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}}\ \ \bigvee_{\ell^{\prime\prime}\in\{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\}}\left[{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma}([a_{\pi}]\mathsf{ff}\wedge[b_{\pi^{\prime}}]\mathsf{ff})\right]_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}

where

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime\prime}}_{\sigma}([a_{\pi}]\mathsf{ff}\wedge[b_{\pi^{\prime}}]\mathsf{ff})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(a.(!\emptyset,a).\mathsf{no}+b.(!\emptyset,b).\mathsf{yes})\ \otimes&\text{if $\ell=\ell^{\prime}=\ell^{\prime\prime}$}\\
\quad(b.(!\emptyset,b).\mathsf{no}+a.(!\emptyset,a).\mathsf{yes})\\
(a.(!\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).\mathsf{no}+b.(!\{\ell^{\prime}\},b).\mathsf{yes})\ \otimes&\text{if $\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}$ and $\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell$}\\
\quad(a.((?\{\ell^{\prime}\},b).\mathsf{no}+(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).\mathsf{yes})\ +\\
\quad\ \,b.((?\{\ell^{\prime}\},b).\mathsf{no}+(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).\mathsf{yes}))\\
(a.((?\{\ell\},a).\mathsf{no}+(?\{\ell\},b).\mathsf{yes})\ +&\text{if $\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}$ and $\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell^{\prime}$}\\
\ \,b.((?\{\ell\},a).\mathsf{no}+(?\{\ell\},b).\mathsf{yes}))\\
\otimes\ (b.(!\{\ell\},b).\mathsf{no}+a.(!\{\ell\},a).\mathsf{yes})&\phantom{\text{if $\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}$ and $\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell^{\prime}$}}\blacktriangleleft\end{array}\right.

Remark 4.14.

We explicitly added in Table
11
a rule for formulas of the form
\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi
even though such formulas are logically equivalent to

(4)

[a_{\pi}]\psi\textstyle\wedge\bigwedge_{b\neq a}[b_{\pi}]\mathsf{ff}.

However, working with this formulation of the diamond has drawbacks in terms of the size of the resulting monitor.
To showcase this, consider the decentralized synthesis applied on Wolper’s property (
\varphi^{h}_{e}
of (
2
) from Example
2.4
),
expressed here as
\exists\pi.\psi
, with

\psi=\max x.(\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2})\qquad\psi_{1}=[a_{\pi}]\langle a_{\pi}\rangle x\qquad\psi_{2}=[b_{\pi}]\langle a_{\pi}\rangle x

Let
{\mathcal{L}}=\{1,2\}
and
\mathsf{Act}=\{a,b\}
.
The synthesis is applied thus:

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\emptyset}(\varphi^{h}_{e})=\textstyle\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}\left[\mathsf{rec}~x.\left({}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\psi_{1})\otimes{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\psi_{2})\right)\right]_{\ell}

with

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\psi_{1})&=&a.(!\emptyset,a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\langle a_{\pi}\rangle x)+b.(!\emptyset,b).\mathsf{yes}\\
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\psi_{2})&=&b.(!\emptyset,b).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\langle a_{\pi}\rangle x)+a.(!\emptyset,a).\mathsf{yes}\end{array}

and, by using the decentralized monitor synthesis for diamond formulas based on (
4
),

(5)

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\langle a_{\pi}\rangle x)=(a.(!\emptyset,a).x+b.(!\emptyset,b).\mathsf{yes})\ \otimes\ (b.(!\emptyset,b).\mathsf{no}+a.(!\emptyset,a).\mathsf{yes})

This indicates that the synthesis for diamonds based on (
4
) leads to monitors with a high degree of parallelism; for simplicity, we have just two parallel components in (
5
) because we assumed to have just two actions. However,
|\mathsf{Act}|-1
parallel conjunctions are required in general for every diamond (and may lead to an exponential blow up with several diamonds in sequence).
By contrast, using the rule for diamonds given in Table
11
reduces (
5
) to:

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\langle a_{\pi}\rangle x)=a.(!\emptyset,a).x+b.(!\emptyset,b).\mathsf{no}

and the synthesized monitor now contains no occurrence of any parallel operator.

\blacktriangleleft

Soundness and violation completeness for the synthesis defined in
Table

11
follow from
Corollary

4.5
and
4.6
by using
Theorem

4.10
, once we prove the following key result:

Theorem 4.15.

The synthesis function
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}
defined in
Table

11
is principled.

The proof is long and technical. It is carried out by showing that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}
satisfies the six properties
of
Definition

4.8
. The details are in Appendix
B
.

{textAtEnd}

[allend, category=corr]
We now prove that the synthesis defined in
Table

11
satisfies the properties in
Definition

4.8
by proving one property in each of the following subsections.
We start with some preliminary definitions and results that will be used for proving different properties.

We start with a lemma that is the equivalent of
Lemma

3.8
for
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(-)
; the proof is identical. Then, we show that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)
cannot communicate.

Lemma 4.16.

If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})=m
,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})=n
, and the free location variables of
\psi_{2}
are not bound in
\psi_{1}
, then
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1}\{{}^{\psi_{2}}/{}_{x}\})=m\{{}^{n}/{}_{x}\}
.

Lemma 4.17.

Let
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)
be defined; then, for all
[m]_{\ell}\in{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)
and
c\in\{(!G,\gamma),(?\ell,\gamma)\mid G\subseteq{\mathcal{L}},\ell\in{\mathcal{L}},\gamma\in\mathsf{Con}\}
, we have that
m\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
.

Proof.

The statement follows from a straightforward induction on
\varphi
and the following claim: for all
\ell,\psi,\sigma
and
c\in\{(!G,\gamma),(?\ell,\gamma)\mid G\subseteq{\mathcal{L}},\ell\in{\mathcal{L}},\gamma\in\mathsf{Con}\}
, it holds that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
.
Such claim can be proved by induction on
\xrightarrow{c}
to show that, if
m\xrightarrow{c}n
, then
m\neq{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
for all
\sigma,\psi,\ell
.
∎

As most results in this work concern monitors that are reached in a run from a synthesized decentralized monitor, it is convenient to capture this in a definition.

Definition 4.18.

We say that a monitor
M
is
A
-derived
if, for all
[m]_{\ell}\in M
, there exist
\psi,\sigma,m^{\prime}
such that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m
. A monitor
M
is
action-derived
if there exists an
A
such that
M
is
A
-derived. We call a local monitor
m
action-derived/A-derived for
\ell
if
[m]_{\ell}
is action-derived/A-derived.

We define a syntactic characterization of the kinds of monitors that are action-derived. This provides us with a convenient inductive proof principle.

Definition 4.19.

The class of
relevant local monitors
is defined for
\psi,\psi_{a}\in\mathsf{Qf}
by the grammar:

\displaystyle m::=~

\displaystyle{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)

\displaystyle~\mid~

\displaystyle(!G,a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)

\displaystyle~\mid~

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})~\mid~

\displaystyle m\otimes m

\displaystyle~\mid~

\displaystyle m\oplus m.

The class of relevant communicating monitors is then defined with the following grammar:

\displaystyle M::=~~[m]_{\ell}~~\mid~~M\land M~~\mid~~M\lor M,\hfill

where
m
is a relevant local monitor and
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
.
When it is clear from the context if a monitor is a relevant local monitor or a relevant communicating monitor, we will simply call it a relevant monitor, or say that the monitor is relevant.

The classes of relevant communicating/local monitors are closed under transitions:

Lemma 4.20.

Let
M
and
m
be a relevant communicating monitor and a relevant local monitor, respectively. If
M\xrightarrow{\lambda}M^{\prime}
and
m\xrightarrow{\lambda}m^{\prime}
, then
M^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime}
are a relevant communicating monitor and a relevant local monitor, respectively.

Proof.

The proof for
M\xrightarrow{\lambda}M^{\prime}
follows directly from the result for
m\xrightarrow{\lambda}m^{\prime}
;
this can be proved by induction on
\xrightarrow{\lambda}
and the only interesting case is for
m={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
.
By
Lemma

4.17
,
it must be that
\lambda=a\in\mathsf{Act}
.
The only base cases are
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=a.m^{\prime}
, but this cannot occur because of definition of the synthesis function and our assumption that
|\mathsf{Act}|\geq 2
, or
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m^{\prime}=v
, that is trivial.

For the inductive case, when
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=n_{1}+n_{2}
and
n_{1}\xrightarrow{a}m^{\prime}
,
\psi
must be of the form
[a^{\prime}_{\pi}]\psi^{\prime}

or
\langle a^{\prime}_{\pi}\rangle\psi^{\prime}
;
so

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=a^{\prime}.(!\{\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\mid\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\neq\ell\},a^{\prime}).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})+\sum_{b\neq a^{\prime}}b.(!\{\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\mid\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\neq\ell\},b).v

or

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\sum_{b\in\mathsf{Act}}b.\big((?\{\sigma(\pi)\},a^{\prime}).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})+\sum_{b\neq a^{\prime}}(?\{\sigma(\pi)\},b).v\big)

where
v
is
\mathsf{yes}
, if
\psi
is a box, and is
\mathsf{no}
, if
\psi
is a diamond.
In all cases, it is immediate that
m^{\prime}
is a relevant monitor, regardless of whether
a=a^{\prime}
or not.

Next we consider the case for
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m=m_{1}\odot m_{2}
and
m^{\prime}=m_{1}^{\prime}\odot m_{2}^{\prime}
. Hence,
\psi=\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2}
(for
\diamond=\wedge
if
\odot=\otimes
and
\diamond=\vee
if
\odot=\oplus
),
m_{1}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\xrightarrow{a}m_{1}^{\prime}
and
m_{2}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})\xrightarrow{a}m_{2}^{\prime}
. From the induction hypothesis it follows that
m_{1}^{\prime}
and
m_{2}^{\prime}
are relevant monitors, thus making
m^{\prime}
relevant as well.

Last we consider
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.n\xrightarrow{a}m^{\prime}
because
n\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.n}/_{x}\}\xrightarrow{a}m^{\prime}
. Thus we obtain that
\psi=\max x.\psi^{\prime}
and
n={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
. As
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\})
(
Lemma

4.16
), we use the induction hypothesis to obtain that
m^{\prime}
is relevant.
∎

Relevant monitors can be used to prove properties of action-derived monitors, because of the following:

Lemma 4.21.

Every action-derived monitor
M
is relevant.

Proof.

Straightforward from
Lemma

4.20
.
∎

{textAtEnd}

[allend, category=va]

To relate verdicts of centralized and decentralized monitors, we need the following intermediary result:

Lemma 4.22.

Let
\psi\in\mathsf{Qf}
and
\varphi\in
PHyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML.

(1)

If there exists
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
for which
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=v
, then
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=v
.

(2)

If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=v
, then for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=v
.

Proof.

For the first statement we only prove the case for
v=\mathsf{yes}
, the case for
v=\mathsf{no}
is dual. We observe that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{yes}
implies the following:
\psi=\mathsf{tt}
,
\psi=(\pi=\pi^{\prime})
and
\sigma(\pi)=\sigma(\pi^{\prime})
, or
\psi=(\pi\neq\pi^{\prime})
and
\sigma(\pi)\neq\sigma(\pi^{\prime})
. In all these cases we obtain immediately that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{yes}
.

For the second statement, we prove only the case with
v=\mathsf{no}
. We observe that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{no}
implies that
\psi=\mathsf{ff}
,
\psi=(\pi=\pi^{\prime})
and
\sigma(\pi)\neq\sigma(\pi^{\prime})
, or
\psi=(\pi\neq\pi^{\prime})
and
\sigma(\pi)=\sigma(\pi^{\prime})
(by definition,
\psi
is quantifier-free). In all these cases we have that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{no}
, for all
\ell
.
∎

We first relate the verdicts of centralized and decentralized synthesized monitors for
\psi
-formulas.

Lemma 4.23.

Let
\sigma
be such that
\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{l}}(\psi)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
.
If there exists
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
such that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\Rrightarrow v
, then
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\Rrightarrow v
. Conversely, if
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\Rrightarrow v
, then
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\Rrightarrow v
, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

First, observe that
\psi
is quantifier-free (by definition) and that the definition of
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)
is the same as the definition of
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
except for the box and diamond modalities.
Hence, we only prove the first statement by induction on
\Rrightarrow
, as the other proof is identical.

For the base case, we can skip the case for
\mathsf{end}
(as a synthesized monitor never takes that shape) and so the only possibility is
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=v\Rrightarrow v
: the result follows immediately from
Lemma

4.22
.

For the inductive case, we can skip the case for
+
: if
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m+n
, then either
\psi=[a_{\pi}]\psi^{\prime}
or
\psi=\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi^{\prime}
; from the synthesis function, it then follows that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
cannot evaluate to a verdict.

If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m\otimes n\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
because
m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
, then
\psi=\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})=m
.
We use the induction hypothesis to derive that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
and thus
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\Rrightarrow v
.
The case for
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m\oplus n\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
because
m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
is dual.

Next we consider

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m\otimes n\Rrightarrow v
because
m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
and
n\Rrightarrow v
. Thus
\psi=\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})=m
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})=n
. We use the induction hypothesis to derive that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})\Rrightarrow v
, which concludes the proof.
The case for
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m\oplus n\Rrightarrow v
because
m\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
and
n\Rrightarrow v
is dual.

Finally, if
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.m\Rrightarrow v
because
m\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.m}/_{x}\}\Rrightarrow v
, then
\psi=\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}
and
m={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
. As
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\})
, from
Lemma

4.16
, we get that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\})\Rrightarrow v
. By the induction hypothesis, we obtain that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\})\Rrightarrow v
. This, together with
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\})
(that holds by
Lemma

3.8
), implies that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})}/_{x}\}\Rrightarrow v
. The latter gives that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})\Rrightarrow v
.
∎

The next lemma states a few basic facts about verdict evaluation in a centralized and decentralized setting; the proof is immediate. Notationally, when
I=\emptyset
, we let
\bigvee_{i\in I}m_{i}=\mathsf{no}=\bigoplus_{i\in I}m_{i}
and
\bigwedge_{i\in I}m_{i}=\mathsf{yes}=\bigotimes_{i\in I}m_{i}
.

Lemma 4.24.

(1)

\bigoplus_{i\in I}m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \exists i\in I.m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \bigvee_{i\in I}m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
;

(2)

\bigoplus_{i\in I}m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \forall i\in I.m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \bigvee_{i\in I}m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
;

(3)

\bigotimes_{i\in I}m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \forall i\in I.m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \bigwedge_{i\in I}m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
;

(4)

\bigotimes_{i\in I}m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \exists i\in I.m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}\ \ \Leftrightarrow\ \ \bigwedge_{i\in I}m_{i}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
.

Now we can relate verdicts for monitors synthesized from any formula in the centralized and decentralized setting, thus proving Verdict Agreement.

Lemma 4.25.

Let
\varphi\in
PHyper-maxHML and
\sigma
be such that
\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{l}}(\varphi)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
. Then,

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\Rrightarrow v\Leftrightarrow{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\Rrightarrow v
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on
\varphi
. From the synthesis functions we know that
v\neq\mathsf{end}
. For the base case we consider
\varphi=\psi
. We distinguish when

\sigma=\emptyset
or not. If
\sigma=\emptyset
,
by assumption we know that
\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{l}}(\psi)=\emptyset
and so
\psi
is trivial;
therefore,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=v\Rrightarrow v
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)=[v]_{\ell_{0}}\Rrightarrow v
.
Let us now consider the case for
\sigma\neq\emptyset
.
If
v=\mathsf{no}
, we derive:

\displaystyle{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)]_{\ell}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}

(
Lemma

4.24
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma).[{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)]_{\ell}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}

(
Lemma

4.23
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}

If
v=\mathsf{yes}
, we have:

\displaystyle{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)]_{\ell}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}

(
Lemma

4.24
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\exists\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma).[{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)]_{\ell}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\exists\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}

(
Lemma

4.23
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}

For the inductive step, we first consider the case for
\exists\pi.\varphi
.
If
v=\mathsf{yes}
, we derive

\displaystyle{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\exists\pi.\varphi)

\displaystyle=\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi)\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}

(
Lemma

4.24
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\exists\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi)\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle IH}}{{\Leftrightarrow}}\exists\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi)\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}

(
Lemma

4.24
)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\exists\pi.\varphi)\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}

The case for
v=\mathsf{no}
is dual.
The cases for
\forall\pi.\varphi
,
\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
and
\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
are similar.
∎

{textAtEnd}

[allend, category=vi]

We start by proving Verdict Irrevocability at the level of local monitors.

Lemma 4.26.

Let
m
be a relevant local monitor. If
m\xrightarrow{c}n
and
m\Rrightarrow v
, then
n\Rrightarrow v
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
m
.
The base case for
m={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
can be concluded from
Lemma

4.17
; in the base cases for
m=(!G,a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
and
m=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})
, the premise
m\Rrightarrow v
is not satisfied.

For the inductive step,
if
m=n_{1}\otimes n_{2}
with
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
relevant monitors, then
m\Rrightarrow v
yields two cases (by
Lemma

3.2
,
v\neq\mathsf{end}
and we can thus exclude that case):

(1)

n_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
and
v=\mathsf{no}
.
From
m\xrightarrow{c}n
we derive another three cases, where we let
n=o_{1}\otimes o_{2}
:

(a)

n_{1}\xrightarrow{c}o_{1}
and
n_{2}=o_{2}
. We use the induction hypothesis to obtain that
o_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
. Then,
n\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
follows immediately.

(b)

n_{2}\xrightarrow{c}o_{2}
and
n_{1}=o_{1}
. Like before,
o_{2}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
and
n\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
.

(c)

c=(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)
,
n_{1}\xrightarrow{c}o_{1}
and
n_{2}\xrightarrow{c}o_{2}
. By induction,
o_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
,
o_{2}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
and
n\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
.

(2)

n_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
and
n_{2}\Rrightarrow v
.
This case is symmetric.

The case for
\oplus
is similar to the one for
\otimes
and, therefore, we omit it.
∎

Next we show that the verdict is not
\mathsf{end}
and that verdicts carry over receiving transitions:

Lemma 4.27.

Let
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
be action-derived. If
M\Rrightarrow v
, then
v\neq\mathsf{end}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from a simple induction on
\Rrightarrow
and
Lemma

3.2
.
∎

Lemma 4.28.

Let
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
be action-derived.
If
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N
and
M\Rrightarrow v
, then
N\Rrightarrow v
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N
. For the first base case, we consider
M=[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}
,
N=[n]_{\ell^{\prime}}
and
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n
: then,
M\Rrightarrow v
implies that
m\Rrightarrow v
, and (via
Lemma

4.26
, that can be used thanks to
Lemma

4.21
) we can conclude
n\Rrightarrow v
, thus
N\Rrightarrow v
. In the other two base cases, we have
M=N
and the result follows immediately.

For the inductive step we consider
M=M_{1}\otimes M_{2}
(the case for
M=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
is similar),
N=N_{1}\otimes N_{2}
,
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}
and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
. From
M_{1}\otimes M_{2}\Rrightarrow v
we distinguish two cases, where we use
Lemma

4.27
to exclude the case for
v=\mathsf{end}
:

(1)

M_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
and
M_{2}\Rrightarrow v
, From the induction hypothesis we get
N_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
and
N_{2}\Rrightarrow v
. We immediately obtain that
N_{1}\otimes N_{2}\Rrightarrow v
according to the transition rules for
\Rrightarrow
.

(2)

M_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
and
v=\mathsf{no}
. We use the induction hypothesis to obtain that
N_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
, and the result follows immediately.∎

We first prove Verdict Irrevocability for just one communication step;
Verdict Irrevocability then easily follows via straightforward induction.

Lemma 4.29.

Let
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
be action-derived.
If
M\xrightarrow{c}N
and
M\Rrightarrow v
, then
N\Rrightarrow v
.

Proof.

Let
c=\ell:(!G,\gamma)
; we proceed by induction on
M\xrightarrow{c}N
. For the base case, we consider
M=[m]_{\ell}
,
N=[n]_{\ell}
and
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n
: then,
M\Rrightarrow v
implies that
m\Rrightarrow v
; we conclude by
Lemma

4.21
and
4.26
.

For the inductive step, we consider
M=M_{1}\otimes M_{2}
(the case for
M=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
is similar),
N=N_{1}\otimes N_{2}
,
M_{1}\xrightarrow{c}N_{1}
and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
. From
M_{1}\otimes M_{2}\Rrightarrow v
we distinguish four cases, where we use
Lemma

4.27
to exclude the case for
v=\mathsf{end}
:

(1)

M_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
,
v=\mathsf{no}
. We use the induction hypothesis to obtain that
N_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
, and the result follows.

(2)

M_{2}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
,
v=\mathsf{no}
. We use
Lemma

4.28
to derive that
N_{2}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{no}
, which concludes the proof.

(3)

M_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
,
M_{2}\Rrightarrow v
. We use the induction hypothesis to obtain that
N_{1}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
and
Lemma

4.28
to derive that
N_{2}\Rrightarrow v
, and the result follows.

(4)

M_{2}\Rrightarrow\mathsf{yes}
,
M_{1}\Rrightarrow v
. Same as the previous case.
∎

Corollary 4.30.

If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M_{2}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M
and
M_{2}\Rrightarrow v
, then
M\Rrightarrow v
.

Proof.

By induction on the number of transitions in
M_{2}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M
. In the base case,
M_{2}=M
and the claim is trivial. For the inductive step, we consider
M_{2}\xrightarrow{c}N\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M
: via
Lemma

4.29
, we get that
N\Rrightarrow v
and the result then follows from the induction hypothesis.
∎

{textAtEnd}

[allend, category=react]

We start by defining an ordering on the formulae of kind
\psi
, based on the number of top-level fixed-point operators in them, where by “top-level” we mean “not under the scope of a modality”. For example, the number of top-level fixed-points in

(6)

\max x.\max y.([a_{\pi}]x\wedge\langle b_{\pi^{\prime}}\rangle y)\ \ \vee\ \ \max z.[a_{\pi}]\max t.(\langle b_{\pi^{\prime}}\rangle t\wedge z)

is 3,
\max t
being the only non-top-level fixed-point operator.

Definition 4.31.

We let
\psi\prec\psi^{\prime}
iff

(1)

the number of top-level fixed-point operators in
\psi
is smaller than the number of top-level fixed-point operators in
\psi^{\prime}
, or

(2)

the number is the same and
\psi
is a strict sub-formula of
\psi^{\prime}
.

Notice that
\prec
is well-founded and that the bottom elements are

\mathsf{tt}
,
\mathsf{ff}
,
\pi=\pi
,
\pi\neq\pi
and
x
. Coming back to (
6
), we have that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\max z.[a_{\pi}]\max t.(\langle b_{\pi^{\prime}}\rangle t\wedge z)&\prec&(\max x.[b_{\pi}]x\wedge\max y.\langle a_{\pi^{\prime}}\rangle y)\\
\max t.(\langle b_{\pi^{\prime}}\rangle t\wedge z)&\prec&\max z.[a_{\pi}]\max t.(\langle b_{\pi^{\prime}}\rangle t\wedge z)\end{array}

respectively by point 1 and 2 of
Definition

4.31
.

Lemma 4.32.

For all
\varphi\in
PHyper-maxHML without free recursion variables,
\sigma
such that
\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{l}}(\varphi)\subseteq\mathsf{dom}(\sigma)
, and
A:{\mathcal{L}}\rightarrow\mathsf{Act}
, there exists
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
such that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\varphi
;
the proof is trivial for all the inductive cases. So we only treat the base case, where
\varphi=\psi\in\mathsf{Qf}
and has no free recursion variables.
We prove that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
is reactive by induction on
\prec
.
The base cases are the bottom elements of
\prec
and they all entail that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
is a verdict; then, the claim follows immediately because
v\xrightarrow{a}v
holds for each
v
and
a
.
For the inductive case:

•

If
\psi=[a_{\pi}]\psi^{\prime}
or
\psi=\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi^{\prime}
, for some
a
,
\pi
and
\psi^{\prime}
, then
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
is reactive by construction.

•

If
\psi=\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2}
, then
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\odot{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})
(with
\odot=\otimes
if
\diamond=\wedge
and
\odot=\oplus
if
\diamond=\vee
). By
Definition

4.31
,
\psi_{1}\prec\psi
and
\psi_{2}\prec\psi
; hence, the inductive hypothesis yields that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})
are reactive. Reactivity of
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
immediately follows from the operational rules for
\odot
.

•

If
\psi=\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}
, we show that, for every
a\in\mathsf{Act}
, there exists
m\in\mathsf{LMon}
such that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime})=\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{a}m
. Since formulas are guarded,
\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\}\prec\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}
; therefore, the induction hypothesis yields that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\})\xrightarrow{a}m
for some
m
. By
Lemma

4.16
, we have that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\})
