, which concludes the proof, by using the operational rule for recursive monitors.

∎

{textAtEnd}

[allend, category=bc]

We first show the existence of a finite communication path to a monitor that cannot communicate, and then argue that this monitor is unique.
To this aim, we start with a few lemmas that are all stating basic facts regarding communication, and are used in many of the lemmas that follow.
We start with three basic facts regarding receiving and sending of messages for action-derived local monitors: the first item states that local monitors can never receive the same message from the same sender in consecutive communications; the second one states that, if a monitor cannot receive a certain message from some sender, then it cannot receive that message in the future after taking only communication transitions; the last one states that monitors can only send the action they read last.

Lemma 4.33.

Let
m\in\mathsf{LMon}
be
A
-derived for
\ell
. Then:

(1)

If
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n
, then
n\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
.

(2)

If
m\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
and
m\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n
, then
n\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
.

(3)

If
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n
, then
\gamma=A(\ell)
.

Proof.

We prove the first two items for relevant local monitors, which is sufficient because
m
is a relevant local monitor via
Lemma

4.21
. We proceed by induction on the structure of
m
.

In the base case for
m={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
, by
Lemma

4.17

m
cannot communicate; hence, the premise of the first item is not satisfied and in the second item we get
n=m
, making the statement trivially true.
For the base case with
m=(!G,a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
, the premise of the first item is not satisfied; for the second item,
n=m
or
n={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
, and the result follows from the premise of the lemma or
Lemma

4.17
.
If
m=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})
, both items follow immediately from
Lemma

4.17
.

For the inductive step, let
m=m_{1}\odot m_{2}
, with
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
relevant monitors. For the first statement, we derive two cases from
m_{1}\odot m_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n
.

(1)

n=n_{1}\odot n_{2}
such that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n_{1}
and
m_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n_{2}
. The induction hypothesis gives that
n_{1}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
and
n_{2}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
. Hence,
n\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
.

(2)

n=n_{1}\odot m_{2}
such that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n_{1}
and
m_{2}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
. From the induction hypothesis, we get that
n_{1}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
. Hence,
n=n_{1}\odot m_{2}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
.

For the second statement we derive from
m_{1}\odot m_{2}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
that
m_{1}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
and
m_{2}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
. Furthermore, we know that
n=n_{1}\odot n_{2}
such that
m_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n_{1}
and
m_{2}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n_{2}
. From the induction hypothesis, we obtain that
n_{1}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
and
n_{2}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
. Hence,
n\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
.

Let us now prove the third item.
Because
m
is
A
-derived for
\ell
, we know that there exist
\sigma
,
\psi
and
m^{\prime}
such that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m
; we proceed by induction on
\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}
.
In the base case
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=a.m^{\prime}
; from the synthesis function and our assumption that
|\mathsf{Act}|\geq 2
, we observe that this never occurs.

For the inductive case, if
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m_{1}+m_{2}
and
m_{1}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m^{\prime}
,
\psi
must be of the form
[a_{\pi}]\psi^{\prime}

or
\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi^{\prime}
;
so

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=a.(!\{\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\mid\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\neq\ell\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})+\sum_{b\neq a}b.(!\{\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\mid\sigma(\pi^{\prime})\neq\ell\},b).v

or

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\sum_{b\in\mathsf{Act}}b.\big((?\{\sigma(\pi)\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})+\sum_{b\neq a}(?\{\sigma(\pi)\},b).v\big)

where
v=\mathsf{yes}/\mathsf{no}
according to the modality (resp., box/diamond).
It is immediate that
\gamma=A(\ell)
in
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n
.

Next we consider the case for
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m_{1}^{\prime\prime}\odot m_{2}^{\prime\prime}
and
m^{\prime}=m_{1}^{\prime}\odot m_{2}^{\prime}
. Hence,
\psi=\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2}
(for
\diamond=\wedge
if
\odot=\otimes
and
\diamond=\vee
if
\odot=\oplus
),
m_{1}^{\prime\prime}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{1}^{\prime}
and
m_{2}^{\prime\prime}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{2}^{\prime}
. Let
m=m_{1}\odot m_{2}
, where
m_{1}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m_{1}
and
m_{2}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m_{2}
.
We assume without loss of generality that
n=n_{1}\odot m_{2}
and
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n_{1}
. From the induction hypothesis, it follows that
\gamma=A(\ell)
.

Last we consider
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.m^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m^{\prime}
because
m^{\prime\prime}\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.m^{\prime\prime}}/_{x}\}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m^{\prime}
. Thus,
\psi=\max x.\psi^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
. By
Lemma

4.16
,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\})
and we use the induction hypothesis to obtain the desired result.
∎

We generalize the third property from
Lemma

4.33
to communicating monitors: if an action-derived monitor
M
can take a communicating transition, the message it sends is always the last action taken by the location of the sender:

Lemma 4.34.

Let
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
be
A
-derived. If
M\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}N
, then
\gamma=A(\ell)
.

Proof.

The result follows by induction on
\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}
. For the base case, we consider
M=[m]_{\ell}
,
N=[n]_{\ell}
and
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n
; by
Lemma

4.33
, we derive that
\gamma=A(\ell)
. The inductive step follows immediately from the induction hypothesis.
∎

We now state a few other basic properties:

Lemma

4.47
uses a ‘commutativity’ property for communicating monitors (
Lemma

4.45
), and this is based on five similar results for local monitors (
Lemma

4.35
-
Lemma

4.39
), that are in addition used in a variety of other lemmas.

The following lemma shows that, if action-derived local monitors can both send and receive a message, the order in which this happens does not matter.

Lemma 4.35.

Let
m
be a relevant local monitor such that

m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m_{1}
and
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m_{2}
, for some
G\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
and
\ell^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
. Then,
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m_{3}
and
m_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m_{3}
, for some
m_{3}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
m
.
In the base case for
m={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
, we conclude by
Lemma

4.17
; in the base cases for

m=(!G,a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
and
m=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})
, the premise of the lemma is not satisfied.

For the inductive step, let
m=n_{1}\odot n_{2}
, with
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
relevant monitors and
\odot\in\{\otimes,\oplus\}
.
From
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m_{1}
we derive w.l.o.g. that
m_{1}=n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n_{2}
and
n_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n^{\prime}_{1}
. From
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m_{2}
we derive three cases.

(1)

m_{2}=m^{\prime}_{2}\odot m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
such that
n_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m^{\prime}_{2}
and
n_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
.
From the induction hypothesis, we derive that
n^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m^{\prime}_{3}
and
m^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}_{3}
, for some
m^{\prime}_{3}
. Thus,
m_{1}=n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m^{\prime}_{3}\odot m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
and
m_{2}=m^{\prime}_{2}\odot m^{\prime\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}_{3}\odot m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
, as desired.

(2)

m_{2}=m^{\prime}_{2}\odot n_{2}
such that
n_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m^{\prime}_{2}
and
n_{2}\hskip 16.72357pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-16.72357pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}
. From the induction hypothesis, we derive
n^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m^{\prime}_{3}
and
m^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}_{3}
, for some
m^{\prime}_{3}
. Thus,
m_{1}=n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m^{\prime}_{3}\odot n_{2}
and
m_{2}=m^{\prime}_{2}\odot n_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}_{3}\odot n_{2}
.

(3)

m_{2}=n_{1}\odot m^{\prime}_{2}
such that
n_{1}\hskip 16.72357pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-16.72357pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}
and
n_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m^{\prime}_{2}
. We obtain that
m_{2}=n_{1}\odot m^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n^{\prime}_{1}\odot m^{\prime}_{2}
and
m_{1}=n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}n^{\prime}_{1}\odot m^{\prime}_{2}
, where we use
Lemma

4.33
to conclude that
n^{\prime}_{1}\hskip 16.72357pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-16.72357pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}
from
n_{1}\hskip 16.72357pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-16.72357pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}
.
∎

The next lemma proves uniqueness of reached states after a receiving transition.

Lemma 4.36.

Let
m
be a relevant local monitor such that

m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n_{1}
and
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n_{2}
. Then,
n_{1}=n_{2}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
m
. In the base case for
m={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
, we conclude by
Lemma

4.17
. For
m=(!G,a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
, the premise of the lemma is not satisfied; for
m=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})
, we can conclude because there is only one entry in the sum for each combination of
{\ell^{\prime\prime}}
and
a
.

For the inductive step, let
m=m_{1}\odot m_{2}
, with
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
relevant monitors. From
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n_{1}
we derive two cases.

(1)

n_{1}=n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n^{\prime\prime}_{1}
such that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n^{\prime}_{1}
and
m_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n^{\prime\prime}_{1}
. From
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n_{2}
we derive two more cases.

(a)

n_{2}=n^{\prime}_{2}\otimes n^{\prime\prime}_{2}
such that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n^{\prime}_{2}
and
m_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n^{\prime\prime}_{2}
. From the induction hypothesis, we conclude that
n^{\prime}_{1}=n^{\prime}_{2}
and
n^{\prime\prime}_{1}=n^{\prime\prime}_{2}
, and thus that
n_{1}=n_{2}
.

(b)

n_{2}=n^{\prime}_{2}\odot m_{2}
such that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n^{\prime}_{2}
and
m_{2}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
. This is a contradiction with
m_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n^{\prime\prime}_{1}
.

(2)

n_{1}=n^{\prime}_{1}\odot m_{2}
such that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n^{\prime}_{1}
and
m_{2}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
. We then conclude from
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n_{2}
that
n_{2}=n^{\prime}_{2}\odot m_{2}
such that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n^{\prime}_{2}
. From the induction hypothesis, we conclude that
n^{\prime}_{1}=n^{\prime}_{2}
and thus that
n_{1}=n_{2}
.
∎

The next lemma states that, if a monitor can receive a message from two different senders, the order in which this happens does not matter.

Lemma 4.37.

Let
m
be a relevant local monitor such that

m\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{1},\gamma_{1})}m_{1}

\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}

m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{2},\gamma_{2})}m_{3}

m_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{1},\gamma_{1})}m_{3}

m_{3}

The following lemma shows that if action-derived local monitors can send two different messages, the order in which this happens does not matter.

Lemma 4.38.

Let
m
be a relevant local monitor such that

m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m_{1}
and
m\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m_{2}
, for some
G,G^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
and
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Con}
such that either
G\neq G^{\prime}
or
\gamma\neq\gamma^{\prime}
. Then,
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m_{3}
and
m_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m_{3}
, for some
m_{3}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
m
. The base case for
m={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
follows from
Lemma

4.17
; for
m=(!G,a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
and
m=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})
, the premise of the lemma is not satisfied.

For the inductive step, let
m=n_{1}\odot n_{2}
, with
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
relevant monitors. From
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m_{1}
we derive w.l.o.g. that
m_{1}=n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n_{2}
and
n_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n^{\prime}_{1}
. From
m\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}m_{2}
we derive two cases.

(1)

m_{2}=n^{\prime\prime}_{1}\odot n_{2}
such that
n_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}n^{\prime\prime}_{1}
.
From the induction hypothesis, we derive that
n^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}n^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n^{\prime}
, for some
n^{\prime}
. Thus,
n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}n^{\prime}\odot n_{2}
and
n^{\prime\prime}_{1}\odot n_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n^{\prime}\odot n_{2}
, as desired.

(2)

m_{2}=n_{1}\odot n^{\prime\prime}_{2}
such that
n_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}n^{\prime\prime}_{2}
. Then,
n_{1}\odot n^{\prime\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n^{\prime\prime}_{2}
and
n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})}n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n^{\prime\prime}_{2}
.
∎

The following lemma is the version of
Lemma

4.36
for sending.

Lemma 4.39.

Let
m
be a relevant local monitor such that

m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n_{1}
and
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n_{2}
. Then, either
n_{1}=n_{2}
, or
n_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}
and
n_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}
, for some
m^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
m
. If
m=(!G,a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
, it is immediate that
n_{1}=n_{2}
. If
m={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
or
m=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})
, the premise of the lemma is not satisfied.

Let
m=m_{1}\odot m_{2}
, with
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
relevant monitors. From
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n_{1}
, we derive that
n_{1}=m^{\prime}_{1}\odot m^{\prime}_{2}
such that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}_{1}
.
From
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n_{2}
we derive two cases.

(1)

n_{2}=m^{\prime\prime}_{1}\odot m_{2}
such that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime\prime}_{1}
. From the induction hypothesis, we conclude that
m^{\prime}_{1}=m^{\prime\prime}_{1}
(and thus that
n_{1}=n_{2}
), or
m^{\prime\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}r
and
m^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}r
, for some
r
. In the latter case, we derive that
n_{1}=m^{\prime}_{1}\odot m_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}r\odot m_{2}
and
n_{2}=m^{\prime\prime}_{1}\odot m_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}r\odot m_{2}
.

(2)

n_{2}=m_{1}\odot m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
such that
m_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
. In this case, we derive that
n_{1}=m^{\prime}_{1}\odot m_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}_{1}\odot m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
and
n_{2}=m_{1}\odot m^{\prime\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m^{\prime}_{1}\odot m^{\prime\prime}_{2}
.
∎

Before we continue on the path to proving
Lemma

4.47
, we show how to formally capture the state of a decentralized monitor after taking a receiving transition, for which we only need
Lemma

4.36
. To this aim, we use
M[[n]_{\ell}/[m]_{\ell}]
as notation for replacing
[m]_{\ell}
in
M
with
[n]_{\ell}
(the operation does not change
M
in case
[m]_{\ell}
does not occur in
M
).

Lemma 4.40.

Let
M
and
N
be action-derived monitors such that
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N
; let
\{[m_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}
be the set of all the
[m]_{\ell}\in M
.
For each
1\leq i\leq k
, let
n_{i}
be
m_{i}
, when
\ell_{i}\notin G
or
m_{i}\hskip 14.01869pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-14.01869pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}
, and be such that
m_{i}\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n_{i}
, otherwise.
Then,
M[[n_{1}]_{\ell_{1}}/[m_{1}]_{\ell_{1}}]\cdots[[n_{k}]_{\ell_{k}}/[m_{k}]_{\ell_{k}}]=N
.

Proof.

Lemma

4.36
yields that each
n_{i}
is uniquely defined.
Then, the lemma follows by an easy induction on
\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}
.
∎

Lemma

4.41
-
Lemma

4.44
lift the previous results from local to decentralized monitors and are used to prove
Lemma

4.45
.
This result, together with
Lemma

4.46
, leads to
Lemma

4.47
, which captures that the order of communication does not matter: regardless of the order in which the communication steps are executed, the ‘final’ state (i.e., the state where no more communication can take place) is unique.

Lemma 4.41.

Let
M
be action-derived. If
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N
, then
N\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N
. In the first base case we consider
M=[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}
,
N=[n]_{\ell^{\prime}}
,
\ell^{\prime}\in G
, and
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n
: by
Lemma

4.33
, which we can apply because
M
is action-derived, we obtain that
n\hskip 14.01869pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-14.01869pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}
, and so
[n]_{\ell^{\prime}}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[n]_{\ell^{\prime}}
.
In the second and third base case we consider
M=[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}=N
, and thus we are done immediately.
In the inductive case we consider
M=M_{1}\diamond M_{2}
,
N=N_{1}\diamond N_{2}
,
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}
and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
. From the induction hypothesis we get that
N_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}
and
N_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
, and thus that
N=N_{1}\diamond N_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}\diamond N_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 4.42.

Let
M
be
A
-derived and such that
M\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}
and
M\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
. Then, either
N_{1}=N_{2}
, or
N_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2}\,A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{3}
and
N_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{3}
, for some
N_{3}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
M\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}
. In the first base case we consider
M=[m]_{\ell}
,
N_{1}=[n_{1}]_{\ell}
,
\ell\in G_{1}
, and
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))}n_{1}
. We distinguish two cases:

(1)

N_{2}=[n_{2}]_{\ell}
,
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))}n_{2}
,
\ell\in G_{2}
. Since
M
is action-derived, we can use
Lemma

4.37
to obtain two further cases:

(a)

\ell_{1}\neq\ell_{2}
and
n_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))}q
and
n_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))}q
. Hence,
N_{1}=[n_{1}]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[q]_{\ell}
and
N_{2}=[n_{2}]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[q]_{\ell}
.

(b)

\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
. Via
Lemma

4.36
we conclude that
n_{1}=n_{2}
and thus that
N_{1}=N_{2}
.

(2)

N_{2}=[m]_{\ell}
and either
\ell\notin G_{2}
or
m\hskip 23.62686pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-23.62686pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))}
. In the latter case, we know by
Lemma

4.33
(2) that
n_{1}\hskip 23.62686pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-23.62686pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))}
. Hence, in both cases,
N_{1}=[n_{1}]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[n_{1}]_{\ell}=N_{1}
. Since
N_{2}=M
, we know that
N_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}
, as desired.

For the next two base cases, we consider
N_{1}=[m]_{\ell}
and either
\ell\notin G_{1}
or
m\hskip 23.62686pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-23.62686pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))}
. We distinguish two cases:

(1)

N_{2}=[n_{2}]_{\ell}
,
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))}n_{2}
,
\ell\in G_{2}
. If
m\hskip 23.62686pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-23.62686pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))}
,
Lemma

4.33
(2) entails that
n_{2}\hskip 23.62686pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-23.62686pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))}
. Hence, in all cases,
N_{2}=[n_{2}]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[n_{2}]_{\ell}=N_{2}
. Since
N_{1}=M
, we know that
N_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
, as desired.

(2)

N_{2}=[m]_{\ell}
and either
\ell\notin G_{2}
or
m\hskip 23.62686pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-23.62686pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))}
. Hence,
M=N_{2}=N_{1}
, and we are done immediately.

For the inductive step, we let
M=M_{1}\diamond M_{2}
,
N_{1}=O_{1}\diamond O_{2}
,
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{1}
and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{2}
. This implies that
N_{2}=O_{3}\diamond O_{4}
, with
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{3}
and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{4}
. We apply the induction hypothesis to
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{1}
to obtain two cases:

(1)

O_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q_{1}
and
O_{3}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q_{1}
. We apply the induction hypothesis to
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{2}
to obtain two more cases:

(a)

O_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q_{2}
and
O_{4}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q_{2}
.
Hence,
O_{1}\diamond O_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q_{1}\diamond Q_{2}
and
O_{3}\diamond O_{4}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q_{1}\diamond Q_{2}
.

(b)

O_{2}=O_{4}
. We apply
Lemma

4.41
to
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{4}
to conclude that
O_{4}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{4}
.
Being
O_{2}=O_{4}
, we obtain that
O_{1}\diamond O_{2}=O_{1}\diamond O_{4}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q_{1}\diamond O_{4}
and
O_{3}\diamond O_{4}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q_{1}\diamond O_{4}
.

(2)

O_{1}=O_{3}
. We apply the induction hypothesis to
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{2}
to obtain two more cases:

(a)

O_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q_{2}
and
O_{4}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q_{2}
.
We apply
Lemma

4.41
to
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{3}
to conclude that
O_{3}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{3}
.
Being
O_{1}=O_{3}
, we have that
O_{1}\diamond O_{2}=O_{3}\diamond O_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{3}\diamond Q_{2}
and
O_{3}\diamond O_{4}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},A(\ell_{1}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{3}\diamond Q_{2}
.

(b)

O_{2}=O_{4}
. Then we immediately have
N_{1}=N_{2}
.∎

Lemma 4.43.

Let
M
be action-derived and such that
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}
and
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
. Then,
N_{1}=N_{2}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}
. In the first base case we consider
M=[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}
,
N_{1}=[n_{1}]_{\ell^{\prime}}
,
\ell^{\prime}\in G
, and
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n_{1}
. We distinguish two cases:

(1)

N_{2}=[n_{2}]_{\ell^{\prime}}
,
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n_{2}
. We apply
Lemma

4.36
(
M
is action-derived) to conclude that
n_{2}=n_{1}
, and thus
N_{1}=N_{2}
.

(2)

N_{2}=[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}
and either
m\hskip 14.01869pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-14.01869pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}
or
\ell^{\prime}\notin G
. This would contradict
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n_{1}
and
\ell^{\prime}\in G
, thus we can exclude this case.

In the second and third base case we consider
M=[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}=N_{1}
, and either
\ell^{\prime}\notin G
or
m\hskip 14.01869pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-14.01869pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}
. We distinguish two cases:

(1)

N_{2}=[n_{2}]_{\ell^{\prime}}
,
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n_{2}
,
\ell^{\prime}\in G
. This would contradict
m\hskip 14.01869pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-14.01869pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}
or
\ell^{\prime}\notin G
. Hence we can exclude this case.

(2)

N_{2}=[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}
and either
\ell^{\prime}\notin G
or
m\hskip 14.01869pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-14.01869pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}
. Hence,
M=N_{2}=N_{1}
, and we are done immediately.

For the inductive case, let
M=M_{1}\diamond M_{2}
,
N_{1}=O_{1}\diamond O_{2}
,
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{1}
and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{2}
. This implies that
N_{2}=O_{3}\diamond O_{4}
, with
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{3}
and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{4}
. We apply the induction hypothesis twice to obtain that
O_{1}=O_{3}
and
O_{2}=O_{4}
, and thus
N_{1}=N_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 4.44.

Let
M
be action-derived and such that
M\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}N_{1}
and

M\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
. Then,
N_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{3}
and
N_{2}\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}N_{3}
, for some
N_{3}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
M\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}N_{1}
. In the base case we consider
M=[m]_{\ell_{1}}
,
N_{1}=[n_{1}]_{\ell_{1}}
and
m\xrightarrow{(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}n_{1}
. We distinguish two cases based on
M\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
:

(1)

N_{2}=[n_{2}]_{\ell_{1}}
,
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))}n_{2}
, and
\ell_{1}\in G_{2}
. Hence, we can apply
Lemma

4.35
to obtain that there exists
m_{1}
such that
n_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))}m_{1}
and
n_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}m_{1}
. Hence,
N_{2}\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}[m_{1}]_{\ell_{1}}
and
N_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[m_{1}]_{\ell_{1}}
.

(2)

N_{2}=[m]_{\ell_{1}}
and either
\ell_{1}\notin G_{2}
or
m\hskip 23.62686pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-23.62686pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))}
.
The latter case implies that
n_{1}\hskip 23.62686pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-23.62686pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))}
, by applying
Lemma

4.33
to
m\xrightarrow{(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}n_{1}
. As
M=N_{2}
, in both cases we have
N_{2}\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}N_{1}
and
N_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}
, as desired.

For the inductive case, we consider
M=M_{1}\diamond M_{2}
,
N_{1}=O_{1}\diamond O_{2}
,

M_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}O_{1}
and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},\gamma_{1})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{2}
. We deduce that
N_{2}=O_{3}\diamond O_{4}
, with
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{3}
and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{4}
. We use the induction hypothesis to conclude that there exists
Q
such that
O_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q
and
O_{3}\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}Q
. Since
M\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}N_{1}
, we know that
\gamma_{1}=A(\ell_{1})
(
Lemma

4.34
). Hence, we can apply
Lemma

4.42
on
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},\gamma_{1})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{2}
and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{4}
and have two cases:

(1)

O_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q^{\prime}
and
O_{4}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},\gamma_{1})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q^{\prime}
. Hence,
N_{1}=O_{1}\diamond O_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q\diamond Q^{\prime}
and
N_{2}=O_{3}\diamond O_{4}\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}Q\diamond Q^{\prime}
.

(2)

O_{2}=O_{4}
. We apply
Lemma

4.41
to
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},\gamma_{1})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{2}

to conclude that
O_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},\gamma_{1})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{2}
.
Being
O_{2}=O_{4}
, we have

N_{1}=O_{1}\diamond O_{2}=O_{1}\diamond O_{4}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},A(\ell_{2}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q\diamond O_{4}
and
N_{2}=O_{3}\diamond O_{4}\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}:(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}Q\diamond O_{4}
.
∎

Lemma 4.45.

Let
M
be action-derived and such that
M\xrightarrow{c_{1}}M_{1}
and
M\xrightarrow{c_{2}}M_{2}
. Then, either

c_{1}=c_{2}
and
M_{1}=M_{2}
, or
M_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}N
and
M_{2}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}N
, for some
N
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
M\xrightarrow{c_{1}}M_{1}
. Let
c_{1}=\ell_{1}:(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})
and
c_{2}=\ell_{2}:(!G_{2},\gamma_{2})
.
In the base case we consider
M=[m]_{\ell_{1}}
,
M_{1}=[m^{\prime}]_{\ell_{1}}
and
m\xrightarrow{(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}m^{\prime}
. Thus we know that
M_{2}=[m^{\prime\prime}]_{\ell_{1}}
,
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
and
m\xrightarrow{(!G_{2},\gamma_{2})}m^{\prime\prime}
. We distinguish two cases:

(1)

G_{1}\neq G_{2}
or
\gamma_{1}\neq\gamma_{2}
. Then we apply
Lemma

4.38
to obtain that
m^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(!G_{2},\gamma_{2})}n
and
m^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}n
. Hence,
M_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}[n]_{\ell_{1}}
and
M_{2}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}[n]_{\ell_{1}}
.

(2)

G_{1}=G_{2}
and
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
. We apply
Lemma

4.39
to obtain that
m^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(!G_{2},\gamma_{2})}n
and
m^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{(!G_{1},\gamma_{1})}n
, or that
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime\prime}
. In the former case, we conclude like in the first item; in the latter case, we get immediately that
M_{1}=M_{2}
and
c_{1}=c_{2}
.

For the inductive case, let
M=N_{1}\diamond N_{2}
,
M_{1}=N_{1}^{\prime}\diamond N_{2}^{\prime}
,

N_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}N_{1}^{\prime}
and
N_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},\gamma_{1})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}^{\prime}
. We distinguish two cases.

(1)

M_{2}=O_{1}\diamond O_{2}
, with

N_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}O_{1}
and
N_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},\gamma_{2})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{2}
. We obtain two further cases from the induction hypothesis applied to
N_{1}
:

(a)

N_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}Q
and
O_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}Q
. Since
M\xrightarrow{c_{1}}M_{1}
and
M\xrightarrow{c_{2}}M_{2}
, we obtain via
Lemma

4.34
that
\gamma_{1}=A(\ell_{1})
and
\gamma_{2}=A(\ell_{2})
. Thus we can apply
Lemma

4.42
to
N_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},\gamma_{1})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}^{\prime}
and
N_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},\gamma_{2})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{2}
to obtain two further cases:

(i)

N_{2}^{\prime}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},\gamma_{2})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q^{\prime}
and
O_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},\gamma_{1})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q^{\prime}
. Hence
N_{1}^{\prime}\diamond N_{2}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}Q\diamond Q^{\prime}
and
O_{1}\diamond O_{2}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}Q\diamond Q^{\prime}
, as desired.

(ii)

N_{2}^{\prime}=O_{2}
. By
Lemma

4.41
,
O_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},\gamma_{2})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{2}
;
hence,
N_{1}^{\prime}\diamond N_{2}^{\prime}=N_{1}^{\prime}\diamond O_{2}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}Q\diamond O_{2}
and
O_{1}\diamond O_{2}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}Q\diamond O_{2}
.

(b)

N_{1}^{\prime}=O_{1}
and
c_{1}=c_{2}
. Since
c_{1}=c_{2}
, we obtain from
Lemma

4.43
that
N_{2}^{\prime}=O_{2}
. Hence,
M_{1}=M_{2}
and we can conclude.

(2)

M_{2}=O_{1}\diamond O_{2}
, with

N_{1}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},\gamma_{2})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}O_{1}
and
N_{2}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}O_{2}
. By
Lemma

4.44
(applied twice, to
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
, which we can do because, like before,
\gamma_{1}=A(\ell_{1})
and
\gamma_{2}=A(\ell_{2})
), we obtain
N_{1}^{\prime}\overset{\textup{$G_{2}:(?\ell_{2},\gamma_{2})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q
and
O_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}Q
, and
N_{2}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}Q^{\prime}
and
O_{2}\overset{\textup{$G_{1}:(?\ell_{1},\gamma_{1})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}Q^{\prime}
. Hence,
N_{1}^{\prime}\diamond N_{2}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}Q\diamond Q^{\prime}
and
O_{1}\diamond O_{2}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}Q\diamond Q^{\prime}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.46.

If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
for all
c
and
M^{\prime}\xrightarrow{c}M^{\prime\prime}
for some
c
, then
M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the length of
M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N
. The base case is for
M^{\prime}=N
: as
N
cannot send messages, we then also have
M^{\prime}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, and the desired result trivially holds.
In the inductive step, we consider
M^{\prime}\xrightarrow{c^{\prime}}M_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N
. Let
c=\ell:(!G,\gamma)
and
c^{\prime}=\ell^{\prime}:(!G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
;
by
Lemma

4.45
, either there exists
N^{\prime}
such that
M_{1}\xrightarrow{c}N^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{c^{\prime}}N^{\prime}
, or
M^{\prime\prime}=M_{1}
. In the latter case we can conclude immediately, because
M_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N
. In the former case we use the induction hypothesis on
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N
and
M_{1}\xrightarrow{c}N^{\prime}
to obtain that
N^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N
from which we can conclude because
M^{\prime}\xrightarrow{c}M^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{c^{\prime}}N^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 4.47.

If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
for all
c
and
M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime\prime}
, then
M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the length of
M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime\prime}
. If the length is
0
, then
M^{\prime}=M^{\prime\prime}
and we are done immediately. Otherwise we consider
M^{\prime}\xrightarrow{c}M_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime\prime}
with
c=\ell:(!G,\gamma)
;
by
Lemma

4.46
,
M_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N
. From the induction hypothesis on
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N
and
M_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime\prime}
,
we obtain that
M^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N
and conclude.
∎

The next three lemmas are used to prove
Lemma

4.71
,
Lemma

4.59
,
Lemma

4.60
and
Lemma

4.75
. They describe ‘correct’ communication paths of local monitors, where ‘correct’ means that the local monitors only take receiving transitions that correspond to messages containing the last action of the sender.
Lemma

4.49
shows that in correct communication paths, the order of communication does not matter for the final destination (when no sending and receiving can take place).
Lemma

4.50
is a direct generalization of
Lemma

4.49
, and is the equivalent of
Lemma

4.47
on the level of local monitors.
Lemma

4.51
is used to show that those correct communication paths can be combined when multiple local monitors are put in parallel, which is used in
Lemma

4.71
and
Lemma

4.75
.

We first define formally what is such a correct communication path.

Definition 4.48.

Let
m\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m^{\prime}
denote the existence of an integer
h>0
, of

h
monitors
m_{1},\ldots,m_{h}
and of
h-1
communication actions
c_{1},\ldots,c_{h-1}

such that
m_{1}=m
,
m_{h}=m^{\prime}
,

m_{i}\xrightarrow{c_{i}}m_{i+1}
for every
i=1,\ldots,h-1
and, if
c_{i}=(?\ell,\gamma)
,
then

\gamma=A(\ell)
.

Hence,
\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces
is the same as
\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces
but with the extra constraint on actions of kind
(?\ell,\gamma)
.

Lemma 4.49.

Let
m
be a relevant local monitor such that
m\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m^{\prime}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, for all
c
, and either
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n
, for some
\ell^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
, or
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n
, for some
G\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
. Then
n\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
m\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m^{\prime}
in
h
steps;
we proceed by induction on
h
.
In the base case,
m=m^{\prime}
and, as
m^{\prime}
cannot communicate, the premise of the lemma is not satisfied.
Now suppose that
m\xrightarrow{c_{1}}m_{1}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m^{\prime}
, with
c_{1}=(!G,\gamma)
or
c_{1}=(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))
for some
\ell^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
.
First, let us consider the case in which
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n
, for some
\ell^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
;
depending on the form of
c_{1}
, we have two subcases:

(1)

c_{1}=(!G,\gamma)
. We use
Lemma

4.35
to obtain that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n^{\prime}
and
n\xrightarrow{c_{1}}n^{\prime}
. Hence,
m_{1}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m^{\prime}
in
h-1
steps, and
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n^{\prime}
. We apply the induction hypothesis to obtain that
n^{\prime}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m^{\prime}
, from which we can conclude because
n\xrightarrow{c_{1}}n^{\prime}
.

(2)

c_{1}=(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))
for some
\ell^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
. Via
Lemma

4.37
, we get that either
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n^{\prime}
and
n\xrightarrow{c_{1}}n^{\prime}
(in which case we proceed as before), or
\ell^{\prime}=\ell^{\prime\prime}
. In the latter case, we use
Lemma

4.36
to derive that
m_{1}=n
and conclude, because
m_{1}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m^{\prime}
.

Then, we consider the case in which
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n
, for some
G\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
, and we again distinguish two subcases:

(1)

c_{1}=(!G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
.
If
G\neq G^{\prime}
or
\gamma\neq\gamma^{\prime}
, then
we use
Lemma

4.38
to obtain that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n^{\prime}
and
n\xrightarrow{c_{1}}n^{\prime}
and proceed like in the first subcase above.
If
G=G^{\prime}
and
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
, we obtain from
Lemma

4.39
that
m_{1}=n
and proceed like the second subcase above.

(2)

c_{1}=(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))
for some
\ell^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
. Via
Lemma

4.35
, we get that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n^{\prime}
and
n\xrightarrow{c_{1}}n^{\prime}
; we then proceed as in the first subcase above.
∎

We can extend the previous lemma to a more general statement:

Lemma 4.50.

Let
m
be a relevant local monitor such that
m\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m^{\prime}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, for all
c
, and
m\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n
. Then
n\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
m\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n
in
h
steps;
we prove the lemma by induction on
h
.
In the base case,
n=m
and we are done immediately. For the inductive step, we consider
m\xrightarrow{c_{1}}n^{\prime}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n
, for
c_{1}=(!G,\gamma)
or
c_{1}=(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))
, where
n^{\prime}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n
in
h-1
steps. By
Lemma

4.49
, we get
n^{\prime}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m^{\prime}
; this, together with
n^{\prime}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n
, allows us to apply the induction hypothesis and conclude.
∎

Lemma 4.51.

Let
m_{1}^{1}
and
m_{1}^{2}
be relevant local monitors such that

m_{1}^{1}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{1}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
and
m_{1}^{2}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{2}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, for all
c
.
Then,
m_{1}^{1}\odot m_{1}^{2}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{1}\odot n_{2}
, for
\odot\in\{\otimes,\oplus\}
.

Proof.

Let
m_{1}^{1}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{1}
in
h
steps; we proceed by induction on
h
. In the base case,
m_{1}^{1}=n_{1}
; since
n_{1}
cannot communicate, we get that
m_{1}^{1}\odot m_{1}^{2}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m_{1}^{1}\odot n_{2}=n_{1}\odot n_{2}
.

For the inductive step, we consider
m_{1}^{1}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}n^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{1}
, with
c_{1}=(!G,\gamma)
or
c_{1}=(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))
.We distinguish three cases.

(1)

If
c_{1}=(!G,\gamma)
, then
m_{1}^{1}\odot m_{1}^{2}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}n^{\prime}_{1}\odot m_{1}^{2}
. From the induction hypothesis, we get that
n^{\prime}_{1}\odot m_{1}^{2}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{1}\odot n_{2}
, as desired.

(2)

c_{1}=(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))
and
m_{1}^{2}\hskip 22.42891pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-22.42891pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}
. In this case
m_{1}^{1}\odot m_{1}^{2}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}n^{\prime}_{1}\odot m_{1}^{2}
, and we continue as in the previous case.

(3)

c_{1}=(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))
and
m_{1}^{2}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}n^{\prime}_{2}
. Thus,
m_{1}^{2}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{2}
and
m_{1}^{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n^{\prime}_{2}
; by
Lemma

4.49
, we conclude that
n^{\prime}_{2}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{2}
. We can then apply the induction hypothesis to conclude that
n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n^{\prime}_{2}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{1}\odot n_{2}
. Together with
m_{1}^{1}\odot m_{1}^{2}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n^{\prime}_{2}
, this concludes the proof.
∎

The following lemma shows that, for every receiving transition in a monitor
m
that is reached from a monitor synthesized from some
\psi\in\mathsf{Qf}
, there exists a corresponding sending transition in another local monitor reached from a synthesized local monitor for
\psi
located at a different location.

Lemma 4.52.

Let
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m_{1}
and
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}m^{\prime}
. Then
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
,
\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell
, and there are
n,n^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}
such that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell^{\prime})}n\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n^{\prime}
and
\ell\in G^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}
. If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=v
, the premise of the lemma is not satisfied; moreover, we do not consider the case where
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=a.m
, as this cannot occur in the synthesis function (because of our assumption that
|\mathsf{Act}|\geq 2
).

For the first inductive case, we consider
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=n_{1}+n_{2}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m
, because
n_{1}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m
or
n_{2}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m
. Hence,
\psi=[a_{\pi}]\psi^{\prime}

or
\psi=\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi^{\prime}
.
As
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}m^{\prime}
, we know that
\sigma(\pi)=\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell
. Thus, from the synthesis function, we immediately have that
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell^{\prime})}n
such that
n\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n^{\prime}
with
\ell\in G^{\prime}
(being
\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
).

Next we consider
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=n_{1}\odot n_{2}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}n^{\prime}_{1}\odot n^{\prime}_{2}(=m)\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n^{\prime\prime}_{1}\odot n^{\prime\prime}_{2}(=m_{1})
, with
n_{1}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}n^{\prime}_{1}
,
n_{2}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}n^{\prime}_{2}
,
n^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n^{\prime\prime}_{1}
, and
n^{\prime}_{2}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n^{\prime\prime}_{2}
. From the synthesis function, we know that
\psi=\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2}
(with
\diamond=\wedge
if
\odot=\otimes
and
\diamond=\vee
if
\odot=\oplus
),
n_{1}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})
and
n_{2}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})
.
The transition
m_{1}=n^{\prime\prime}_{1}\odot n^{\prime\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}m^{\prime}
can happen in two ways:

(1)

m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{1}\odot m^{\prime}_{2}
, for
n^{\prime\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}m^{\prime}_{1}
and
n^{\prime\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}m^{\prime}_{2}
.
From the induction hypothesis, we get that
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\xrightarrow{A(\ell^{\prime})}o_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G_{1}^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}o_{1}^{\prime}
and
\ell\in G_{1}^{\prime}
; similarly,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})\xrightarrow{A(\ell^{\prime})}o_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G_{2}^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}o_{2}^{\prime}
and
\ell\in G_{2}^{\prime}
. Hence,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\odot{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})\xrightarrow{A(\ell^{\prime})}o_{1}\odot o_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G_{1}^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}o_{1}^{\prime}\odot o_{2}
with
\ell\in G_{1}^{\prime}
, as desired.

(2)

m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{1}\odot n^{\prime\prime}_{2}
, for
n^{\prime\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}m^{\prime}_{1}
and
n^{\prime\prime}_{2}\hskip 15.37112pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.37112pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
(the symmetric case is identical).
From the induction hypothesis, we get that
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\xrightarrow{A(\ell^{\prime})}o\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}o^{\prime}
and
\ell\in G^{\prime}
. It is immediate from the synthesis function that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})\xrightarrow{A(\ell^{\prime})}o_{2}
, for some monitor
o_{2}
.
Thus,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\odot{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})\xrightarrow{A(\ell^{\prime})}o\odot o_{2}\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}o^{\prime}\odot o_{2}
with
\ell\in G^{\prime}
, as desired.

Last, we consider
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.o\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m
because
o\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.o}/_{x}\}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m
. Thus,
\psi=\max x.\psi^{\prime}
and
o={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
. By
Lemma

4.16
,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\})
; from induction hypothesis, we obtain that
\ell^{\prime}\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\})\xrightarrow{A(\ell^{\prime})}n\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n^{\prime}
and
\ell\in G^{\prime}
. Since
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi)\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi)}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}}/_{x}\})
, we get that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime})=\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell^{\prime})}n
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma

4.55
is used to prove
Lemma

4.56
and it states that a communicating monitor never makes a choice between a send and a receive, so
if
M_{1}
can send (with some transition labelled with
c
) and reach
M_{2}
, it cannot have reached
M_{2}
by receiving some message (intuitively because the send from
c
is still present if
M_{1}
takes a receiving transition, and it is not present in
M_{2}
). Instead, if a monitor can both send and receive, this must be because multiple monitors are connected in parallel, either locally with
\oplus/\otimes
or as communicating monitors via
\vee/\wedge
.

To this aim, we first define a function
cc
from relevant (local) monitors to natural numbers that counts the number of top-level send prefixes and prove that it decreases after every send action and remains the same after every receive action.

Definition 4.53.

For arbitrary
\sigma,\ell
and
\psi
, we define:

•

cc({}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi))=cc(\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a}))=0
;

•

cc((!G,a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi))=1
;

•

cc(m_{1}\odot m_{2})=cc(m_{1})+cc(m_{2})
, where
\odot
denotes either
\oplus
or
\otimes
;

•

cc([m]_{\ell})=cc(m)
; and

•

cc(M\diamond N)=cc(M)+cc(N)
, where
\diamond
denotes either
\vee
or
\wedge
.

Lemma 4.54.

Let
m_{1}
be a relevant local monitor.

(1)

If
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m_{2}
, then
cc(m_{1})>cc(m_{2})
.

(2)

If
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?G,\gamma)}m_{3}
, then
cc(m_{1})=cc(m_{3})
.

Proof.

For the first claim, we proceed by induction on
m_{1}
. In the base cases for
m_{1}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
and
m_{1}=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})
, we have that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m_{2}
does not hold (by using
Lemma

4.17
for
m_{1}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
);
for
m_{1}=(!G,\gamma).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
, we have that
m_{2}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
and, thus,
cc(m_{2})=0<1=cc((!G,\gamma).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi))
.
For the inductive case, let
m_{1}=m\odot m^{\prime}
, with
m
and
m^{\prime}
relevant monitors; the result follows immediately from the definition of
cc
and the inductive hypothesis.

For the second claim, we again proceed by induction on
m_{1}
.
The base cases for
m_{1}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
and
m_{1}=(!G,\gamma).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
entail that
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?G,\gamma)}m_{3}
does not hold;
for
m_{1}=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})
, then
m_{3}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})
, for some
\psi_{a}
, and therefore
cc(m_{1})=cc(m_{3})=0
.
The case for
m_{1}=m\odot m^{\prime}
is straightforward from the inductive hypothesis.
∎

Lemma 4.55.

Let
m_{1}
and
M_{1}
be action-derived monitors.

(1)

If
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}m_{2}
and

m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?G,\gamma)}m_{3}
, then
m_{2}\neq m_{3}
.

(2)

If
M_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}M_{2}
and

M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}M_{3}
, then
M_{2}\neq M_{3}
.

Proof.

Notice that
m_{1}
is a relevant local monitor by
Lemma

4.21
.
For the first claim, we use
Lemma

4.54
to obtain that
cc(m_{3})=cc(m_{1})<cc(m_{2})
and so
m_{2}\neq m_{3}
.
The second statement is proved similarly, by observing that

M_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}M_{2}
implies
cc(M_{1})>cc(M_{2})

and that

M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}M_{3}
implies
cc(M_{1})=cc(M_{3})

(both claims can be proved by a straightforward induction, by using
Lemma

4.54
).
∎

From the previous lemma we can then conclude that, if
M_{1}
reaches
M_{2}
by a sending transition, and we combine
M_{1}
with another monitor
N_{1}
that transitions to
M_{2}
composed with
N_{2}
, then
N_{1}
must have taken a receiving step. This is useful for
Lemma

4.59
and
Lemma

4.65
, which are in turn used to prove
Lemma

4.70
,
Lemma

4.71
and
Lemma

4.76
.

Lemma 4.56.

Let
M_{1}
be action-derived,
M_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}M_{2}
and

M_{1}\diamond N_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}M_{2}\diamond N_{2}
.
Then,
N_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
.

Proof.

From the semantics and
M_{1}\diamond N_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}M_{2}\diamond N_{2}
, we obtain that
M_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}M_{2}
and
N_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
, or
N_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}N_{2}
and
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}M_{2}
. In the first case we are done; in the second case,

M_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}M_{2}
and
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}M_{2}
would contradict
Lemma

4.55
, hence
N_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
.
∎

The next lemma states that action transitions are deterministic for synthesized monitors, and is used in the proof of
Lemma

4.59
.

Lemma 4.57.

If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}n
, then
m=n
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}
. The interesting cases are the inductive ones.
If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m_{1}+m_{2}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m
, then either
\psi=[a_{\pi}]\psi^{\prime}

or
\psi=\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi^{\prime}
, for some
a
,
\pi
and
\psi^{\prime}
. As there is only one possible transition for each
a\in\mathsf{Act}
, we have obtained the required result.

If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.m_{1}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m
because
m_{1}\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.m_{1}}/_{x}\}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m
, we know that
\psi=\textrm{max}~x.\psi^{\prime}
and
m_{1}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
.
By
Lemma

4.16
,
m_{1}\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.m_{1}}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime}\{^{\psi}/_{x}\})

(note that the free location-variables of
\psi
are not bound in
\psi^{\prime}
); thus, we can use the induction hypothesis to get
m=n
.

If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m_{1}\odot m_{2}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{3}\odot m_{4}=m
, we know from the synthesis definition that
\psi=\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2}
(for
\diamond=\vee
if
\odot=\oplus
and
\diamond=\wedge
if
\odot=\otimes
), for some
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
such that
m_{1}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})
and
m_{2}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})
. Hence,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{3}
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{4}
. Similarly, we know that
n=n_{1}\odot n_{2}
for some
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
such that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}n_{1}
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}n_{2}
. We use the induction hypothesis to conclude that
m_{3}=n_{1}
and
m_{4}=n_{2}
, from which we derive that
m=n
.
∎

In what follows we need to specify a condition on a group of local monitors who all take some ‘correct’ set of communication transitions (correct as in following the restrictions in
\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces
). This condition states that the reached states in the group of local monitors will no longer take receiving transitions that match a sending transition that occurred on one of the local communication paths.

Definition 4.58.

Let
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}\in{\mathcal{L}}
and
m_{1},\ldots,m_{k},n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}
be monitors.
We write

m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n_{i}

if, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, there exist an integer
h_{i}>0
,

h_{i}
monitors
m_{i}^{1},\ldots,m_{i}^{h_{i}}
, and
h_{i}-1
actions
c_{i}^{1},\ldots,c_{i}^{h_{i}-1}

such that
m_{i}=m_{i}^{1}
,
m_{i}^{h_{i}}=n_{i}
,

m_{i}^{j}\xrightarrow{c_{i}^{j}}m_{i}^{j+1}
for every
j=1,\ldots,{h_{i}}-1
and

(1)

if
c_{i}^{j}=(?\ell,\gamma)
for some
\gamma
and
\ell
, then

\gamma=A(\ell)
;

(2)

if
c_{i}^{j}=(!G,\gamma)
for some
G
and
\gamma
, then, for every
i^{\prime}\neq i
,

\ell_{i^{\prime}}\in G
implies that

n_{i^{\prime}}\hskip 15.60262pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.60262pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{i},\gamma)}
.

Essentially,
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n_{i}
amounts to saying that, for every
i\leq k
,

m_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{i}
plus condition 2.

The next lemma is used in the proof of
Lemma

4.60
. It proves that local monitors that ‘accidentally’ receive messages from another local monitor’s communication transitions are such that they cannot take any more receiving transitions matching those accidental messages. This captures the intuition that a local monitor always takes all receiving transitions that match a message at once, and no new receiving transitions appear for a local monitor when it is taking a chain of consecutive communication transitions.

Important in the proof is
Lemma

4.52
: in
\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces
local monitors can send and receive freely, but in
\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces
between decentralized monitors, communication is always initiated by a sender. Therefore, all receiving transitions that need to happen need to be initiated by some sending transition.

Lemma 4.59.

Let
\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)=\{\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{k}\}\neq\emptyset

and, for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell_{i}}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell_{i})}m_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces o_{i}
, with
o_{i}
that cannot communicate.
If
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n_{i}
,
then for every
j\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
there exist some

q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}
such that

\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[n_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[q_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
,

n_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}
,
and
q_{j}=o_{j}
.

Proof.

Fix any
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
let
n_{j}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces o_{j}
in
h_{j}
steps; we proceed by induction on
h_{j}
. In the base case, we have that
n_{j}=o_{j}
and so it suffices to consider
q_{i}=n_{i}
, for every
i
.

For the inductive step, let
n_{j}\xrightarrow{c}n^{\prime}_{j}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces o_{j}
with
c=(!G,\gamma)
or
c=(?\ell,A(\ell))
for some
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
:

(1)

Case
c=(!G,\gamma)
: by
Lemma

4.33
, we know that
\gamma=A(\ell_{j})
; moreover,

\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[n_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\xrightarrow{\ell_{j}:(!G,\gamma)}\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[n^{\prime}_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
, where
\bigvee_{i\in\{1..k\}\setminus\{j\}}[n_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell_{j},\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}\bigvee_{i\in\{1..k\}\setminus\{j\}}[n^{\prime}_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
by
Lemma

4.56
.

We now argue that
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n^{\prime}_{i}
. For each
\ell_{i}\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
, we have that
m_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{i}
, say in
g_{i}
steps.
By
Lemma

4.40
, we obtain that either
n_{i}\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{j},A(\ell_{j}))}n^{\prime}_{i}
or
n_{i}=n^{\prime}_{i}
, for all
i\neq j
; thus,
m_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n^{\prime}_{i}
for each
\ell_{i}\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
in
f_{i}=g_{i}+1
or
f_{i}=g_{i}
steps, respectively.
Furthermore, if
f_{i}=g_{i}+1
,
then
c_{i}^{g_{i}+1}=(?\ell_{j},A(\ell_{j}))
, for all
i\neq j
, and
c_{j}^{g_{j}+1}=c
.
For the second condition of
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n^{\prime}_{i}
,
let

i\neq j
and
m_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n^{\prime}_{i}
in

f_{i}
steps;
let
f\leq f_{i}
and
c_{i}^{f}=(!G^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
.
Now take some
\ell_{h}\in G^{\prime}\cap\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
with
h\neq i
, and we have to show that
n^{\prime}_{h}\hskip 16.95505pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-16.95505pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{i},\gamma^{\prime})}
.
If
f\leq g_{i}
, then
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n_{i}
implies that
n_{h}\hskip 16.95505pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-16.95505pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{i},\gamma^{\prime})}

and, by
Lemma

4.33
, we can conclude that
n^{\prime}_{h}\hskip 16.95505pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-16.95505pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{i},\gamma^{\prime})}
.
If
f=g_{i}+1
, then we have reasoned above that
i=j
,
G^{\prime}=G
and
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma
.
Furthermore, we know that either
n_{h}\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{i},\gamma)}n^{\prime}_{h}
or
n^{\prime}_{h}=n_{h}\hskip 15.60262pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.60262pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{i},\gamma)}
; in both cases,
Lemma

4.33
entails that
n^{\prime}_{h}\hskip 15.60262pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.60262pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{i},\gamma)}
and so
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n^{\prime}_{i}
.

By
Lemma

4.49
, for all
\ell_{i}\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
, we have that

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell_{i}}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell_{i})}m_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces o_{i}
. Since
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n^{\prime}_{i}
, we can apply the induction hypothesis to obtain that

\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[n^{\prime}_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[q_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
, for some
q_{i}
s such that
n^{\prime}_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}
and
q_{j}=o_{j}
.
It remains to show that
n_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}
:

m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n^{\prime}_{i}

and

n^{\prime}_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}

immediately yield
the first condition for
n_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}

and they yield the second condition by using

Lemma

4.33
.

(2)

Case
c=(?\ell,A(\ell))
: by
Lemma

4.52
,
\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
(say,
\ell=\ell_{h}
),
j\neq h
, and

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell_{h}}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell_{h})}p_{h}\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},A(\ell_{h}))}p^{\prime}_{h}
, for some
G^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
such that
\ell_{j}\in G^{\prime}
.
From
Lemma

4.57
,
p_{h}=m_{h}
.
Since
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n_{i}
, then in particular

m_{h}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{h}
with the same transitions;
by

Lemma

4.38
and
Lemma

4.35
,
n_{h}\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},A(\ell_{h}))}n^{\prime}_{h}
, if

m_{h}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{h}

does not include any transition labeled by
(!G^{\prime},A(\ell_{h}))
.
If such a transition does exist, from
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n_{i}
, we get that
n_{i}\hskip 24.36182pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-24.36182pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{h},A(\ell_{h}))}
, for all
i\neq h
and
\ell_{i}\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)\cap G^{\prime}
; but
this would contradict
n_{j}\xrightarrow{c}n^{\prime}_{j}
, since
\ell_{j}\in G^{\prime}
.
Hence, we have
n_{h}\xrightarrow{(!G^{\prime},A(\ell_{h}))}n^{\prime}_{h}
and so

\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[n_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\xrightarrow{\ell_{h}:(!G^{\prime},A(\ell_{h}))}\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[n^{\prime}_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
,
where, by
Lemma

4.56
,

\bigvee_{i\in\{1..k\}\setminus\{h\}}[n_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\overset{\textup{$G^{\prime}:(?\ell_{h},A(\ell_{h}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}\bigvee_{i\in\{1..k\}\setminus\{h\}}[n^{\prime}_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
.
We can now argue that
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n^{\prime}_{i}
as in case 1 above.
By
Lemma

4.50
,
n^{\prime}_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces q_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and, by the induction hypothesis,

\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[n^{\prime}_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[q_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}

with
n^{\prime}_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}
and
q_{j}=o_{j}
.
We can then conclude, by arguing that
n_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}
as in case 1.
∎

The next lemma is important for
Lemma

4.71
and
Lemma

4.76
(part of the lemmas for Formula Convergence). It is actually stronger than what is needed for
Lemma

4.71
, as therein we shall just need that
\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[n_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
transitions to some monitor that cannot comunicate, and not exactly to
\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[o_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
. However, the latter is needed for
Lemma

4.76
, so we present the stronger statement already here. The lemma shows that local monitors can be combined and, even if their communications ‘accidentally’ influence one another, the same final state (where no communication can take place) is reached. It also shows another important property: in
\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces
local monitors can send and receive freely, but in
\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces
between decentralized monitors communication is always initiated by a sender. This lemma captures that all the receiving transitions a local monitor can do before it reaches a state that cannot communicate are triggered by sending actions present in other local monitors located at locations found in the range of
\sigma
.

Lemma 4.60.

Let
\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)=\{\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{k}\}
and, for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell_{i}}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell_{i})}m_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces o_{i}
, with
o_{i}
that cannot communicate.
If
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n_{i}
, then
\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[n_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[o_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
k
. If
k=0
, the result is trivial.
In the inductive step,
by
Lemma

4.59
, there exist
q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
such that
\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[n_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}[q_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
,
n_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}
, and
q_{j}=o_{j}
.
For all
i\neq j
, we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell_{i}}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell_{i})}m_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces o_{i}
and
n_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces q_{i}
; by
Lemma

4.50
,
q_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces o_{i}
.
From this, we derive that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell_{i}}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell_{i})}m_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces q_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces o_{i}
, for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.

We now show that
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}
.
For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, we have
m_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{i}
(say, in
f_{i}
steps) and
n_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces q_{i}
(say, in
g_{i}
steps); thus,
m_{i}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces q_{i}
in
h_{i}=f_{i}+g_{i}
steps.
For the second condition of
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{i}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}
, take any
i
, any
h\leq h_{i}
(with
c_{i}^{h}=(!G,\gamma)
) and any
\ell_{i^{\prime}}\in G\cap\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
with
i^{\prime}\neq i
, and we have to show that
q_{i^{\prime}}\hskip 15.60262pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.60262pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{i},\gamma)}
.
If
h\leq f_{i}
, then
m_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}n_{i}
entails that
n_{i^{\prime}}\hskip 15.60262pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-15.60262pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{i},\gamma)}
; by
Lemma

4.33
,
q_{i^{\prime}}\hskip 14.72502pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-14.72502pt\xrightarrow{(?i\ell_{,}\gamma)}
.
On the other hand, if
h>f_{i}
, the result follows from
n_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{\ell_{i}}_{A}}{\!\!}^{k}_{i=1}q_{i}
.

Finally, the induction hypothesis entails that
\bigvee_{i\in\{1..k\}\setminus\{j\}}[q_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{i\in\{1..k\}\setminus\{j\}}[o_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
. Hence,
\bigvee_{i\in\{1..k\}}[n_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{i\in\{1..k\}\setminus\{j\}}[q_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\vee[o_{j}]_{\ell_{j}}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{i\in\{1..k\}}[o_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}
. In the last step we use that
o_{j}
cannot communicate and therefore does not change anymore.
∎

Another important feature that we need for Bounded Communication and Formula Convergence is the following: if different monitors influence each other via communication when combined (so a decentralized monitor is combined with another monitor and all of a sudden a local monitor in the decentralized system receives messages that it did not receive before), this makes no difference on the states the monitors reach when no more communication can take place.

We first set up a definition that helps us
with two things. First, it allows us to
describe what a decentralized monitor
M
looks like if it is combined via
\vee
or
\wedge
with another decentralized monitor, and then transitions according to a communication path of that other monitor. In other words,
M
will change state when the other monitor sends messages that local monitors in
M
can receive, and we formally capture what
M
looks like after these changes. Second, this definition will allow us to prove that influences from another monitor captured via
Definition

4.61
do not affect the final outcome (when no more communication takes place). These two elements then lead to
Lemma

4.70
, where the different monitors in a join will send messages that ‘accidentally’ reach the others and it is shown this is not a problem.
Lemma

4.70
is used to prove
Lemma

4.71
(Bounded Communication) and
Lemma

4.80
(Formula Convergence).

To this end, we define a set
L
of monitors that can all take the same receiving transition.
Definition

4.61
defines this set properly, and
Lemma

4.62
proves three general facts about this set.

Definition 4.61.

Let
H\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
and
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
. Let
L=\{[m_{1}]_{\ell_{1}},\dots,[m_{k}]_{\ell_{k}}\}
.
We say that
L
is a
set of recipients for
H:(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)
if, for all
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
:

(1)

\ell_{j}\in H
, and

(2)

m_{j}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n_{j}
, for some
n_{j}\in\mathsf{LMon}
.

We call a set
L
an
M
-cover
for
H:(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)
if

(1)

L
is a set of recipients for
H:(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)
,

(2)

every
[m]_{\ell}\in L

is action-derived, and

(3)

if
[m]_{\ell}\in M
,
\ell\in H
and

m\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n
,
then
[m^{\prime}]_{\ell}\in L
.

Lemma 4.62.

Let
H\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
,
M
be action-derived, and
L=\{[m_{1}]_{\ell_{1}},\dots,[m_{k}]_{\ell_{k}}\}
be an
M
-cover for
H:(?\ell,\gamma)
.

(1)

If
M=[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}\notin L
, and
M\xrightarrow{\ell^{\prime}:(!G,\gamma)}N
or
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N
, then
N=[n]_{\ell^{\prime}}\notin L
.

(2)

If
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N
, then
L
is also an
N
-cover for
H:(?\ell,\gamma)
.

(3)

If
M\xrightarrow{\ell^{\prime}:(!G,\gamma)}N
, then
L
is also an
N
-cover for
H:(?\ell,\gamma)
.

Proof.

For the first item, we observe that
N=[n]_{\ell^{\prime}}
, where

•

m=n
; or

•

m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime\prime},\gamma)}n
, or;

•

m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n
.

In the first case, we have
[n]_{\ell^{\prime}}=[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}\notin L
. In the other two cases, because
L
is an
M
-cover, either
\ell^{\prime}\notin H
, and so
[n]_{\ell^{\prime}}\notin L
, or
m\hskip 14.01869pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-14.01869pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}
, and by
Lemma

4.33
this implies that
n\hskip 14.01869pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-14.01869pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}
and again
[n]_{\ell^{\prime}}\notin L
.

For the second item, we proceed by induction on
\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}
.
For the base case,
M=[m]_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}
,
N=[n]_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}
,
\ell^{\prime\prime}\in G
, and
m=n
or
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}n
. Suppose that
n\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n_{2}
and
\ell^{\prime\prime}\in H
. Then, we also know that
m\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n_{2}
; as
[m]_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}=M
, because
L
is an
M
-cover, we obtain that
[n_{1}]_{\ell^{\prime\prime}}\in L
, yielding that
L
is an
N
-cover.
For the inductive case,
M=M_{1}\diamond M_{2}
,
N=N_{1}\diamond N_{2}
,
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}
and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
. Since
L
is an
M
-cover, it is also an
M_{1}
-cover and an
M_{2}
-cover. From the induction hypothesis,
L
is an
N_{1}
-cover and an
N_{2}
-cover, and thus an
N
-cover.

For the third item, we proceed by induction on
\xrightarrow{\ell^{\prime}:(!G,\gamma)}
. For the base case,
M=[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}
,
N=[n]_{\ell^{\prime}}
, and
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n
. Suppose that
n\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n_{2}
. Then, we also know that
m\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}n_{2}
; as
[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}=M
, because
L
is an
M
-cover, we obtain that
[n_{1}]_{\ell^{\prime}}\in L
, yielding that
L
is an
N
-cover.
For the inductive case,
M=M_{1}\diamond M_{2}
,
N=N_{1}\diamond N_{2}
,
M_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell^{\prime}:(!G,\gamma)}N_{1}
and
