M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
. Since
L
is an
M
-cover, it is also an
M_{1}
-cover and an
M_{2}
-cover. From the induction hypothesis and the previous item,
L
is an
N_{1}
-cover and an
N_{2}
-cover, and thus an
N
-cover.
∎

In what follows it is useful to describe the state of a monitor
M
after receiving the message corresponding to
L
. To do this formally, we observe the following:

Lemma 4.63.

Let
L
be an
M
-cover for
H:(?\ell,\gamma)
.
For every
[m]_{\ell^{\prime}}\in L
, if

m\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}m_{1}
and
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell,\gamma)}m_{2}
, then
m_{1}=m_{2}
and
[m_{1}]_{\ell}\notin L
.

Proof.

The lemma follows immediately from
Lemmas

4.33
and
4.36
.
∎

With this lemma, we can then define what
M
after
L
is:

Definition 4.64.

Let
L=\{[m_{1}]_{\ell_{1}},\dots,[m_{k}]_{\ell_{k}}\}

be an
M
-cover for
H:c
and, for each
i\leq k
, let
n_{i}
be the unique monitor (by
Lemma

4.63
) such that

m_{i}\xrightarrow{c}n_{i}
.
We define
M[L]=M[[n_{1}]_{\ell_{1}}/[m_{1}]_{\ell_{1}}]\dots[[n_{k}]_{\ell_{k}}/[m_{k}]_{\ell_{k}}]
.

We can now use
Definitions

4.61
and
4.64
and
Lemma

4.62
to describe a monitor that ‘accidentally’ receives messages from another monitor’s communication transitions.

Lemma 4.65.

Let

M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be
A
-derived and such that

M_{1}^{1}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}M_{1}^{2}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}\dots\xrightarrow{c_{n-1}}M_{1}^{n}

and

M_{1}^{1}\diamond M_{2}^{1}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}M_{1}^{2}\diamond M_{2}^{2}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}\dots\xrightarrow{c_{n-1}}M^{n}_{1}\diamond M^{n}_{2}
,
where

M_{1}^{1}=M_{1}
,

M_{2}^{1}=M_{2}
,

n>0
,
and

c_{i}=\ell_{i}:(!G_{i},A(\ell_{i}))
, for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
.
Then, for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, there exists
an
M_{2}
-cover

L_{i}
for

G_{i}:(?\ell_{i},A(\ell_{i}))
,
such that

M_{2}[L_{1}]\dots[L_{i}]=M_{2}^{i+1}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
. If
n=1
, then
the lemma holds vacuously.
Otherwise, we have

M_{1}^{1}\diamond M_{2}^{1}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}M_{1}^{2}\diamond M_{2}^{2}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}\dots\xrightarrow{c_{n-2}}M^{n-1}_{1}\diamond M^{n-1}_{2}\xrightarrow{c_{n-1}}M^{n}_{1}\diamond M^{n}_{2}.

The induction hypothesis tells us that there exist

L_{1},\dots,L_{n-2}
such that, for each
i\in\{1,\dots,n-2\}
,
L_{i}
is an

M_{2}
-cover
for
G_{i}:(?\ell_{i},A(\ell_{i}))
,
and

M_{2}[L_{1}]\cdots[L_{i}]=M_{2}^{i+1}
.
Let

L_{n-1}=\{[m^{\prime}]_{\ell}\mid\ell\in G_{n-1}\text{ and }\exists\,[m]_{\ell}\in M_{2}.~m\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{n-1},A(\ell_{n-1}))}m^{\prime\prime}\}.

Then,
L_{n-1}
is an

M_{2}
-cover
for
G_{n-1}:(?\ell_{n-1},A(\ell_{n-1}))
by
Definition

4.61
,
and also an

M_{2}^{i}
-cover
for
G_{n-1}:(?\ell_{n-1},A(\ell_{n-1}))
by
Lemmas

4.56
and
4.62
.

What is left to show is that
M_{2}[L_{1}]\cdots[L_{n-2}][L_{n-1}]=M_{2}^{n}
. We can conclude this from the induction hypothesis, if we show that
M_{2}^{n-1}[L_{n-1}]=M_{2}^{n}
. By
Lemma

4.56
,
M_{2}^{n-1}\overset{\textup{$G_{n-1}:(\ell_{n-1},\gamma_{n-1})$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}M_{2}^{n}
.
Let
\{[m_{i}]_{\ell_{i}}\mid 1\leq i\leq k\}=\{[m]_{\ell}\in M_{2}^{n-1}\}
.
For each
1\leq i\leq k
, let
m^{\prime}_{i}
be
m_{i}
, when
\ell_{i}\notin G_{n-1}
or
m_{i}\hskip 24.68666pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-24.68666pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{n-1},\gamma_{n-1})}
, or be such that
m_{i}\xrightarrow{(?\ell_{n-1},\gamma_{n-1})}m^{\prime}_{i}
, otherwise.
Then,
M[L_{n-1}]=M_{2}^{n-1}[[m^{\prime}_{1}]_{\ell_{1}}/[m_{1}]_{\ell_{1}}]\cdots[[m^{\prime}_{k}]_{\ell_{k}}/[m_{k}]_{\ell_{k}}]=M_{2}^{n}
, by
Lemma

4.40
.
∎

Lemma

4.66
and
Lemma

4.67
are used to prove
Lemma

4.68
, and they state some basic facts of behavior of
M[L]
w.r.t. the behavior of
M
.

Lemma 4.66.

Let
H\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
,
M
be
A
-derived, and
L
be an
M
-cover for
H:(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))
. If
M\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N
, for some
\ell^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}
, then
M[L]\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N[L]
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}
. In the first base case,
M=[m]_{\ell}
,
N=[n]_{\ell}
,
\ell\in G
and
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))}n
. If
[m]_{\ell}\notin L
, then
[n]_{\ell}\notin L
via
Lemma

4.62
. Hence,
M=M[L]
and
N=N[L]
, which concludes the proof. Otherwise,
M[L]=[m_{1}]_{\ell}
with
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}m_{1}
and
\ell\in H
. We distinguish two cases:

(1)

\ell^{\prime\prime}\neq\ell^{\prime}
. We then apply
Lemma

4.37
on
m
:

n\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n_{1}
and
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))}n_{1}
. From this, we derive that
M[L]=[m_{1}]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[n_{1}]_{\ell}
. Since
L
is an
M
-cover, it is also an
N
-cover via
Lemma

4.62
.
Thus, from
n\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n_{1}
we obtain that

N=[n]_{\ell}\in L
. Hence, from

n\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n_{1}
and
via
Lemma

4.63
, we get

N[L]=[n_{1}]_{\ell}

and
M[L]=[m_{1}]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[n_{1}]_{\ell}=N[L]
.

(2)

\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell^{\prime}
. Then,
A(\ell^{\prime\prime})=A(\ell^{\prime})
, and, via
Lemma

4.36
, we obtain that
m_{1}=n
; so,
M[L]=[m_{1}]_{\ell}=[n]_{\ell}=N
. From
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}m_{1}
and
Lemma

4.33
, we conclude that
m_{1}\hskip 22.42891pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-22.42891pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}
. Since
\ell^{\prime\prime}=\ell^{\prime}
and
m_{1}=n
, it holds that
n\hskip 24.63379pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-24.63379pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))}
.
and
n\hskip 22.42891pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-22.42891pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}
, which implies that
[n]_{\ell}\notin L
, and therefore
N=N[L]
.
From
n\hskip 24.63379pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-24.63379pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))}
we derive that
M[L]=[n]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[n]_{\ell}=N=N[L]
, which is what we wanted to show.

In the second base case, we consider
M=[m]_{\ell}
,
N=[m]_{\ell}
, and
m\hskip 24.63379pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-24.63379pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))}
. If
M=M[L]
, then
[m]_{\ell}\notin L
(by
Lemma

4.63
), and thus also
N=N[L]
, which concludes the proof. Otherwise, we have
that
M[L]=[m_{1}]_{\ell}
with
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}m_{1}
and
\ell\in H
. We know that
M=N
, so
M[L]=N[L]
. We know that
m\hskip 24.63379pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-24.63379pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))}
and, via
Lemma

4.33
, that
m_{1}\hskip 24.63379pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-24.63379pt\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))}
. We can conclude that
M[L]=[m_{1}]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[m_{1}]_{\ell}=N[L]
.

In the third base case, we consider
M=[m]_{\ell}
,
N=[m]_{\ell}
and
\ell\notin G
. If
M=M[L]
, then
[m]_{\ell}\notin L
, and thus also
N=N[L]
, which concludes the proof. Otherwise, we have
that
M[L]=[m_{1}]_{\ell}
with
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}m_{1}
and
\ell\in H
. We know that
M=N
, so
M[L]=N[L]
. Since
\ell\notin G
, we can conclude that
M[L]=[m_{1}]_{\ell}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}[m_{1}]_{\ell}=N[L]
.

For the inductive step,
M=M_{1}\diamond M_{2}
,
N=N_{1}\diamond N_{2}
,
M_{1}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}
, and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
. Clearly,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are
A
-derived and
L
is an
M_{1}
-cover and an
M_{2}
-cover; thus, we can apply the induction hypothesis to obtain that
M_{1}[L]\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}[L]
, and
M_{2}[L]\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}[L]
. From this, we derive
M[L]=M_{1}[L]\diamond M_{2}[L]\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell^{\prime\prime},A(\ell^{\prime\prime}))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{1}[L]\diamond N_{2}[L]=N[L]
.
∎

Lemma 4.67.

Let
H\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
,
M
be
A
-derived and
L
be an
M
-cover for
H:(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))
.
If
M\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}N
, then
M[L]\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}N[L]
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by induction on
\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}
. For the base case,
M=[m]_{\ell}
,
N=[n]_{\ell}
and
m\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n
. If
M=M[L]
, then also
N=N[L]
(by
Lemma

4.62
) and the proof is done. Otherwise, we must have that
M[L]=[m]_{\ell}[[m_{1}]_{\ell}/[m]_{\ell}]=[m_{1}]_{\ell}
for
m\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}m_{1}
and
\ell\in H
. By
Lemma

4.35
,
n\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n_{1}
and
m_{1}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}n_{1}
. Since
L
is an
M
-cover, we know it is also an
N
-cover (
Lemma

4.62
) and therefore
[n]_{\ell}\in L
. Then we obtain from
n\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},A(\ell^{\prime}))}n_{1}

and
Lemma

4.63

that

N[L]=[n_{1}]_{\ell}
;
hence,
M[L]=[m_{1}]_{\ell}\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}[n_{1}]_{\ell}=N[L]
.

For the inductive step,
M=M_{1}\diamond M_{2}
,
N=N_{1}\diamond N_{2}
,
M_{1}\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}N_{1}
, and
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,\gamma)$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
. Clearly,
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are
A
-derived, and
L
is an
M_{1}
-cover and an
M_{2}
-cover; thus, we can apply the induction hypothesis to obtain that
M_{1}[L]\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}N_{1}[L]
. By
Lemma

4.34
,
\gamma=A(\ell)
. Since
M_{2}\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,A(\ell))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}
, we can apply
Lemma

4.66
to get
M_{2}[L]\overset{\textup{$G:(?\ell,A(\ell))$}}{\resizebox{0}{0}{$\rightsquigarrow$}}N_{2}[L]
. Thus
M[L]=M_{1}[L]\diamond M_{2}[L]\xrightarrow{\ell:(!G,\gamma)}N_{1}[L]\diamond N_{2}[L]=N[L]
.
∎

The next lemma shows that
M_{i}[L]
is the state of
M_{i}
after receiving the message corresponding to
L
. This can be understood as
M_{i}
receiving messages from some other decentralized monitor (as what happens in
Lemma

4.70
). So, even if
M_{i}
receives at any point some messages from another decentralized monitor, the same state is reached when no more communication can take place.

Lemma 4.68.

Let
H\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
,
M_{1}
be action-derived, and

M_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}M_{2}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}\dots\xrightarrow{c_{n-1}}M_{n}\xrightarrow{c_{n}}N
, where
n\geq 1
and
N
cannot communicate. If
s\leq n
and
L
is an
M_{s}
-cover for
H:(?\ell,A(\ell))
,
then, for all
i\in\{s,\ldots,n\}
, it holds that

M_{i}[L]\xrightarrow{c_{i}}M_{i+1}[L]\xrightarrow{c_{i+1}}\dots\xrightarrow{c_{n-1}}M_{n}[L]\xrightarrow{c_{n}}N
.

Proof.

We prove this by induction on

n-i
.
For the base case,
i=n\geq 1
; thus, we know that
M_{n}\xrightarrow{c_{n}}N
, and we need to prove that
M_{n}[L]\xrightarrow{c_{n}}N[L]
. By
Lemma

4.62
,
L
is an
M_{n}
-cover;
hence, we can apply
Lemma

4.67

to conclude that
M_{n}[L]\xrightarrow{c_{n}}N[L]
. Since
N
cannot communicate by assumption, we know that
N[L]=N
.

In the inductive step, we obtain from the induction hypothesis that
M_{i+1}[L]\xrightarrow{c_{i+1}}\dots\xrightarrow{c_{n-1}}M_{n}[L]\xrightarrow{c_{n}}N
. Then, we apply
Lemma

4.67
to conclude that
M_{i}[L]\xrightarrow{c_{i}}M_{i+1}[L]
, which completes the proof.
∎

We can generalize the previous result to include a union of
L
’s.
Notationally, if
L=L_{1}\cup\ldots\cup L_{k}
, we denote
M[L_{1}]\ldots[L_{k}]
as
M[L]
.
This lemma is a cornerstone for proving
Lemma

4.70
(together with
Lemma

4.65
).

Lemma 4.69.

Let
H\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
,
M_{1}
be action-derived, and

M_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}M_{2}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}\dots\xrightarrow{c_{n-1}}M_{n}\xrightarrow{c_{n}}N
,
where
n\geq 1
and
N
cannot communicate.
Let
s\leq n
and

L=\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}L_{j}

such that
L_{j}
is an
M_{s}
-cover for
H_{j}:(?\ell_{j},A(\ell_{i}))
, for all
j
.
Then, for all
i\in\{s,\ldots,n\}
, it holds that

M_{i}[L]\xrightarrow{c_{i}}M_{i+1}[L]\xrightarrow{c_{i+1}}\dots\xrightarrow{c_{n-1}}M_{n}[L]\xrightarrow{c_{n}}N
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
k
. If
k=0
, the result is trivial.
In the inductive step, we obtain from the induction hypothesis that

M_{i}[L_{1}]\dots[L_{k-1}]\xrightarrow{c_{i}}M_{i+1}[L_{1}]\dots[L_{k-1}]\xrightarrow{c_{i+1}}\dots\xrightarrow{c_{n-1}}M_{n}[L_{1}]\dots[L_{k-1}]\xrightarrow{c_{n}}N.

If we show that
L_{k}
is a cover for
M_{i}[L_{1}]\dots[L_{k-1}]
, the result follows immediately from
Lemma

4.68
. Take
[m]_{\ell}\in M_{i}[L_{1}]\dots[L_{k-1}]
with
\ell\in H_{k}
such that
m\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{k},A(\ell^{k}))}n
. Hence, we know that there exists
[m_{1}]_{\ell}\in M_{i}
such that
m_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m
, and thus also
m_{1}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m^{\prime}
. As
L_{k}
is an
M_{s}
-cover, it is also an
M_{i}
-cover via
Lemma

4.62
. Hence, we obtain that
[m^{\prime}]_{\ell}\in L_{k}
, as desired.
∎

The next lemma is actually stronger than what is needed for Bounded Communication (for Bounded Communication it is sufficient that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}M_{\ell}
in the lemma below can transition to some decentralized monitor that cannot communicate, and it does not exactly need to be
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}N_{\ell}
). However, for Formula Convergence we need the lemma in its strong version, so we prove only that one.

Lemma 4.70.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{k}\}
and, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})\xrightarrow{A}M_{i}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N_{i}
, where
N_{i}
cannot communicate.
Then,

\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{i=1}^{k}M_{i}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{i=1}^{k}N_{i}
.

Proof.

By induction on
k
.
If
k=1
, we are done immediately. If
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell_{1},\dots\ell_{k+1}\}
, let
M_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}M_{1}^{2}\xrightarrow{c_{2}}\dots\xrightarrow{c_{n}}N_{1}
for
n\geq 0
;
by
Lemma

4.34
,
c_{j}=\ell_{j}:(!G_{j},A(\ell_{j}))
, for all
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
By
Lemma

4.65
, there exist
L_{1},\dots,L_{n}
such that, for each
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
L_{j}
is a
(\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{i=2}^{k}M_{i})
-cover for
G_{j}:(?\ell_{j},A(\ell_{j}))
.
Let

N=\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{\ell=2}^{k}M_{i}[L_{1}]\dots[L_{n}];

then,

\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{i=1}^{k}M_{i}=M_{1}\diamond(\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{i=2}^{k}M_{i})\xrightarrow{c_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow{c_{n}}N_{1}\diamond N
.
From the
induction hypothesis, we know that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{i=2}^{k}M_{i}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{i=2}^{k}N_{i}
.
Thus, we can apply
Lemma

4.69
to conclude that
N\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{i=2}^{k}N_{i}
.
From
\mathop{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{2}{$\displaystyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Diamond$}}}}}_{i=1}^{k}M_{i}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N_{1}\diamond N
we can conclude, since
N_{1}
cannot take any communication steps and therefore does not change anymore.
∎

We can now finally prove Bounded Communication:

Lemma 4.71.

For every
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}
, there exists
M^{\prime\prime}
such that
M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime\prime}
and
M^{\prime\prime}
cannot communicate.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\varphi
. The base case is for
\varphi\in\mathsf{Qf}

and we have two possible subcases. If
\sigma=\emptyset
, then
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=[v]_{\ell_{0}}\xrightarrow{A}[v]_{\ell_{0}}=M=M^{\prime}
and
[v]_{\ell_{0}}
cannot communicate, since
v\not\xrightarrow{c}
, for all
c
.
If
\sigma\neq\emptyset
, then

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\varphi)]_{\ell}\xrightarrow{A}\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[m_{\ell}]_{\ell}=M
and
M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[n_{\ell}]_{\ell}=M^{\prime}
, where
m_{\ell}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n_{\ell}
. Actually, we have that
m_{\ell}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n_{\ell}
because we know from the semantics that, for any receive
\xrightarrow{(?\ell^{\prime},\gamma)}
in the path
m_{\ell}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces n_{\ell}
, there exists
\ell^{\prime}
such that
m_{\ell^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{\ell^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{(!G,\gamma)}
(with
\ell\in G
) and, by
Lemma

4.33
, that
\gamma=A(\ell^{\prime})
.

As
n_{\ell}
is a relevant local monitor, we prove by induction on the structure of relevant local monitors that there exists
n^{\prime}_{\ell}
such that
n_{\ell}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n^{\prime}_{\ell}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, for all
c
. The first two base cases (
n_{\ell}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
and
n_{\ell}=(!G,a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
) are trivial because of
Lemma

4.17
. For the third base case,
n_{\ell}=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})
, we observe that there is some
a\in\mathsf{Act}
such that
a=A(\ell^{\prime})
; so, we take that transition and conclude again by
Lemma

4.17
. For the inductive case,
n_{\ell}=n_{1}\odot n_{2}
and, from the inductive hypothesis,
n_{1}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m_{1}
and
n_{2}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m_{2}
such that
m_{1}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
and
m_{2}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, for all
c
. By
Lemma

4.51
,
n_{1}\odot n_{2}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces m_{1}\odot m_{2}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, for all
c
.

Hence,
n_{\ell}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n^{\prime}_{\ell}
such that
n^{\prime}_{\ell}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, for all
c
. From this, we derive that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{\ell}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces n^{\prime}_{\ell}
, for all
\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)
. We can then apply
Lemma

4.60
(with
m_{\ell}=n_{\ell}
in the statement of the lemma) to conclude that
\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[m_{\ell}]_{\ell}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[n^{\prime}_{\ell}]_{\ell}
. It is immediate that
\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[n^{\prime}_{\ell}]_{\ell}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, for all
c
. We apply
Lemma

4.47
to derive that
M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[n^{\prime}_{\ell}]_{\ell}
, as desired.

We only treat one of the inductive cases, the others are similar. Let
\varphi=\exists\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
. Then
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{A}\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}M_{\ell}=M
.
From the induction hypothesis we obtain that
M_{\ell}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N_{\ell}
such that
N_{\ell}
cannot communicate. We apply
Lemma

4.70
and conclude that
M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}N_{\ell}
. Then we can use
Lemma

4.47
to derive that
M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}N_{\ell}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

{textAtEnd}

[allend, category=pca]

We showed the first item of Processing-Communication Alternation in
Lemma

4.17
.
For the second item, we actually prove the contrapositive, because this simplifies the inductive proofs; we first show the result for local monitors and then we conclude by an induction based on
Lemma

4.72
.

Lemma 4.72.

Let
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces m\xrightarrow{A^{\prime}(\ell)}n
. Then
m\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, for all
c
.

Proof.

We focus on relevant local monitors, because
m
is a relevant local monitor via
Lemma

4.21
.
We proceed by induction on
m
.
In the base case, for
m={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
the result follows from
Lemma

4.17
;
for
m=(!G,a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)
and
m=\sum_{a\in\mathsf{Act}}(?\{\ell^{\prime\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{a})
, the premise of the lemma is not satisfied.

For the inductive step, let
m=m_{1}\odot m_{2}
with
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
relevant monitors. We derive from
m_{1}\odot m_{2}\xrightarrow{A^{\prime}(\ell)}n
that
n=n_{1}\odot n_{2}
,
m_{1}\xrightarrow{A^{\prime}(\ell)}n_{1}
and
m_{2}\xrightarrow{A^{\prime}(\ell)}n_{2}
. Hence, we can apply the induction hypothesis to obtain that
m_{1}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
and
m_{2}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, from which we conclude.
∎

Lemma 4.73.

Let
M\in\mathsf{DMon}
be action-derived. If
M\xrightarrow{A}N
, then
M\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, for all
c
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\xrightarrow{A}
.
For the first base case, we consider
M=[m]_{\ell}
,
N=[n]_{\ell}
,
m\xrightarrow{a}n
and
A(\ell)=a
; by
Lemma

4.72
,
m\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
, from which we obtain that
M\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
.
For the second base case, we consider
M=[m]_{\ell}
, and by assumption
m\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
.
For the inductive step, we consider
M=M_{1}\diamond M_{2}
,
N=N_{1}\diamond N_{2}
,
M_{1}\xrightarrow{A}N_{1}
and
M_{2}\xrightarrow{A}N_{2}
. From the induction hypothesis, it follows that
M_{1}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
and
M_{2}\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
; so,
M\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
.
∎

{textAtEnd}

[allend, category=fc]

We start with a lemma that
captures a basic equality between synthesized centralized and decentralized local monitors, used in the proofs of
Lemma

4.75
and
Lemma

4.78
.

Lemma 4.74.

If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
, for either
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
or
|{\mathcal{L}}|=1
,
then
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\psi
.
As a first base case, we consider
\psi=\mathsf{tt}
: we have
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{yes}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
and, by
Lemma

4.22
, we conclude that, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
, we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{yes}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
. The base cases for
\psi=\mathsf{ff}
,
\psi=(\pi=\pi^{\prime})
and
\psi=(\pi\neq\pi^{\prime})
are similar.
The last base case is for
\psi=x
: then,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=x={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
, from which we can conclude that
\psi^{\prime}=x
. Immediately it follows that, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=x={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
.

We now proceed with the inductive cases. We first consider
\psi=[a_{\pi}]\psi_{1}

(the case for
\psi=\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi_{1}
is the same, with
\mathsf{no}
in place of
\mathsf{yes}
);
thus
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=a_{\sigma(\pi)}.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})+\sum_{b\neq a}b_{\sigma(\pi)}.\mathsf{yes}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
and so
\psi^{\prime}=[a_{\pi^{\prime}}]\psi_{2}
,
\sigma(\pi)=\sigma^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime})
and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi_{2})
. We use the induction hypothesis to obtain that, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
, we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi_{2})
. We have two possibilities:

(1)

\sigma(\pi)\ (=\sigma^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime}))=\ell
. In this case,

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=a.(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)\setminus\{\ell\}),a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})+\sum_{b\neq a}b.(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)\setminus\{\ell\}),b).\mathsf{yes}
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})=a.(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma^{\prime})\setminus\{\ell\}),a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi_{2})+\sum_{b\neq a}b.(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma^{\prime})\setminus\{\ell\}),b).\mathsf{yes}
. Using the induction hypothesis, we obtain the required result, since
\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)\setminus\{\ell\}=\mathsf{rng}(\sigma^{\prime})\setminus\{\ell\}
, both if
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
and if
|{\mathcal{L}}|=1
(and so
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell\}
).

(2)

\sigma(\pi)\ (=\sigma^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime}))\neq\ell
(notice that this case is not possible if
|{\mathcal{L}}|=1
). Then,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\sum_{b\in\mathsf{Act}}b.\left(?(\{\sigma(\pi)\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})+\sum_{b\neq a}(?(\{\sigma(\pi)\}),b).\mathsf{yes}\right)

and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})=\sum_{b\in\mathsf{Act}}b.\left(?(\{\sigma^{\prime}(\pi)\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi_{2})+\sum_{b\neq a}(?(\{\sigma^{\prime}(\pi^{\prime})\}),b).\mathsf{yes}\right)
. Using the induction hypothesis, we obtain the required result.

Next we consider
\psi=\textrm{max}~x.\psi_{1}
. Hence,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
. From this we conclude that
\psi^{\prime}=\textrm{max}~x.\psi_{2}
and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi_{2})
. We use the induction hypothesis to obtain that, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
, we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi_{2})
; thus, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
,
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\textrm{max}~x.\psi_{1})=\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})=\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi_{2})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\textrm{max}~x.\psi_{2})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
.

Finally, let
\psi=\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2}
. Hence,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\odot{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi^{\prime})
(for
\odot=\otimes
if
\diamond=\wedge
and
\odot=\oplus
if
\diamond=\vee
)), with
\psi^{\prime}=\psi_{1}^{\prime}\diamond\psi_{2}^{\prime}
,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi_{1}^{\prime})
and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\psi_{2}^{\prime})
. The result immediately follows from the induction hypothesis.
∎

To prove Formula Convergence for quantifier free formulas, we need the following result. Informally, it captures part of the weak bisimulation at the level of local monitors.

Lemma 4.75.

Let
\psi\in\mathsf{Qf}
. If

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
, then
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{\ell}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\xrightarrow{A}
for central monitors.
The base case with
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=a_{\ell}.m
never applies, because of the assumption that
|\mathsf{Act}|\geq 2
. Hence, the only base case is for
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=v={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
; then,
Lemma

4.22
entails that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)=v={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
, and we can conclude, as
v\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}v\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces v
for any
A(\ell)
.

For the inductive case, let
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m+n\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
;
hence, either
\psi=[a_{\pi}]\psi^{\prime\prime}

or
\psi=\langle a_{\pi}\rangle\psi^{\prime\prime}
,
and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=a_{\sigma(\pi)}.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime\prime})+\sum_{b\neq a}b_{\sigma(\pi)}.v
,
where
v=\mathsf{yes}
, in case of box, and
v=\mathsf{no}
, in case of diamond.
We distinguish two cases.

(1)

\sigma(\pi)=\ell^{\prime}
and
A(\ell^{\prime})=a
; this entails that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime\prime})
.
Fix
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
. If
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
, we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)\setminus\{\ell\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime\prime})
; if
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}
, we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime\prime})+\sum_{b\neq a}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},b).v
. Thus, in both situations we can have that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{\ell}\xrightarrow{c}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime\prime})
, where
\gamma=A(\ell^{\prime})\ (=a)
whenever
c=(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},\gamma)
. From
Lemma

4.74

and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime\prime})
, we conclude
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime\prime})
, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
.

(2)

\sigma(\pi)=\ell^{\prime}
,
A(\ell^{\prime})=b
, and
a\neq b
; this entails that
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})=v
. We use
Lemma

4.22
to conclude that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})=v
, for all
\ell
.
Fix
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
. If
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
, we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)\setminus\{\ell\}),b).v=(!(\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)\setminus\{\ell\},b).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
; if
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}
, we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime\prime})+\sum_{b\neq a}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},b).v=(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},a).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime\prime})+\sum_{b\neq a}(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},b).{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
. Thus in both situations we can conclude that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{\ell}\xrightarrow{c}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
, where
\gamma=A(\ell^{\prime})
whenever
c=(?\{\ell^{\prime}\},\gamma)
.

Next we consider the recursive case:
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\mathsf{rec}~x.m\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
because
m\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.m}/_{x}\}\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
. Thus we obtain that
\psi=\max x.\psi_{1}
and
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})
. As
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi_{1}}/_{x}\})
(
Lemma

3.8
), we use the induction hypothesis to obtain that, for all
\ell
, we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi_{1}}/_{x}\})\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{\ell}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
. Using that

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})}/_{x}\}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1}\{^{\textrm{max}~x.\psi_{1}}/_{x}\})
(
Lemma

4.16
), we get that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\{^{\mathsf{rec}~x.{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})}/_{x}\}\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{\ell}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
. This implies that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{\ell}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
.

Last, let
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)=m\odot n\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}\odot n^{\prime}
because
m\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
and
n\xrightarrow{A}n^{\prime}
. As
\psi
is quantifier-free, we get that
\psi=\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2}
(for
\diamond=\wedge
if
\odot=\otimes
and
\diamond=\vee
if
\odot=\oplus
),
m={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})
and
n={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})
. Also,
\psi^{\prime}=\psi_{1}^{\prime}\diamond\psi_{2}^{\prime}
,
m^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1}^{\prime})
and
n^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2}^{\prime})
. From the induction hypothesis we obtain that, for all
\ell
, we have
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1})\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{\ell}^{1}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1}^{\prime})
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2})\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{\ell}^{2}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2}^{\prime})
. Thus, for all
\ell
, we have that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1}\diamond\psi_{2})\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{\ell}^{1}\odot m_{\ell}^{2}
and, via
Lemma

4.51
(that can be used thanks to
Lemma

4.17
), we conclude the desired
m_{\ell}^{1}\odot m_{\ell}^{2}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{1}^{\prime})\odot{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi_{2}^{\prime})
.
∎

We can now prove Formula Convergence for
\psi\in\mathsf{Qf}
:

Lemma 4.76.

Let
\psi\in\mathsf{Qf}
.
If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}
, with
M^{\prime}
that cannot communicate, and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
, for some formula
\psi^{\prime}
, then
M^{\prime}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
.

Proof.

First, since
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A}M
, we have that
\mathsf{fv}_{\mathsf{l}}(\psi)\neq\emptyset
and so
\sigma\neq\emptyset
.
Then,
Lemma

4.75
tells us that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A(\ell)}m_{\ell}\ignorespaces\Rightarrow_{\!\ignorespaces\mathnormal{A}}\ignorespaces{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
, for all
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
. Thus, we know that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)=\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi)]_{\ell}\xrightarrow{A}\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[m_{\ell}]_{\ell}
; because
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})\hskip 5.05597pt\mathclap{/}\hskip-5.05597pt\xrightarrow{c}
for all
c
(
Lemma

4.17
), we can use
Lemma

4.60
(by taking
n_{\ell}=m_{\ell}
in the statement of that result) to obtain that
\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[m_{\ell}]_{\ell}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathsf{rng}(\sigma)}[{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})]_{\ell}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
cannot communicate. Hence, we apply
Lemma

4.47
to derive that
M^{\prime}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
. Since
M^{\prime}
cannot communicate by assumption, we get that
M^{\prime}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\psi)
.
∎

Then we show a result for the synthesis function in the centralized setting specifically for
\psi
-formulas, which differs from
Section

3.2
because
\sigma
remains constant. This allows us to prove the base case in the proof for Formula Convergence (
Lemma

4.80
) via
Lemma

4.76
.

Lemma 4.77.

If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi)\xrightarrow{A}m^{\prime}
, then
m^{\prime}={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\psi^{\prime})
for some
\psi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The proof is a simplified version of the proof for
Section

3.2
.
∎

The next lemma is used to prove
Lemma

4.79
, which in turn is used in the final steps of the proof of Formula Convergence (
Lemma

4.80
).

Lemma 4.78.

If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
and
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell\}
, then
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\varphi
. In the base case we consider
\varphi\in\mathsf{Qf}
: by
Lemma

4.74

and
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell\}
, we obtain
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=[{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma}(\varphi)]_{\ell}=[{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{m}^{\ell}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})]_{\ell}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
.

In the inductive case for
\varphi=\exists\pi.\varphi^{\prime\prime}
, we have
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
. From the induction hypothesis we derive that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
, which proves the claim, since
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)
because
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell\}
. The case for
\varphi=\forall\pi.\varphi^{\prime\prime}
is similar

Finally, let
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\diamond\varphi_{2}
. Hence,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{1})\odot{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{2})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
(for
\odot=\otimes
if
\diamond=\wedge
and
\odot=\oplus
if
\diamond=\vee
). As
{\mathcal{L}}=\{\ell\}
, we know that
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\diamond\varphi_{2}^{\prime}
with
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{1})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi_{1}^{\prime})
and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{2})={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi_{2}^{\prime})
. From the induction hypothesis we conclude that
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{1})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi_{1}^{\prime})
and
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{2})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi_{2}^{\prime})
. The result now follows:
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{1})\diamond{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{2})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi_{1}^{\prime})\diamond{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi_{2}^{\prime})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
.
∎

Lemma 4.79.

If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\oplus_{i\in I}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
, then
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\bigvee_{i\in I}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
.
If
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\otimes_{i\in I}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
, then
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\bigwedge_{i\in I}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
.

Proof.

We prove only the first claim (the second one is similar) and assume that
\oplus_{i\in I}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
is maximal, i.e., no
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
is of the form

m_{1}\oplus m_{2}
.
We proceed by induction on
\varphi
.
If
|I|=1
(this covers the base cases – i.e., when
\varphi
is an atom – and somee inductive cases – e.g., an existential but with
|{\mathcal{L}}|=1
), then we have
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
and the result follows from
Lemma

4.78
.

If
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}
, then
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{1})\oplus{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{2})=\oplus_{i\in I}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
.
Then, there is some
J\subsetneq I
such that

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{1})=\oplus_{i\in J}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
and

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{2})=\oplus_{i\in I\setminus J}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
.
By the induction hypothesis,

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{1})=\bigvee_{i\in J}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
and

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{2})=\bigvee_{i\in I\setminus J}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
, and therefore

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{1})\vee{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi_{2})=\bigvee_{i\in I}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
.

Now consider
\varphi=\exists\pi.\varphi^{\prime}
, with
|{\mathcal{L}}|>1
; then,

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})
. From our assumption on the maximality of
\oplus_{i\in I}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
, there is a partition
(I_{\ell})_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}
of
I
such that

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})=\oplus_{i\in I_{\ell}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
, for every
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
.
By the induction hypothesis,

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})=\bigvee_{i\in I_{\ell}}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
, for every
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
, and we can conclude that

{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime})=\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}\bigvee_{i\in I_{\ell}}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})=\bigvee_{i\in I}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{i}}(\varphi_{i})
.
∎

Lemma 4.80.

If
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}
, with
M^{\prime}
that cannot communicate, and
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
, for some formula
\varphi^{\prime}
and environment
\sigma^{\prime}
, then
M^{\prime}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\varphi
.
The case for
\psi
follows from
Lemma

4.76
, where we know that
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
because of
Lemma

4.77
.

We start with the inductive case for
\varphi=\exists\pi.\varphi^{\prime\prime}
. Since
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma}(\exists\pi.\varphi^{\prime\prime})=\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
, we have
M=\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}M_{\ell}
, where
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})\xrightarrow{A}M_{\ell}
, for each
\ell
. By Bounded Communication, we obtain that, for all
\ell
, there exists
N_{\ell}
such that
M_{\ell}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N_{\ell}
and
N_{\ell}
cannot communicate. We apply
Lemma

4.70
and conclude that
M\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}N_{\ell}
. Then we can use
Lemma

4.47
to derive that
\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}N_{\ell}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces M^{\prime}
, which implies that
\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}N_{\ell}=M^{\prime}
because
\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}N_{\ell}
cannot communicate.
Moreover,
{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma}(\varphi)=\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})
and

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})=\bigoplus_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{\ell}}(\varphi_{\ell})
, where

{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})\xrightarrow{A}{}{\textsc{c}}\mathsf{m}_{\sigma_{\ell}}(\varphi_{\ell})
, for each
\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}
.
From
{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma[\pi\mapsto\ell]}(\varphi^{\prime\prime})\xrightarrow{A}M_{\ell}\ignorespaces\rightarrow\ignorespaces N_{\ell}
and the induction hypothesis, we can obtain that
N_{\ell}={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{\ell}}(\varphi_{\ell})
; hence,
M^{\prime}=\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{\ell}}(\varphi_{\ell})
and we conclude via
Lemma

4.79
, that implies that
\bigvee_{\ell\in{\mathcal{L}}}{}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma_{\ell}}(\varphi_{\ell})={}{\textsc{d}}\mathsf{M}_{\sigma^{\prime}}(\varphi^{\prime})
.

The case for
\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
is similar; the cases for
\forall\pi.\varphi^{\prime\prime}
and
\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
are dual.
∎

5.
Conclusion

We provided two methods to synthesize monitors for hyperproperties expressed as fragments of Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML.
Our first synthesis procedure constructs monitors that analyse hypertraces in a centralized manner and are guaranteed to correctly detect all violations of the respective formula, as long as it does not have a least fixed-point operator.
Our second synthesis algorithm constructs monitors that operate in a decentralized manner and communicate with one another using multicast to share relevant information between
them.
The decentralized-monitor synthesis provides the same correctness guarantees as the centralized one, but is only defined for formulas with trace quantifiers that do not appear inside any fixed-point operator.
This additional restriction, which is natural and present in many monitoring set-ups for hyperlogics,
e.g.

(
Finkbeiner et al., 2019
;
Clarkson et al., 2014
;
Agrawal and Bonakdarpour, 2016
;
Brett et al., 2017
;
Gutsfeld et al., 2021
;
Bonakdarpour and Finkbeiner, 2018
)
, allows us to focus on examining the intricacies of monitoring in a decentralized setting with monitor communication.
More precisely, it allows us to fix the
\sigma
in the synthesis function which, in turn, produces a
static
set of locations with which a monitor
can communicate.
Despite the restriction to PHyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML, our synthesis algorithm still covers properties that were previously not even expressible, hence not monitorable, in state-of-the-art hyperlogics.

Future work

Of course, the picture is still incomplete: we have a centralized-monitor synthesis procedure for an expressive fragment of Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML,
whereas our decentralized-monitor synthesis deals with a more restricted fragment of that logic.
It is not clear if this restriction is necessary;
for example, a different decentralized-monitor synthesis for a larger fragment might be obtained by utilizing a different communication paradigm other than multicast, which was adopted in this study.
In fact, we conjecture that broadcast communications might allow us to synthesize decentralized monitors for a larger Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML fragment, including formulae that mix greatest fixed-points and quantifiers, like
\varphi_{a}
defined in (
1
); currently, monitors only send messages to the locations in the range of the specified
\sigma
.

A second interesting direction is to allow monitors to infer information from communications they did not receive.
A good starting point to explore such a synthesis algorithm (and prove its correctness) can be the synthesis properties in
Definition

4.8
.
To fully delineate the power of decentralized monitoring, a maximality result in the spirit of those presented in
(
Aceto et al., 2019a
;
Aceto et al., 2021
)
is needed, which we intend to establish in the future.

A third avenue for future investigation is to synthesize monitors that detect all satisfying hypertraces for the respective dual fragments of Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML, instead of focusing on monitors that detect violations (that is, we can focus on satisfaction completeness instead of on violation completeness).
A fourth possible direction is to provide an estimation (or, at least, a lower/upper bound) of the size of synthesized centralized and decentralized monitors for a given formula; this will require non-trivial combinatorial arguments, like those in
(
Aceto et al., 2019b
)
.
Another possible direction is to extend the proposed approach to hyperlogics that adopt asynchronous semantics, like the one adopted in
(
Chalupa and Henzinger, 2023
;
Gutsfeld et al., 2021
)
. Furthermore, it would also be challenging to understand how we can model a scenario where the number of localities
varies over time, and how we can handle the dynamic creation/deletion of the associated local monitors.

Finally, another relevant direction we intend to pursue in future is the development of tools for monitoring Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML specifications at runtime, based on the results of this article.
We expect that our decentralised-monitor synthesis procedure can be implemented by generating a dedicated monitor for every location in a way that is very similar to the synthesis of
\mu
HML monitors presented in
(
Attard et al., 2021
;
Aceto et al., 2022b
;
Aceto et al., 2024b
)
and implemented in the tool
detectEr
available at
https://duncanatt.github.io/detecter/
.

Related Work.

To the best of our knowledge, Agrawal and Bonakdarpour were the first to study RV for hyperproperties expressed in HyperLTL in
(
Agrawal and Bonakdarpour, 2016
)
, where they investigated monitorability for
k
-safety hyperproperties expressed in HyperLTL. They also gave a semantic characterization of monitorable
k
-safety hyperproperties, which is a natural extension to hyperproperties of the ‘universal version’ of the classic definition of monitorability presented by Pnueli-Zaks
(
Pnueli and Zaks, 2006
;
Aceto et al., 2021
)
. In contrast to this work, we do not restrict ourselves to alternation-free formulas (see
Eq.

1
) and every monitorable formula considered by Agrawal and Bonakdarpour can be expressed in our monitorable fragment. Brett et al.
(
Brett et al., 2017
)
improve on the work presented in
(
Agrawal and Bonakdarpour, 2016
)
by presenting an algorithm for monitoring the full alternation-free fragment of HyperLTL. They also highlight challenges that arise when monitoring arbitrary HyperLTL formulas, namely
(i)
quantifier alternations,
(ii)
inter-trace dependencies and
(iii)
relative ordering of events across traces.
Our decentralized-monitor synthesis addresses
(i)
by using the number of locations as an upper bound on the number of traces, and
(ii)
and
(iii)
via synchronized multicasts.

In
(
Finkbeiner et al., 2019
)
, Finkbeiner et al. investigate RV for HyperLTL
(
Clarkson et al., 2014
)
formulas w.r.t. three different input classes, namely the bounded sequential, the unbounded sequential and the parallel classes.
They also develop the monitoring tool RVHyper
(
Finkbeiner et al., 2018
)
based on the sequential algorithms developed for those input classes.
The parallel class is closest to our set-up, since it consists in a
fixed
number of system executions that are processed synchronously.

Beutner et al.
(
Beutner et al., 2024
)
study runtime monitoring for
\textsc{Hyper}^{2}\textsc{LTL}_{\text{fp}}
, a temporal logic that is interpreted over sets of
finite
traces of
equal length
.
Unlike
\textsc{Hyper}^{2}\textsc{LTL}

(
Beutner et al., 2023
)
,
\textsc{Hyper}^{2}\textsc{LTL}_{\text{fp}}
permits quantification under temporal operators, which is also allowed in our logic Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML.
In contrast to HyperLTL,
\textsc{Hyper}^{2}\textsc{LTL}_{\text{fp}}
features second-order quantification over sets of finite traces and can express properties like common knowledge.

In
(
Gutsfeld et al., 2021
)
, Gustfeld et al. study automated analysis techniques for asynchronous hyperproperties and propose a novel automata-theoretic framework, the so-called alternating asynchronous parity automata, together with the fixed-point logic
H_{\mu}
for expressing asynchronous hyperproperties.
The logic
H_{\mu}
has commonalities with PHyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML,
but
it only allows for prenex formulas;
moreover,
its semantics
progresses asynchronously on each trace.
Properties such as “an atomic proposition does not occur at a certain level in the tree (of traces)” are not expressible in their logic
H_{\mu}
, but
can be described in Hyper-
\mathsf{rec}
HML.

Chalupa and Henzinger
(
Chalupa and Henzinger, 2023
)
explore the potential of
monitoring for hyperproperties using prefix transducers.
They develop a transducer language, called prefix expressions, give it an operational semantics over a hypertrace (reminiscent of the semantics in Section
4
) and then implement
it to assess the induced overheads.
They show how transducers can use the writing capabilities as a method for monitor synchronization across traces, akin to the monitor communication and verdict aggregation
of
Section

4
.
Since transducers are, in principle, more powerful that passive monitors,
additional guarantees
are required to ensure that they do not interfere unnecessarily with system executions.
